Shell · SHEL-GB · the new Memo conveyor's card of 25 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Shell

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 48,979 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

–not yet rated

Memo topic 1, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Judgement: the external forces deliver Shell an exceptional but transient earnings tailwind in 2026, driven by supply scarcity rather than demand. Shell's integrated trading model is unusually well placed to monetise it. Underneath sits a moderately supportive structural backdrop: LNG demand growth, a delayed energy transition, an energy-security policy turn and capital discipline across the industry. That backdrop is offset by a predictable 2027-30 normalisation (refining margins, the LNG supply wave), rising fiscal and carbon costs, and service inflation.

The Consumer should build the deck in three layers:

  • a mid-cycle base: Brent about USD 70-75, refining about USD 10-12/bbl, European gas normalising toward about USD 8-10/mmbtu by 2028-30;
  • a windfall layer that decays from H2 2026 through 2027;
  • explicit scenarios for windfall taxes and for a sudden de-escalation.

The external picture argues for confidence in Shell's earnings engine and for caution about capitalising 2026.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0248C-0543C-0752C-0947C-3552C-3944
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

C-488th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: competitive intensity is stable to slightly rising. It is rising in two specific places, the competition for barrels (the "resource race") and LNG supply, while price competition in Shell's markets stays benign.

  • The resource race has restarted.
  • BP has moved about USD 10bn a year of capex to upstream and cut low-carbon capex by 75%.
  • Equinor has added USD 1bn to oil and gas while halving its renewables budget.
  • TotalEnergies guides 3% organic production growth to 2030.
  • Conventional discoveries fell below 5bn boe in 2026, against 55bn boe in 2010.
  • Together these raise the price of acquired resource. Shell paid about a 20% premium for ARC, and its own M&A head resigned after Wael Sawan refused to bid for BP.
  • LNG supply competition intensifies from 2027. TotalEnergies targets 50% LNG volume growth over 2025-30. US developers sell spot during commissioning; Shell lost an ICC arbitration against Venture Global over Calcasieu Pass cargoes. Qatar is expanding.
  • Downstream. Asian refinery start-ups and Chinese chemical exports pressure margins. Against that, European chemicals capacity is closing (Bernstein: about 15% of Shell's European chemicals capacity could shut), which helps the survivors.
  • Price behaviour. Majors keep capex caps and 40-50% payout frameworks, and TotalEnergies screens projects at USD 50 a barrel. None is buying share.
  • Net effect on Shell. Mildly negative for the cost of growth (inorganic barrels get dearer) and for LNG optimisation margins after 2027; neutral for pricing.
C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

D4310th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: input costs and supplier concentration are a mild and persistent headwind. They mean the fixed USD 20-22bn capex budget buys fewer barrels each year. Shell's cost programme and scale offset much of it in opex, but not in capital costs.

  • Rigs.
  • Day rates: ultra-deepwater USD 375,000-450,000; harsh-environment sixth-generation semi-submersibles USD 450,000-525,000.
  • New rigs need well above USD 720,000 a day, so none are being built. This is the Wisdom Library's "customers' capital discipline is the asset owners' scarcity": the pricing window for rig owners runs at least to 2028.
  • Subsea and services.
  • TechnipFMC, SLB OneSubsea and Baker Hughes take about 98% of subsea tree awards.
  • Subsea EPCI pricing is up 5-10% year on year in 2026, vessel utilisation is 85-90%, and Subsea 7 guides a 24% EBITDA margin.
  • Gas turbines are in serious backlog, a schedule risk for LNG expansions including LNG Canada Phase 2.
  • Offsets.
  • Centralised procurement and standard designs: Whale replicated Vito's design.
  • Scale bargaining power.
  • The capex cap stops Shell chasing inflation.
  • Unit production cost fell from USD 16.20/boe (2014) to USD 8.54 (2024).
  • Supply of hydrocarbons as an input.
  • Qatar's force majeure removed equity LNG, and Shell met contracts through record third-party sourcing plus LNG Canada.
  • The Pearl GTL outage forced expensive alternative base-oil supply for lubricants, a Q3 2026 headwind management flagged.
  • Venture Global's failure to deliver shows supplier risk.
  • FX. A modest net headwind, as appreciation of local currencies inflates European costs. Net transaction exposure is under 2% of adjusted net income.
  • For the model. Hold capex at the guided USD 20-22bn for 2027-28, but assume 5-10% a year less physical activity per dollar. Assume part of the remaining cost savings is absorbed by inflation (BP saw about USD 3bn of gross savings offset).
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

B+523rd of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the macro force is strong. It bites hardest at industry level, through commodity prices; next at sector level, through the discount rate and rotation risk; and least at company level, through FX and interest. For Shell's 18-month earnings it is currently favourable, but its skew is negative.

  • The regime.
  • An energy-led inflation shock: US core 3.1%; euro-area HICP 3.8% in September with energy at 18.8%.
  • The Fed is hiking in December with the US 10-year near 5.10%; the ECB deposit rate is 2.5% (terminal 2.75% expected).
  • French spreads at 160bp, with spillover into Italy.
  • IMF global growth 3.0% in 2026 and 3.4% in 2027.
  • Industry level. The oil and gas shock is Shell's earnings (sensitivities in C-75). The reversal path is demand destruction or recession, or a peace deal.
  • Sector level.
  • Energy is the regime's hedge and is Overweight in the Fitness for Fighting tilts.
  • Positioning is neutral at the 69th percentile, but systematic commodity exposure sits at the 91st percentile, so a sharp unwind is possible.
  • History: in the 1990 Gulf War shock equities fell 20% in three months, and the 2000s capex boom de-rated oil to about 5x EV/DACF.
  • Rising yields raise every asset's discount rate (Wisdom Library), a headwind to the multiple even as earnings rise.
  • Company level.
  • USD makes up about 70% of sales and about 75% of cost of goods.
  • A 10% sterling appreciation reduces pre-tax income by USD 156m; net transaction exposure is under 2% of adjusted net income.
  • A 1% rise in rates improves pre-tax income by USD 148m.
  • For the model. FX and interest are rounding items; the macro enters through the commodity deck and through the exit multiple.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–47

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the other external dynamics are, net, mildly negative and mostly add variance.

  1. Shipping and freight. Cape rerouting lengthens voyages by 35-45%. That raises trading opportunity, but it also raised the variable components of Shell's shipping leases by about USD 3bn (Jefferies), a non-cash increase in reported net debt and gearing.
  2. European sovereign stress. French spreads at decade highs raise the probability that governments fund deficits with energy windfall taxes.
  3. Positioning and flows. Energy positioning is neutral, but systematic commodity allocations sit at the 91st percentile, and Europe's market leadership comes from what it lacks (AI exposure) rather than from anything it has.
  4. Litigation momentum. Climate damages cases are spreading across the UK, Brazil and the Philippines.
  5. Peer fragility. BP's balance sheet and governance turmoil keep Shell-BP merger speculation alive. Shell is "not actively considering" an offer, and antitrust would bite.
  6. FX outlook. Bearish on the dollar (EUR/USD above 1.20 later), mildly negative for Shell's European cost base.
  7. El Niño. A threat to agricultural inflation, which feeds the rates path.
  8. LNG buyer behaviour. Sovereign buyers historically press to renegotiate slopes when spot collapses; Shell has extended contracts without margin erosion so far.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

D5213th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: new entrants are a modest threat to Shell's core and a moderate one to its LNG optimisation margins. The threat is rising as LNG markets become more liquid.

  • Adjacent entrants: national oil companies going international and downstream.
  • ADNOC is buying Shell's South African network and building its XRG portfolio; QatarEnergy trades its own volumes; Aramco is integrating downstream.
  • The threat is moderate. They compete for assets (raising acquisition prices) and capture optimisation margins Shell used to earn as an intermediary.
  • Standard entrants: US LNG developers and commodity traders.
  • Venture Global, Cheniere's expansions, the Mercuria-Eni joint venture, Vitol and Trafigura in LNG.
  • The threat is moderate. As the market becomes more liquid and oversupplied after 2027, the premium for a portfolio player's flexibility narrows.
  • Orthogonal entrants.
  • Hyperscalers building behind-the-meter power; EV-charging specialists; battery storage.
  • Low near term. They threaten Marketing's long-run fuel volumes and limit Shell's power ambitions, which Shell has largely abandoned anyway.
  • Barriers protecting the core. LNG costs above USD 1,000 per tonne; deepwater capital and know-how; the scale needed to compete (about 2.3 mboe/d); government control of concessions.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

D4412th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: suppliers are currently a mild source of risk, not support. Oilfield-service and EPC providers hold pricing power, and LNG supply partners have shown reliability risk. Shell's scale and diversified sourcing keep this a cost-efficiency issue, not an earnings swing.

  • Services pricing power.
  • Subsea 7 guides a 24% EBITDA margin; Baker Hughes targets 20% by 2028.
  • Subsea EPCI is up 5-10% year on year; drilling rigs and subsea vessels are near full utilisation, with contractors booked into 2027-28.
  • Three firms supply about 98% of subsea trees.
  • Gas-turbine backlogs force "creative aeroderivative" solutions on LNG and midstream projects.
  • Shell's mitigants.
  • Scale and centralised procurement; standardised designs.
  • Integrated well-construction contracts with SLB and Halliburton; the OneSubsea joint venture.
  • A capex cap that paces final investment decisions.
  • A self-inflicted gap. Shell did not keep the Deepsea Mira rig after Namibia, so it lacks secured harsh-environment capacity if Namibia develops quickly.
  • Supply partners.
  • QatarEnergy's force majeure.
  • Venture Global's non-delivery (arbitration lost).
  • Pearl GTL's damage forcing third-party base-oil purchases.
  • Shell's portfolio absorbed all of these in 2026, which shows the strength of a diversified portfolio.
  • For the model.
  • Capex inflation of about 5-10% a year (my assumption from the cited service pricing) inside a flat capex budget, which means fewer barrels per dollar.
  • LNG maintenance costs rising: cryogenic valve service is growing 5-6% a year.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0158C-0362C-0440C-0855C-3258C-3742C-4352
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

D5812th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: end-market demand is changing only modestly in volume and is not the force at work; supply and price are changing violently. Structural LNG demand growth is real and visible for more than two years, but its price is set to normalise as a supply wave arrives from 2027.

  • The structural picture is supportive and slow. Global LNG demand runs from 422 mtpa in 2025 to 650-710 mtpa by 2040 (Shell's outlook), with a 6% CAGR for 2026-30 (Morgan Stanley). Oil demand plateaus: 98.5 mmboe/d in 2025 to 103.4 mmboe/d by 2030, peaking around 103 mb/d near 2030 (BP's outlook). AI data centres add gas-for-power demand (Morgan Stanley: US gas demand +24% by 2030).
  • Near-term demand is contracting where it is price-elastic.
  • Global LNG demand fell 5.4% year on year in H1 2026 (China -10%, South Korea -14%; Bernstein) as Asian buyers stopped buying spot.
  • High fuel prices have cut jet demand.
  • The IEA projects European gas demand 8-10% lower over 2024-30, and German industrial production fell to an index of 90.3 in July 2026.
  • The offset is restocking. European storage was 49% full at end-June 2026, 15 points below normal, and Redburn estimates Europe must import 24% more LNG year on year (186.8 bcm) to refill to 90% by October 2027.
  • Visibility. Volume visibility is good: about 80% of Shell's LNG portfolio is on long-term contracts to 2029, averaging 15 years and mostly Brent-indexed with a three-month lag. Price visibility is poor and points down after 2027:
  • more than 150 mtpa of new liquefaction from 2026;
  • the US and Qatar controlling 57% of global capacity by 2031;
  • Bernstein's spot LNG average of USD 9/mmbtu for 2026-28 against USD 12 in 2025, a forecast made before the war;
  • European gas forecast about 40% below 2025 levels by 2028.
  • What it means for Shell. Because most LNG revenue follows Brent through contract slopes, the gas oversupply hits mainly the roughly 20% open length, trading margins and the slopes on new contracts. It does not hit the whole LNG book.
  • For the model.
  • Grow LNG sales volumes at 4-5% a year, in line with management's target to 2030.
  • Price the contracted LNG off the Brent deck with a three-month lag.
  • Fade spot-linked and trading contributions from 2027.
  • Treat demand destruction at oil above USD 100 as the near-term risk, and the LNG supply wave of 2027-30 as the medium-term one.
C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

B+623rd of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the energy-transition disruption force is being partly disproved over the 18-month to four-year horizon, and that acts in Shell's favour. Its long-run threat to Shell's fuels marketing is intact, and high 2026 prices re-accelerate it (a balancing loop).

  • Evidence that disruption is weakening near term.
  • Hydrogen: green hydrogen projects have been cancelled globally, and Shell ceased Aukra blue hydrogen and kept only REFHYNE II (100 MW) and Holland Hydrogen I (200 MW).
  • EV charging: Shell has about 88,000 public charge points and says the business is not profitable.
  • Offshore wind: returns are compressed. Shell exited Atlantic Shores, ScotWind and SouthCoast and sold 4 GW of European onshore renewables to TotalEnergies.
  • Biofuels: Rotterdam was cancelled after about USD 1.4bn of cumulative impairments and provisions.
  • Data centres need firm gas-fired power (Baker Hughes reports strong turbine demand).
  • What that means for Shell. These developments validate the 2023 pivot: low-carbon capital is now below 10% of capital employed, and the return dilution that Redburn criticised is being removed. Renewables & Energy Solutions still impairs (USD 536m in Q2 2026, USD 565m in H1), so the clean-up is not finished.
  • The longer-run force still acts against Marketing. EV adoption erodes fuel and lubricant demand; China's EV penetration is projected toward 44% by 2035. Shell is already shrinking retail (about 500 stations sold a year; Jiffy Lube sold) and redeploying toward convenience and premium products.
  • The balancing loop. High LNG prices accelerate fuel switching in price-sensitive Asian markets (Bernstein). The more 2026 earns, the stronger the substitution response later.
  • For the model. No disruption-driven volume loss beyond a slow decline in Marketing volumes (2.55-2.65 mb/d guided corridor). Continued but shrinking R&ES impairments. Carbon-cost escalation is covered in C-04.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

F4017th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: political and regulatory change is material and rising, and is a net headwind. Its tail is fat precisely because prices are high: the political response function is pro-cyclical, so the more the sector earns the higher the tax risk.

  • Tax.
  • UK. The Energy Profits Levy rose to 38% (a 78% marginal rate), was extended to 31-Mar-30 and lost its main investment allowance. Its successor, the Oil and Gas Revenue Levy, is a 35% tax on gross revenue above USD 90/bbl and 90p/therm; it is regressive for high-cost producers. Shell's UK upstream now sits in the equity-accounted Adura joint venture with Equinor, which softens the cash impact. Shell paid USD 509m of UK levy in H1 2025.
  • Brazil. A 12% crude export levy cost USD 242m in Q2 2026. A trial court provisionally suspended it after a joint appeal by Shell, Equinor and TotalEnergies; the suspension is reversible.
  • Europe. Six EU member states are lobbying for a permanent EU-wide windfall tax, and Spain and France have nationalised the 2022-23 provisions. Germany's solidarity contribution applied only to 2022-23.
  • Carbon. Shell's direct compliance cost was USD 398m in 2025. Shell models about USD 1bn in 2026 and about USD 4bn a year by 2035 (mid-price scenario), a creeping headwind of roughly USD 0.3bn a year. EU CBAM entered its definitive phase in January 2026.
  • Legal.
  • Milieudefensie's appeal to the Dutch Supreme Court (February 2025), after Shell won at The Hague Court of Appeal.
  • More than 30 US climate suits, plus UK, Brazilian and Philippine damages claims.
  • A USD 4bn Brazilian tax dispute over Tupi.
  • Nigerian legacy remediation claims.
  • Supportive moves. EU pragmatism on energy security; a possible 12-month delay to the EU methane regulation; the 2027 EU ban on Russian LNG, to which Shell is not exposed; easier US LNG permitting; and Bernstein's report (February 2026) that the UK is discussing an early end to the levy.
  • For the model.
  • Hold the reported tax rate at roughly 41-45%; it was 41.4%, 49.8% and 44.9% in 2025, 2024 and 2023.
  • Keep the UK levy to March 2030.
  • Add carbon cost at about USD 1bn in 2026, rising.
  • Treat an EU or UK windfall-tax escalation as a scenario. In 2022-23 windfall taxes absorbed about 5% of European majors' free cash flow on average and up to about 10% for some.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

D5513th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the industry-level driver now accelerating Shell's revenue is not demand but supply scarcity, which raises both price and volatility. Shell's integrated trading and logistics monetise exactly those two. The accelerator is real, large and reversible.

  • The supply drivers.
  • The Hormuz closure (about 10% of Shell's production passes through it).
  • QatarEnergy's force majeure from 2-Mar-26, with 17% of its export capacity damaged.
  • Ukrainian strikes on Russian refining.
  • Inventories: oil stocks down 507 mb since February; US distillates heading below 100 mb, the lowest since 2003; NWE Brent cracking margins up 301% year to date.
  • How Shell turns this into earnings.
  • Trading offset the Middle East outages entirely in Q2: Integrated Gas beat consensus by 11% while volumes collapsed.
  • Chemicals & Products beat by 23% at 102% refinery utilisation.
  • Trading contributed an estimated USD 3bn of incremental Q1 earnings (Morgan Stanley).
  • Structural demand drivers exist but are slow: Asian LNG, coal-to-gas switching, data centres, energy security (Japan LNG-to-power).
  • Shell's volume demand. It is limited by Shell's own supply, not by demand: Shell sells everything it produces or can source.
  • For the model. Treat the accelerator as a windfall that decays. Give the trading layer an explicit fade (C-67).
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

D5812th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: customer demand supports the near-term thesis through tight markets and long-term LNG contracts, and moderately supports the mid-term one through structural LNG growth and resilient oil. It is fragile at current prices: price-elastic Asian LNG demand and European industry are already pulling back, and the term-contract cover thins after 2029.

  • LNG buyers.
  • About 80% of Shell's LNG volume is contracted to 2029, averaging 15 years and some longer than 25. Regasification investment and vessel compatibility give buyers steep switching costs.
  • Energy security is driving new long-term contracting: Uniper with Equinor for 15 years; TotalEnergies with Shenergy and BOTAŞ.
  • But Asian spot demand is price-elastic (H1 2026 -5.4%), and price-insensitive Asian utilities are outbidding Europe, so cargoes have turned in mid-ocean.
  • Oil and products.
  • Demand recovered from a Q2 trough of about 99 mb/d toward 106 mb/d in Q4 2026 (Equinor's investor presentation).
  • Diesel and jet are tight, though jet demand destruction is visible.
  • Marketing volumes sit in a 2.55-2.65 mb/d corridor.
  • Chemicals. Customers are cautious and margins are at cyclical lows. Management warns that elevated spreads are softening as new capacity arrives.
  • What makes it fragile.
  • Demand destruction at USD 100+ oil and gas prices above EUR 70/MWh.
  • The European gas demand decline (-8-10% over 2024-30, IEA).
  • A post-2029 LNG contract roll-over into a buyer's market.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

F4217th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: regulators and politicians both help and hurt; the net is negative and fragile (detail in C-04).

  • They help through:
  • an energy-security pragmatism that removes the "transition vehicle" discount;
  • delays to the EU methane rules;
  • easier US LNG permitting;
  • Brazilian court relief;
  • talk of an early end to the UK levy;
  • the 2027 EU Russian LNG ban, which supports non-Russian supply.
  • They hurt through:
  • the UK levy (78% marginal rate to March 2030) and its revenue-based successor;
  • EU windfall-tax lobbying;
  • rising carbon costs (USD 1bn in 2026 to USD 4bn by 2035);
  • CBAM;
  • Scope 3 consent rules for UK projects;
  • Dutch and international climate litigation;
  • the Brazilian export levy;
  • Nigerian remediation scrutiny.
  • Why the position is fragile: political behaviour is pro-cyclical. Record profits and consumer energy bills (French fuel price cap of EUR 1.94 a litre; European electricity tied to gas) generate pressure for windfall taxes. This is a balancing loop that caps how much of a price spike shareholders keep.
  • For the model. Haircut windfall-year cash flows for tax risk in Europe and the UK. Do not haircut the mid-cycle years.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

D5214th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the realistic addressable markets are vast relative to Shell, so market size is not the binding constraint on growth. Resource access and capital discipline are. Over the mid term the markets allow Shell volume growth of about 1-4% a year and LNG growth of 4-6% a year. Revenue growth beyond that comes only from price.

  • Oil. About 98.5 mmboe/d in 2025, rising 4.9% to 103.4 mmboe/d by 2030; Shell's upstream liquids are about 1.4 mb/d, roughly 1.4% of world supply.
  • Gas. About 69.7 mmboe/d rising 9% to 76.3 mmboe/d by 2030.
  • LNG. 422 mtpa (2025) to 650-710 mtpa (2040), with a 6% CAGR for 2026-30. Shell's share is about 15-20%.
  • Spending. Global upstream capex was USD 750bn in 2025.
  • The constraints.
  • Shell grows LNG sales by 4-5% a year, slightly below the market.
  • Group production grows at about 1% organically and about 4% with ARC.
  • These are choices ("value over volume") and constraints (reserve life, hurdle rates), not market limits.
  • Power and low-carbon. Large markets, now deliberately small for Shell (under 10% of capital employed).
  • For the model. Mid-term volume growth of about 4% a year to 2028, weighted to 2027 as ARC and the Qatar restoration arrive, then low single digits. Revenue growth is otherwise the commodity deck.

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

F4215th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Shell's revenue cycle is at or near a price peak and at a volume trough that is turning up. Earnings turn down from H2 2026 into 2027, cushioned by volume recovery and acquisitions. The timing of the price turn is set by geopolitics and is unusually uncertain.

  • Price peak.
  • Q2 2026 revenue was USD 94.7bn, up 44.7% year on year.
  • The Q3 indicative refining margin was USD 42/bbl; Brent was USD 111 in late October and Q3 TTF futures rose 66.6%.
  • FY2026 consensus: revenue +31.8%, EBITDA +44%, EPS +102% (like-for-like on the brokers' basis).
  • FY2027 consensus: -8.0% revenue, -9.4% EBITDA, -17.0% EPS. FY2028: -5.1%, -12.8%, -8.5%.
  • Consensus has timed the price down-leg for 2027.
  • Volume trough.

  • Deck dispersion.
  • Brent: Morgan Stanley USD 81.2 for 2027; DNB Carnegie USD 80, then 75; Redburn long term USD 70; Deutsche Bank peace case about USD 70 by 2027; Morgan Stanley escalation case USD 140-160.
  • The cone is very wide.
  • Skew. From today's spot the skew for earnings is to the downside. Prices are near extremes; windfall taxes would cap the upside; refining margins are the most mean-reverting line; and Redburn expects European refining margins to peak in Q3 2026.
  • For the model.
  • Q3 and Q4 2026 strong: the Q3 print on 29-Oct-26 likely beats on refining.
  • Then a stepped normalisation through 2027 into 2028, with a volume uplift of roughly 15-25% in 2027 against 2026, weighted to low-margin gas.
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

D5511th of 19

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the external picture is strongly favourable today, but the favourable part is the most cyclical part. The structural backdrop is moderately favourable; the 2027-28 outlook is a predictable normalisation that consensus already carries.

Driver by driver (from the Industry and Sector Digests):

  1. "Nobody is building capacity", customers' capital discipline. A tailwind to commodity price, and a headwind to Shell as a buyer of rigs and subsea services. Shell is on the owner's side in LNG liquefaction, which is scarce now and in surplus in 2027-30.
  2. "The industry guides low, so consensus becomes a floor". A near-term tailwind: 2026 beats of 8% and 10%, and a Q3 refining margin well above expectations.
  3. "Balance sheets at record lows make energy a yield group". A tailwind to Shell's rating. Shell's payout Brent floor (about USD 50) is among the lowest in the sector.
  4. "The capital swing back to barrels". A tailwind to the sector multiple, but less incremental for Shell: it pivoted in 2023, and peers catching up erode its distinctiveness.
  5. "What Hormuz feeds and what it hurts". It feeds Shell's trading, refining and price; it hurts its Qatari and Omani volumes (about 20% of production is in the Middle East). Net, it feeds.
  6. Self-funded regional platforms. Adura turned the UK from a capital drain into a cash source.

Value-chain view.

  • Upstream: a strong price tailwind.
  • LNG: a tailwind now, a headwind in 2027-30 (oversupply).
  • Refining: an extraordinary tailwind and the most mean-reverting.
  • Chemicals: a headwind, structurally in Europe.
  • Marketing: a slow structural headwind.

Headwinds. Windfall taxes, carbon costs, service inflation, the LNG supply wave, demand destruction, and rate-driven de-rating.

Internal change forces/risks

Memo topic 2, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Judgement: Shell's internal change is a strong, credible, well-executed self-help and capital-discipline programme now in its harvest phase.

  • What has been delivered. About USD 5.8bn of structural cost out, the portfolio high-graded, 19 consecutive quarters of at-least-USD-3bn buybacks, and a stable leadership team that delivers on what it controls.
  • What the last six months added. One strategic step-change, ARC and the Canadian LNG hub. It lifts 2027-28 volumes but is defensive in value, stock-funded and gas-weighted.
  • What has been accepted along the way. Slow erosion: reserve life, organic growth, technical capability and a governance lapse.

For the Consumer, the internal forces justify modelling the controllables (capex, costs, distributions, the share count) with high confidence and close to guidance. They do not justify adding earnings growth beyond volumes, ARC synergies, the chemicals clean-up and buyback accretion. The next leg of internal change, a higher dividend-growth policy or new CMD targets, is plausible but not yet signalled.

Internal risks

6 answers
Judgement37C-1466C-8638C-5952C-8140D-0344
Group judgement

Risks

D3713th of 19

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement: Shell's own fundamental risk is moderate and well buffered: a strong balance sheet, a flexible payout and working-capital release in a downturn.

  • The investment risk is elevated because three things coincide:
  • earnings near a geopolitical peak;
  • a valuation at the top of its five-year range;
  • crowded sector positioning in a regime that uses energy as a hedge.
  • The dominant risk is a de-escalation. A peace deal or OPEC+ supply response normalises earnings 35-45% below 2026 and de-rates the sector at the same moment, implying a 25-35% share-price fall to a fair trough.
  • The second-order risks: damage to Gulf assets, windfall taxes, a safety incident and audit-transition noise.
  • The structural risk: reserve longevity. Real but slow, and qualitative in most sell-side models.

For the Consumer: model a probability-weighted de-escalation path explicitly. Recognise that distributions survive it but the share price does not.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

B664th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: standards in cost discipline and project and operational execution are clearly improving and now rank among the best in Europe. Two weak spots stand out: audit governance has had a lapse, and human-capital indicators show cost-cutting beginning to consume capability.

  • Improving.
  • Structural cost out of USD 5.83bn (C-13); unit production cost down from USD 16.20/boe (2014) to USD 8.54 (2024).
  • 2025 projects were completed at 99.8% of sanctioned budget.
  • Capex consistently below guidance: USD 21bn in 2024 against USD 22-25bn guided; Q2 2026 USD 4.2bn against USD 4.9bn consensus.
  • Record 102% refinery utilisation in Q2 2026 and record Brazilian deepwater output.
  • A 55% refining-margin capture rate in H1 2026, against 35% at TotalEnergies and 33% at Eni (BP captured 64%).
  • Chemicals turned free-cash-flow positive in Q2 2026, its best result since Q3 2021.
  • The 2026 LTIP adds normalised FCF per share as a performance condition.
  • Weak spots.
  • Audit. EY's partner-rotation breach forced amended 20-Fs. No other supermajor has had a comparable lapse in the period.
  • People. Voluntary turnover rose to 11% in 2025 from 7%; headcount fell from 96,000 to 85,000; formal training days fell; the People Survey response rate slipped to 85%. Experts report "triple-hatting", eroded technical depth and dependence on junior staff.
  • Maintenance. The Q2 2026 capex underspend included deferred non-essential maintenance. Reliability is a lagging indicator, so the cost of this would show up later.
  • Returns. ROACE was 9.4% in 2025, below the roughly 10% framework, before recovering to 12.4% annualised in Q2 2026.
  • For the model.
  • Keep a modest unplanned-downtime allowance.
  • Do not assume further step-changes in efficiency beyond the cost target.
  • The risk is that maintenance and talent cuts surface as reliability incidents in 2027-28.

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

Judgement: the negative corporate developments are mostly slow-acting erosions (reserve life, capability, organic growth) plus several discrete items in 2026 (equity issuance, the buyback trim, the auditor breach, the Pearl damage). None threatens the 18-month financial framework; together they cap the long-term multiple.

Present.

  3. First buyback reduction in years. USD 3.5bn to USD 3.0bn a quarter.

Plausible.

  • A cost overrun on LNG Canada Phase 2.
  • AECO weakness eroding ARC's accretion.

C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

D5211th of 19

Judgement: the system is congruent at the top and mixed underneath.

  • Congruent at the top.
  • The goal is per-share value and FCF per share.
  • The strategy is value over volume.
  • Pay is aligned: the annual bonus weights 35% on CFFO, 35% on operational excellence and 15% on energy transition; the 2026 LTIP adds normalised FCF per share; the CEO shareholding requirement is 900% of salary.
  • Decisions follow: Rotterdam cancelled, BP rejected, buybacks as the benchmark.

  • For the model. Execution of plans is reliable at the corporate level. Watch for front-line slippage in reliability, project timing and safety.
C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

D408th of 19

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: Shell carries several sudden, high-magnitude fundamental risks. The most plausible and largest is a sudden de-escalation followed by an OPEC+ supply surge. The probabilities below are my judgements.

  1. A peace or Hormuz reopening followed by a price collapse. - Brent from about USD 110 to USD 60-70, refining back to about USD 10/bbl, trading normalising. - Plausibility within 18 months: high, about 30-40%. It is low-probability only in its sharp form (Brent below USD 60). - Magnitude: adjusted earnings fall from about USD 36bn (2026) to about USD 19-24bn, EPS down 35-45%. - The buyback likely holds at USD 3bn a quarter (floor at USD 50). Working-capital release cushions cash.
  2. Escalation that damages Shell's own Gulf assets. - About 20% of production is in the Middle East and about 10% transits Hormuz. Pearl is already damaged; Qatar LNG and Oman Block 10 are exposed. - Plausibility: moderate (about 15%). - Magnitude: volumes down 10-20% and possible impairments, partly offset by higher prices. Net earnings effect ambiguous; the risk is to the multiple.
  3. A major safety or environmental incident (deepwater blowout, LNG plant explosion). - Plausibility: low, under 5% a year. - Magnitude: very large. BP's Macondo precedent, with USD 5.88bn of BP's spill payables still outstanding 16 years later.
  4. Windfall-tax escalation (an EU-wide levy, a higher UK rate, a Brazilian levy reinstated). - Plausibility: moderate, about 25% in a high-price regime. - Magnitude: about 5-10% of free cash flow in affected years.
  5. A trading loss or counterparty default (Level 3 derivatives; a USD 902m expected credit loss already booked on trading loans). - Plausibility: low. Magnitude: USD 1-5bn.
  6. A dispute in the auditor transition (PwC from FY2027 revisiting Level 3 valuations or estimates). - Plausibility: low. Magnitude: reputational, with possible restatement.
  7. Climate litigation. The Dutch Supreme Court could reinstate a Scope 3 reduction order. - Plausibility: under 10%. Magnitude: strategic constraint, capital autonomy.
  8. Project and acquisition risk. A LNG Canada Phase 2 overrun above 20%, or AECO staying below about USD 2/mcf. - Plausibility: moderate. Magnitude: USD 1-3bn of NPV.
  9. Legacy liabilities. Nigerian remediation (USD 10.9bn leaked estimate), Russian claims, onshore decommissioning reverting to Shell. - Plausibility: low to moderate. Magnitude: a few USD bn.
D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

–44

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the drivers that separate the forecasts from the last four to six years are extremely concentrated. About 85% of the change in adjusted earnings from FY2026 to FY2028 (a fall of about USD 13bn) comes from four price lines, and about 60% from two, Brent and the refining margin. All four are moved by the same event, the closure of the Strait of Hormuz, and so is the largest offsetting driver, the return of Qatari and Pearl volumes. The forecast is therefore effectively a one-variable forecast with two transmission channels and a partial internal hedge. That makes it simple to monitor and very hard to time. The lines that make the forecast differ structurally from 2020-25, rather than cyclically, are small and diffuse: ARC's low-margin gas, the carbon cost, the tax mix, lease remeasurement with freight, and a share count that falls about 5% a year.

The decomposition (adjusted net income, FY2026 USD 37.5bn to FY2028 USD 24.2bn, using the second set's sensitivities; post-tax).

Driver Assumed move Earnings effect (USDbn) Share of the gross negative moves Moved by
Brent USD 95 to 72 about -7.6 45% Hormuz; OPEC+ response
Indicative refining margin USD 28 to 12 about -5.1 30% Hormuz (Gulf product exports, Pearl, freight); global maintenance
European and Asian gas TTF USD 17 to 10 about -2.1 12% Hormuz (Qatari LNG); the 2027-30 supply wave
Exceptional trading layer USD 3-5bn pre-tax to nil about -2.0 12% Volatility, itself a function of Hormuz
Volumes and self-help (Qatar and Pearl, ARC, LNG Canada, costs, chemicals, less carbon and dilution) about +3.3 the offset Hormuz (for about half); Shell (for the rest)
Net about -13.3

The point is not the arithmetic but the correlation structure. Brent, cracks and TTF are not three independent draws over the next 18 months; they are three readings of one geopolitical state. The second set's three-state cone (fast reopening, gradual, prolonged) is therefore the correct way to think about the forecast, and a Monte Carlo on independent price lines would understate the width.

How this compares with the last four to six years. The 2019-25 record was also deck-driven: FactSet operating EBITDA ran USD 46.8bn (2019), 16.3bn (2020), 49.3bn (2021), 70.1bn (2022), 45.6bn (2023), 46.3bn (2024), 43.7bn (2025), a range set by the 2020 demand collapse and the 2022 Russian gas shock, with a steady undercurrent from buybacks (shares down 27%) and the cost programme. The forecast repeats that structure in reverse order: a spike, then a 40-50% normalisation of earnings, with the per-share undercurrent continuing. What is structurally new is small:

  1. A gas-weighted volume step-up of 15-20% (ARC, the Qatar return) worth only about 11% to mid-cycle earnings because ARC earns about USD 7/boe (roughly USD 1bn on 370 kboe/d including synergies) against about USD 20/boe for Shell's Upstream in FY2026 and USD 12/boe at mid-cycle (second set). Volume growth that lowers the realised dollars per barrel is a new feature, and it is why "production up 20%, earnings down 35%" will read oddly to anyone who looks only at volumes.
  2. A new price exposure, AECO, through 43% of ARC's gas (AECO went negative in September 2025; Morgan Stanley expects the AECO-Henry Hub basis to stay around USD 1.75 wide), partly offset by ARC's JKM-linked Cheniere contract from 2027 and its US Midwest and Dawn sales.
  3. Carbon compliance cost rising about USD 0.3bn a year (USD 1bn in 2026 toward 4bn by 2035), which no broker in the files models explicitly.
  4. A tax mix that moves with the earnings mix (refining and trading taxed lower than Upstream; the adjusted rate on the model's basis moves from 35% to 36.5%) plus Q4 deferred-tax true-ups (47% in Q4 2025 against 37% for the nine months, on Shell's basis).
  5. Lease liabilities (about USD 30-34bn) remeasured with freight, which move reported gearing without cash.
  6. An extra fiscal layer: the UK levy to March 2030 is already inside Adura; the Brazil levy is suspended; a permanent EU windfall tax is being debated. These are tail items that bite only in the prolonged state.

Which one or two inputs explain most of the FY2027 variance. Brent and the refining margin, and behind both the date on which the Strait reopens. The second set's sensitivity makes this concrete: USD 10 on Brent is about USD 0.60 of FY2027 EPS; USD 5 on the refining margin about USD 0.30. The FY2027 EPS range of about USD 4.2-7.0 is almost entirely the Hormuz state; the residual, non-Hormuz variance (quarterly trading swings of about USD 1bn, the Q4 tax true-up, chemicals margins, Attachie's well results) is worth perhaps USD 0.3-0.4 of EPS either way. The internal hedge narrows the cone but does not change its shape: Shell is long the disruption by about four to five times its volume exposure to it (second set, P-01), so the Qatar return offsets about one-fifth of the price effect when the Strait reopens.

Where the drivers are diffuse: the lines below EBITDA and in cash. The share count depends on buybacks (policy), the share price (which falls when earnings fall, raising the retirement rate) and the ARC issue (done). Free cash flow depends on the deck but also on working-capital release, cash-tax timing, capex (guided) and disposals (several small items). Net debt depends on all of these plus leases. These lines are driven by five or six moderately independent items, each inside a narrow range; that diffusion is why the second set, and I, treat cash and per-share figures as the predictable part of the forecast.

Against peers. Shell's concentration runs through the refining-plus-trading layer: Chemicals & Products falls from about USD 11.5bn to 3.7bn, 60% of the group decline, because Products trading earned 126% of the segment's 2025 profit and the indicative margin is normalising from 42. TotalEnergies' decline runs through upstream and LNG with less refining leverage (35% capture) and less Hormuz exposure (Morgan Stanley's volume markdown 6.6% against Shell's 10.1%); its organic growth of 3% is an independent offset Shell lacks. Equinor's variance is almost all TTF. ExxonMobil and Chevron show a 94% correlation of upstream income with Brent against 87% for the Europeans, the difference being gas trading, which is Shell's largest source of quarterly surprise (Redburn: Shell and BP surprise consensus by more than 10% in two-thirds of quarters). Shell is the European major whose forecast is most concentrated on one geopolitical variable and whose quarterly noise around that variable is largest.

What this means for predictability. Concentration on a single, observable, weekly-tracked variable is good for monitoring (D-05) and bad for forecasting, because the variable is binary, political and unforecastable from company data. The forecast's structure is simple; its central input is not. The compensations are that the second-order drivers are controllable and diffuse, and that the two biggest price lines are both visible on forward curves once the state is known.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

D6013th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the strategy is stable at its core, "performance, discipline, simplification" and "value over volume". In the last six months it gained one material new element: buying resource longevity through acquisition and anchoring a Canadian gas-to-LNG hub. It is well articulated; it is resourced by equity rather than balance sheet; and it leaves the post-2030 liquids gap open.

  • Core, unchanged since CMD23 and CMD25.
  • LNG sales growth of 4-5% a year to 2030; upstream liquids held at about 1.4 mb/d.
  • Cash capex USD 20-22bn a year for 2025-28.
  • Low-carbon capital below 10% of capital employed.
  • Distributions of 40-50% of CFFO; FCF per share growth above 10% a year to 2030.
  • Hurdle rates of 15% (Upstream), 11% (Integrated Gas) and 12-15% (chemicals).
  • New in the last six months. 1. ARC Resources. Announced in April 2026 and completed on 2-Sep-26 at an EV of about USD 16.5bn. It adds about 370 kboe/d and lifts the 2025-30 production CAGR from about 1% to 4%. Synergies are USD 250m by the end of year one, and ARC's USD 1.3bn of organic capex is absorbed inside the USD 20-22bn envelope. 2. LNG Canada Phase 2. Final investment decision on 30-Sep-26 to double the Kitimat capacity, fed by ARC's Montney gas. 3. Exploration reset. Shell is "expediting" Namibian appraisal after Merlin-1X found light oil, yet did not exercise its option on a second well. The rig sits idle into Q1 2027 and the earlier Jonker discoveries were declared uneconomic. 4. Chemicals review. Sale of the US chemicals business is being progressed (Morgan Stanley is mandated to test private-equity interest in Deer Park), and European capacity is being reduced.
  • Articulation is clear: a 4% CAGR, a quantified ARC free cash flow of about USD 1.5bn and named synergies.
  • The resourcing choice is odd. About 75% of ARC was paid in new shares (228m) while Shell buys back about USD 13-14bn a year. ING called it puzzling, and Redburn calculates average FCF-per-share accretion of only 7.5% over 2027-29.
  • The open gap. ARC is gas-weighted, so the 200-350 kb/d post-2030 liquids gap (Redburn) is not closed.
  • For the model.
  • Production grows in 2027-28.
  • The mix shifts to gas, so the realised dollars per boe fall.
  • Canadian gas (AECO) price exposure rises: weak AECO directly erodes ARC's accretion. This is a new long gas position alongside Shell's existing net-short Henry Hub sensitivity (-USD 500m of CFFO per USD 1/mmbtu).
  • Capex stays flat.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

B+703rd of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: perimeter reduction is strong, continuing and, in 2026, accelerating. It removes low-return capital and long-tail liabilities at reasonable prices. The earnings given up are small; the simplification gain is real; and the remaining big prize, chemicals, is in progress.

  • Done or agreed.

  • The oil-sands mining interest, swapped for a higher stake in Quest and Scotford.
  • UK upstream folded into the Adura joint venture (December 2025, deconsolidated and self-funded).
  • Jiffy Lube for about USD 1.3bn (closed 30-Jun-26, with a long-term lubricants supply agreement kept).
  • Sprng Energy in India for USD 1.8bn.
  • South African downstream to ADNOC Distribution (USD 1bn EV, closing 2027).
  • 50% of Na Kika and Coulomb for USD 1.7bn, with the buyer assuming decommissioning.
  • Shell Nigeria Gas to Vaaris (13-Oct-26); 4 GW of European onshore renewables to TotalEnergies; Volta Media.
  • More than USD 3bn of divestments were agreed or completed around Q2 2026 alone.
  • Structural. Refineries cut from 15 to 7 while keeping 1.4 mb/d of capacity; about 500 retail stations sold a year.
  • In progress. Sale of the US chemicals business (an ethane-cracker position at the low end of the cost curve) and European chemical capacity cuts. Jefferies counts about USD 45bn of capital that "continues to underperform", much of it chemicals; the chemicals asset base is about USD 25bn.
  • Costs and caveats.
  • Some divestments remove stable cash flows (Jiffy Lube, Na Kika production).
  • Nigerian legacy liabilities stay with Shell: Amnesty's leaked estimate of SPDC decommissioning and pollution liability is USD 10.9bn, and the matter is disputed.
  • Part of the "structural cost savings" comes from divestments, so do not double-count.
  • For the model.
  • Proceeds of about USD 3-4bn a year.
  • Small earnings dilution, about USD 0.3-0.6bn a year. This is my estimate, based on the scale and nature of the assets sold.
  • Chemical losses shrink as the exits complete.
C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

C606th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: entry into new markets is limited, deliberate and sensible. The one material move, the Canadian Montney through ARC, is a logical integration play into Shell's own LNG export asset. Its risk sits in Canadian gas pricing and in the execution of LNG Canada Phase 2.

  • Canada.
  • ARC makes Shell the third-largest operator in the Montney, contiguous with the feedgas for LNG Canada, where Shell holds 40%. Phase 1 is at full capacity and Phase 2 took final investment decision on 30-Sep-26.
  • The logic is low-cost, long-duration gas and condensate feeding Shell-controlled liquefaction, which converts AECO-priced gas into Asian-priced LNG. Jefferies calls the AECO-indexed feed "highly competitive".
  • The risks:
    • AECO has historically traded below USD 2/mcf; the Q1 Earnings Review names that level as a risk to the accretion.
    • Phase 2 capex and timing, with the gas-turbine backlog and EPC cost discussions.
    • Regulatory consents.
    • Integration.
  • Namibia. Light-oil success at Merlin-1X, but a one-well campaign and an idle rig. That is prudent given Jonker, but it lengthens the time before any post-2030 liquids answer.
  • Nigeria. Deepwater (Bonga North; about USD 7bn of combined sanctioned investment with partners) replaces the exited onshore position.
  • Exits. Shell has left Indian power, US offshore wind and South African retail, and is concentrating on fewer, deeper hubs.
  • For the model.
  • Add ARC from Q4 2026, about 370 kboe/d growing modestly.
  • Value it at about USD 1.5bn of free cash flow a year plus synergies ramping to USD 250m.
  • Model LNG Canada Phase 2 spend inside the capex envelope, with no Phase 2 volumes before about 2030. That date is my estimate; it is not given in the files.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

D4711th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the question applies only weakly. Shell is a price-taker on almost all of its revenue, and the 2026 price rises are nominal, cyclical and made by the market, not by Shell. Its closest equivalent to pricing power is selling flexibility and reliability at a premium in dislocated markets. That is real, but it is a volatility rent, not a real price increase.

  • Benchmark-linked revenue. Brent, TTF/JKM, Henry Hub and crack spreads drive the bulk of revenue.
  • LNG contracts.
  • About 80% of volumes are contracted to 2029, Brent-indexed with a three-month lag, averaging about 15 years.
  • Historically, sovereign buyers have pushed to renegotiate slopes after spot collapses, but Shell has "prolonged legacy contracts without structural margin deterioration".
  • New contracts signed in the tight 2026 market may carry firmer terms. The buyer's market expected from 2027 points the other way.
  • Marketing.
  • Premium fuels are 13.5% of volume at equal gross margins to regular fuel, which shows no premium being extracted.
  • Lubricant margins fell in Q2 as the Pearl outage forced expensive sourcing.
  • In unbranded markets Shell is a price-taking reseller with zero customer switching costs.
  • Latent pricing power.
  • Trading's ability to divert cargoes to the premium market: about 200 cargoes redirected to Europe in 2022; full delivery through Qatar's force majeure in 2026.
  • It is evidenced, but it is earned only when markets dislocate.
  • For the model. Model price from benchmark decks with contract lags. Treat the trading premium as a separate, decaying line, not as pricing power.
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

B585th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: M&A has stepped up from almost nothing to one large, strategically coherent deal. Discipline is reasonable (Shell rejected BP and pays at modest accretion), but the deal is defensive in value terms, funded mostly with stock, and too early to judge on integration. Expect further bolt-ons rather than another large deal; a Shell-BP combination is unlikely.

  • ARC terms.
  • Equity of about USD 13.6-13.9bn at a premium of about 20% to the 30-day VWAP; EV of about USD 16.4-16.5bn including about USD 2.5bn of assumed net debt.
  • 75% paid in 228m new shares (about 4% dilution), plus USD 3.3bn in cash.
  • Strategic sense: high. It secures feedgas for LNG Canada Phases 1 and 2, extends reserve life and makes Canada a low-cost "heartland".
  • Discipline: moderate to high.
  • Sawan's high bar was shown in rejecting BP (the M&A head resigned).
  • Valued on free cash flow, the deal yields about 9% before synergies and about 10.5% with them. My calculation: USD 1.5bn plus USD 0.25bn of synergies, over a USD 16.5bn EV.
  • Redburn's 7.5% average FCF-per-share accretion for 2027-29 marks it as "defensive".
  • ING questioned issuing equity at 19% gearing.
  • Integration.
  • Untested so far; synergies are due by about September 2027.
  • Shell's precedent is the BG integration in Brazil (2016), which Sawan himself led successfully.
  • Signals.
  • Jefferies sees disposals funding "higher-return growth initiatives like ARC".
  • The ex-BP commercial director quoted in the expert calls suggests buying assets from weaker rivals. That makes bolt-ons more likely than another large deal.
  • For the model.
  • Consolidate ARC from 2-Sep-26.
  • Share count +228m.
  • Net debt +USD 2.5-2.8bn (assumed debt and leases).
  • Synergies ramp to USD 250m.
C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

B664th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: shareholder returns are high and steady rather than stepping up. In 2026 Shell rebalanced toward the dividend and kept the balance sheet unused for the acquisition. The formula plus the falling gearing creates real step-up optionality; it is a dated near-term catalyst, but not yet a management signal.

  • Policy. Distributions of 40-50% of CFFO since CMD25 (previously 30-40%); 52% achieved in 2025.
  • Buybacks.
  • At least USD 3bn a quarter for 19 consecutive quarters: USD 43bn over three years, shrinking the share count by 19%.
  • 2026 so far: USD 3.5bn in Q1, then USD 3.0bn for the Q2 period, of which USD 1.2bn was suspended under ARC transaction rules and restarted in Q3, giving USD 4.2bn in Q3.
  • Dividend. +5% in Q1 2026, to USD 0.3906 a quarter.
  • Headroom under the formula.
  • 2026 distributions are running at about 36% of CFFO (Leadership Foundations report).
  • With FY2026 consensus operating cash flow of USD 61.5bn, 40-50% means USD 24.6-30.8bn, against roughly USD 21-23bn planned (about USD 9bn of dividends plus about USD 12.5-14bn of buybacks).
  • Because the commitment is "through the cycle", management can choose to bank the windfall for deleveraging. Gorman: "building cash reserves provides the flexibility to accelerate buybacks during cycle downturns."
  • Balance sheet.
  • Gearing of 18.7%, net debt USD 41.75bn, and ARC adding about USD 2.5-2.8bn.
  • Morgan Stanley sees net debt at USD 31.9bn by end-2027.
  • Management targets AA credit metrics.
  • Who is pushing. Morgan Stanley argues the low payout "leaves ample room for DPS acceleration"; Jefferies expects the mix to shift toward dividends.
  • For the model.
  • Base case: USD 3.0-3.5bn a quarter of buybacks, dividend growth of 4-5% a year, and leftover cash reducing net debt.
  • Upside case: a step-up or catch-up at Q3 2026 (29-Oct-26) or with the FY2026 results (February 2027).
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–64

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: several material positive change forces are present or plausible. Most are volume or self-help items that are dated and largely within Shell's control, and they make 2027-28 earnings less price-dependent than the 2026 surge suggests.

  1. Middle East volume restoration. - Qatar production fell from 263 to 78 kboe/d. Pearl GTL Train 2 returns in Q1 2027, and Train 1 can resume within weeks of safe export. - Morgan Stanley cites up to about USD 2.5bn of annual post-tax free cash flow from Pearl when it runs unconstrained.
  2. LNG Canada. Phase 1 at full capacity; Phase 2 under construction.
  3. Chemicals. - Positive free cash flow in Q2 2026, the best result since Q3 2021, plus the planned exits. - Removing chronic losses (USD -117m adjusted in Q1 2026) raises normalised earnings.
  4. Digital and AI. Five trillion rows of data from five million sensors, improving LNG plant reliability by up to 2% at zero capital cost.
  5. Fiscal relief options. A possible early end to the UK levy; the suspended Brazilian export levy.
  6. Self-funded UK joint venture. Adura pays cash dividends to its parents (Equinor reported receiving a USD 150m quarterly dividend from it in 2026).
  7. CCS optionality. - Quest stake raised to 20%; Polaris and the Atlas hub at final investment decision. - Northern Lights (33.3%) Phase 2 to 5 mtpa from 2028.
  8. Incentives. The LTIP now rewards normalised FCF per share.
  9. Plausible, not yet announced. A higher cost target; a mobility carve-out (a former executive suggests USD 20-50bn of latent value); further asset recycling.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

C556th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: controlling actors indicate little scope for a change of strategy. Shell is widely held with no controlling or activist shareholder, and the remaining pressure points in a single direction: faster dividend growth, which management already partly accommodated in 2026.

  • History. Third Point's 2021 campaign (a USD 750m position demanding a split) failed on the break-up but did force simplification of the share structure, the London headquarters move and higher cash returns.
  • Today.
  • No director represents an activist.
  • Elliott's position is a short used as a hedge.
  • The climate activists are losing support: Follow This at 12.7%, and the 2025 LNG-disclosure resolution at 20.6%.
  • Large holders (BlackRock is listed at BP; Shell's register is not detailed in the files) exert pressure through conventional engagement.
  • Live pressures.
  • The sell-side and long-only buy-side call for faster dividend growth to fix the valuation discount (Morgan Stanley's upgrade to Overweight rests on it).
  • European ESG-minded investors and Carbon Tracker ask for absolute Scope 3 targets, which Shell refuses.
  • Litigation as a quasi-actor. The Dutch Supreme Court appeal could, if Milieudefensie wins, constrain capital autonomy. That is a low-probability, high-impact item.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

C-629th of 19

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the positive corporate change is broad and strongly resourced, but maturing.

  • Breadth. It covers costs, portfolio, capital framework, volume and governance refresh.
  • Resourcing. USD 60bn+ of CFFO, a low-gearing balance sheet and a stable, aligned leadership team.
  • Articulation. Clear quantified frameworks.
  • It is maturing because the big self-help legs are largely banked.
  • The cost programme stands at USD 5.83bn of USD 5-7bn.
  • Portfolio high-grading is in its later stages.
  • The share count has already fallen 27% since 2019.
  • The 2026 additions are defensive. ARC buys longevity, and the dividend rebalancing makes the returns more durable.
  • What would add new upside. Because the remaining corporate levers are incremental, any new upside to the change thesis requires one of:
  • a new Capital Markets Day ambition (cost target, distribution policy);
  • an explicit policy of faster dividend growth;
  • a chemicals exit;
  • a credible post-2030 liquids plan.
  • For the 18-month model. Corporate change gives high confidence in delivering the frameworks: capex, costs and distributions. It gives little incremental earnings upside beyond what consensus already holds.
C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

D5213th of 19

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: the staircase ahead is real but low in novelty. Near-term steps are well-founded operational and capital-return events. The 6-18 month steps depend on management choosing to upgrade policy. The 18-36 month window contains as many headwinds (the LNG supply wave, the end of the windfall, the post-2030 gap) as novel positives.

Next six months (to about April 2027).

  1. Q3 2026 results, 29-Oct-26. - The USD 42 refining margin, trading, Brent above USD 100, ARC consolidated from 2-Sep. - Likely an earnings beat and further net-debt reduction. - The buyback decision: at least USD 3bn, with a possible step-up given 2026 distributions are about 36% of CFFO. - Novelty: low to moderate. Foundation: high.
  2. The UK budget, 28-Oct-26. Possible levy changes; Bernstein reported talks about an early end. Novelty: moderate. Foundation: low.
  3. The Q4 2026 update note (early January 2027) and FY2026 results with the dividend decision (early February 2027). - The key novel possibility: dividend growth above 4-5%, which is Morgan Stanley's re-rating trigger. - Novelty: high if delivered. Foundation: moderate.
  4. Pearl GTL Train 2 restart in Q1 2027, plus Train 1 when shipping is safe. Novelty: low. Foundation: moderate, since it depends on Hormuz.
  5. Qatar volume restoration. Undated and binary.
  6. Chemicals transactions. The US chemicals sale; European closures. Novelty: moderate.
  7. Closing of the Nigerian gas sale, and further divestments.

Months 6-18 (to about April 2028).

  1. A possible Capital Markets Day in 2027, on the two-year cadence after June 2023 and March 2025: a new cost target, distribution policy and post-2030 volume plan. Novelty: potentially high. Foundation: moderate.
  2. ARC synergies of USD 250m by about September 2027, and the integration record.
  3. The South Africa retail sale closing in 2027.
  4. Namibia appraisal decisions, where the rig is idle until about Q1 2027.
  5. The first PwC audit cycle (FY2027). Noise rather than catalyst.
  6. Net debt toward about USD 32bn (Morgan Stanley, end-2027), which enables larger distributions.
  7. The headwind. Refining normalisation and LNG supply additions start to bite. Consensus FY2027 EPS is already 17% below FY2026.

Months 18-36 (to about late 2029).

  1. LNG Canada Phase 2 construction progress and capex discipline.
  2. Deepwater start-ups (Bonga North; Brazil).
  3. The answer to the post-2030 liquids gap (Namibia development, M&A).
  4. The headwinds. - The LNG oversupply peak, 2028-30. - The end of the UK levy in March 2030 and its replacement by the revenue-based levy. - Carbon costs rising toward USD 4bn by 2035.

How novel is it overall? Low. The market knows the yield, cost and ARC stories. The one genuinely novel, re-rating step is a policy shift to faster dividend growth, or a materially higher payout once net debt falls. That is plausible because of the balance-sheet and formula headroom, but it is unannounced.

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

D5510th of 19

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

D5510th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement: Shell operates in an industry whose structure currently supports returns.

  • What supports returns. Capital discipline among majors, scarce new supply, high barriers, consolidation toward the advantaged and an energy-security policy turn.
  • What makes it fragile. Price-setting power belongs to a cartel with a history of price wars and to geopolitics. The LNG layer faces a 2027-30 supply wave; refining and chemicals face Asian capacity; politicians tax windfalls.
  • Shell's position.
  • It holds a leading and robust position in the most concentrated and defensible layer, LNG portfolio, trading and logistics, which gains from volatility.
  • It holds a mid-table position in upstream, where reserve longevity is decided and must be bought (ARC).
  • Competitors. They help through discipline and erode Shell's relative distinctiveness through improvement (TotalEnergies, Eni, BP's repair).
  • Suppliers. They take back part of the value through service inflation.

For the Consumer, the value chain argues for mid-cycle margins close to 2023-24 levels, not 2026's. It argues for a slow decay of the trading premium as LNG liquidity rises after 2027, an OPEC+ price-war tail, and capex buying power eroding 5-10% a year inside the fixed envelope.

Value chain dynamics

C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?D5812th of 19full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

B+633rd of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the route to market is a net help and a source of advantage. Shell's own trading, shipping and logistics give it physical optionality; asset-light licensing reduces capital in retail. It is weakest in unbranded markets where it resells third-party product, and fragility is low to moderate.

  • Trading and shipping.
  • Supply comes from more than 10 countries.
  • About 200 LNG cargoes were redirected to Europe in 2022.
  • Record third-party sourcing in 2026 kept customers supplied through Qatar's force majeure.
  • Tolling agreements "dramatically increase secondary trading" (an Ecopetrol LNG trader).
  • Retail channels. Company-owned and licensed franchise sites capture premium margins. Raízen in Brazil and Viva Energy in Australia run Shell-branded stations under licence, transferring operating cost while paying royalties. Shell Fleet Solutions serves more than 8 million commercial customers.
  • Weak spots.
  • In unbranded markets with little Shell refining, Shell acts as a reseller with zero switching costs.
  • Reliance on joint-venture partners and licensees is a potential source of margin leakage.
  • Intermediary and supplier counterparty risk is real: the Venture Global arbitration loss.
  • Shipping leases. Leases are part of the route-to-market cost. Freight rates feed through to reported net debt: about USD 3bn of variable lease components were added in Q1 2026.
  • For the model. Trading is the channel through which volatility becomes profit. Its decay as markets calm is the key modelling choice (C-67).
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

C-559th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: competitors behave in ways that help the industry (discipline) and hurt Shell's relative standing (they are improving faster on growth and per-share returns).

  • TotalEnergies is the benchmark rival.
  • Organic production growth of 3% a year to 2030; reserve life of 12.2 years; unit opex of about USD 5/boe; gearing of 13.1%.
  • A 12-month return of +64% against Shell's +41.5%, at a P/E of 8.7x against 10.0x (dashboard).
  • TotalEnergies' Q2 2026 gas trading miss (a 10% miss on Integrated LNG) against Shell's 11% beat in Integrated Gas shows Shell's trading edge.
  • BP is in repair.
  • Buybacks paused; the USD 14-18bn net debt target reached a year early; refining capture of 64%; a pivot to upstream; the Bumerangue discovery.
  • A recovering BP becomes a stronger competitor for "European yield" capital, and a weak BP remains a takeover option that Shell has declined.
  • Eni. Fast organic growth (4-5%) and a distribution yield of about 10% (Redburn: 9.1% total cash distribution yield for FY2026). It competes for the same investor money.
  • Equinor. The leveraged pure play on European gas.
  • The US majors. Volume growth from the Permian and Guyana, at 14.2x 2027 P/E against 9.3x for the European majors.
  • Shell's position.
  • Secure: balance sheet, LNG and trading.
  • Fragile: growth, reserve life and unit cost.
  • What this means. The peers' convergence on discipline and payouts removes Shell's relative distinctiveness in capital returns, which was the source of its 2023-25 outperformance. That is why Shell lagged peers over the last 12 months.
C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?F4217th of 19full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

D5012th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: disruption forces act on every actor in Shell's value chain, but mostly slowly. Shell's integrated, flexible model gains from the volatility they create. Its most exposed points are European chemicals and refining (regulation plus Asian competition) and Marketing (EVs); its least exposed is the LNG and trading core.

  • On demand.
  • EVs and efficiency on road fuels: slow, negative, aimed at Marketing.
  • AI and data centres on gas-for-power: positive, aimed at Integrated Gas.
  • Price-driven fuel switching in Asia: negative for LNG demand growth.
  • On competition.
  • The US and Qatar LNG wave on LNG prices and optimisation margins, 2027-30: negative.
  • National oil companies building portfolios: negative for intermediary margins.
  • On regulators.
  • Carbon pricing and CBAM on European refining and chemicals: negative. It accelerates closures, which can help survivors.
  • Windfall taxes on upstream.
  • On suppliers.
  • The services oligopoly and tight rigs on project costs: negative.
  • AI and digital tools on opex: positive.
  • On Shell itself. AI-driven reliability; capital-light retail.
  • Fragility. Moderate overall. Disruption is unlikely to break the business model within four years. It shapes the mix: more gas and LNG, less chemicals and retail fuel.

Industry structure
