C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

B725th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Shell ranks number one or two where it earns its premium returns: LNG portfolio and trading, branded retail and lubricants. In upstream oil and gas production, the business that sets reserve longevity, it is a mid-ranked supermajor (number three or four).

  • LNG.
  • Shell runs the largest LNG portfolio of any listed company and handles close to a fifth of global LNG demand, against about 10% for TotalEnergies.
  • Its operating liquefaction capacity (45 mtpa) leads the integrated oil companies, and quarterly LNG sales run at 18-19 Mt.
  • Only QatarEnergy is larger as a producer, and it is a national oil company.
  • Retail and lubricants. 44,109 stations, the largest network, about 40% of the supermajors' combined sites; number one global lubricants supplier for 19 consecutive years.
  • Upstream.
  • About 2.7-2.8 mboe/d in 2025, rising to about 3.4 mboe/d in 2027 with ARC (Redburn).
  • ExxonMobil is far larger (Permian alone 1.8 mboe/d in 2026; 5.4 mboe/d targeted by 2030). Chevron is comparable after Hess.
  • Shell is ahead of TotalEnergies (2.53 mboe/d) and BP (2.31).
  • Refining. Mid-sized at 1.4 mb/d across 7 refineries. Throughput is only 51% of oil production, against 106% at ExxonMobil, so Shell resells third-party product in parts of Marketing.
  • Trading. One of the largest integrated trading houses alongside BP's; the independent traders are larger in oil.
  • What this means. The ranking matches the earnings mix in 2026: trading and LNG beat while upstream was in line. It also matches the investment debate: leadership where volatility is monetised, mid-table where longevity is decided.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

D5811th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: concentration differs sharply by layer.

  • Crude oil. Production is spread across many producers (national oil companies dominate), but the price is administered by a cartel, OPEC+. The top five integrated majors control more than 30% of global deepwater capex.
  • LNG supply. Concentrated and getting more so. Qatar produces about 20% of global output, and the US and Qatar will hold 57% of capacity by 2031 (US 33%).
  • LNG portfolio and marketing. Concentrated and steep at the top. Shell is about twice the next integrated company (TotalEnergies). The top five portfolio players plausibly exceed 30%, though the files do not give an exact figure for the top five against numbers six to ten.
  • Fuel retail and lubricants. Fragmented.
  • Shell's suppliers. Subsea is very concentrated: three firms take about 98% of tree awards; TechnipFMC and Subsea 7 hold 65% of SURF contracts.
  • What this means. Shell's most defensible profit pool sits in the concentrated, steep layer, LNG portfolio and trading. Its largest earnings driver, the commodity price, sits in a cartel-administered market that Shell cannot influence. Its main cost exposure sits with a concentrated supplier oligopoly that currently has pricing power.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

D5213th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: market shares among the majors are stable over three to five years. New entrants have taken a large share of incremental LNG supply (US developers, Qatar's expansion) and of upstream growth (US shale consolidators, Guyana). Shell's own share of the growing LNG market is drifting slightly lower by choice ("value over volume").

  • Upstream. Incremental global oil supply has come mainly from US shale (Permian), Guyana and OPEC+ spare capacity, not from European majors. Shell's organic 1% CAGR means its share of oil production is flat to falling; ARC lifts its gas share.
  • LNG.
  • US developers (Venture Global, Cheniere expansions) are taking a large slice of new capacity, with the US at 33% of global LNG by 2031.
  • Their behaviour differs from the incumbents': Venture Global sold commissioning cargoes spot instead of delivering to its offtakers, and Shell lost the arbitration.
  • National oil companies and traders are building their own portfolios (ADNOC's international expansion; the Mercuria-Eni trading joint venture).
  • Shell targets 4-5% a year of LNG sales growth against 6% a year market growth over 2026-30. An expert consultant calls Shell's LNG growth targets "insufficient" for data-centre demand.
  • Retail. Shell's share is falling deliberately as it sells about 500 stations a year and exits markets (South Africa, Jiffy Lube).
  • What this means. Stability protects Shell's economics. Its niche (flexibility and trading) depends less on share than on scale and logistics, so a modest loss of LNG share does not erode the moat, provided the portfolio stays the largest and most flexible.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

D5812th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: market share is consolidating toward the largest and most advantaged in almost every layer. Shell is among the advantaged on balance sheet, LNG scale, trading and integration, and among the disadvantaged on reserve longevity and organic growth. Its consolidation role is therefore to buy the longevity it lacks.

  • Where consolidation is happening.
  • US shale: record USD 200bn of deals in 2023; ExxonMobil-Pioneer, Chevron-Hess.
  • UK North Sea: Adura (Shell and Equinor), NEO NEXT+, Ithaca with Eni's UK assets.
  • Norway: BlueNord and Var Energi.
  • Subsea services: the Saipem-Subsea 7 combination (USD 300m of targeted synergies).
  • LNG: toward portfolio players with balance sheets.
  • Where the advantage lies.
  • Low cost: TotalEnergies at under USD 5/boe; Shell at USD 8.54/boe sits mid-table.
  • Reserve life: Shell is at the bottom of the European distribution at 7.4-7.8 years, against ExxonMobil above 15 years and TotalEnergies at 12.2.
  • Balance sheet: Shell is a leader.
  • Integration and flexibility: Shell is a leader.
  • What this means. Acquired resource is getting dearer as everyone pivots back to barrels (ARC's premium), so Shell pays a "longevity tax" that peers with organic pipelines (TotalEnergies, Eni) avoid. Expect Shell to keep acquiring bolt-on resource, funded partly by divestments.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

C-589th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the integrated majors put returns and margin first. Their attitude is benign and cooperative in effect, if not by design. OPEC+ is the swing actor whose volume-versus-price stance decides crude prices, and Chinese refiners and chemical producers behave volume-first. Crude prices are defended when demand falls (OPEC+ cuts); LNG, refining and chemical prices are not.

  • The majors.
  • Capex caps: TotalEnergies USD 14-17bn, Chevron USD 18-21bn, BP USD 13-13.5bn, Shell USD 20-22bn.
  • Payout frameworks of 40-50% of CFFO, and project screening at USD 50 Brent.
  • None is buying share.
  • US shale. Disciplined since 2020.
  • OPEC+. Added supply in 2025, which pushed 2026 Brent expectations toward USD 59 before the conflict. Spare capacity is now about 0.9% of demand.
  • Chinese refiners and petrochemicals. Export-led and volume-first, which keeps chemical margins at cyclical lows and pressures Asian refining.
  • What happens when volume falls.
  • Crude: OPEC+ historically cuts to support price.
  • LNG: no cartel, so oversupply passes straight into price (Bernstein: USD 9/mmbtu spot over 2026-28).
  • Refining and chemicals: margins collapse with capacity additions.
  • For the model. Crude is the best-defended price (an OPEC+ floor); LNG spot, refining and chemicals are the least defended. That is why refining and trading are the lines to normalise hardest.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

D4611th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the majors' attitude has been stable for five years, reinforced by shareholder demands and the 2020 trauma, and is if anything strengthening. The industry's unstable, "Michael O'Leary" actor is OPEC+, Saudi Arabia in particular. The shark-attack scenario is realistic once Hormuz reopens and stranded Gulf barrels return.

  • Majors.
  • Discipline has held through the 2022 windfall and the 2026 spike.
  • A small loosening at the margin: TotalEnergies extended capex to USD 14-17bn at CMD 2026 for "discovery optionality"; the resource race is restarting; ExxonMobil and Chevron pursue volume.
  • Peers' sensitivity to investor pressure (Elliott at BP) keeps them disciplined.
  • OPEC+ record. Price wars in 2014-16 and March 2020; a 2025 push to regain market share. Saudi Arabia's behaviour is situation-dependent and reactive.
  • The shark-attack scenario. After a settlement, stranded inventories and restored Gulf flows combine with OPEC+ volume ambitions and non-OPEC supply growth peaking by early 2027. Maybank and Deutsche Bank see USD 70 Brent in 2027; Redburn sees USD 70 long term. OPEC+ could choose market share over price, as in 2014 and 2020.
  • For Shell. The buyback holds to USD 50 Brent; dividend safety sits lower still. A price war would hit earnings (Brent USD 10/bbl ≈ USD 3.7bn of CFFO) but not the dividend.
  • LNG. Qatar's North Field expansion is volume-first by design, and the US merchant developers are price-takers who keep building.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

D5610th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the industry structure supports returns but is fragile.

  • What supports returns.
  • Capital discipline among majors and shale producers.
  • Conventional discoveries at record lows, so little new long-cycle supply.
  • High entry barriers: LNG costs above USD 1,000 per tonne of capacity; deepwater capital needs; government control of concessions.
  • Consolidation, and a policy turn toward energy security.
  • Why it is fragile.
  • The most important price is set by a cartel with a history of price wars and by geopolitics.
  • The LNG layer faces a 2027-30 supply wave with no cartel.
  • Refining and chemical margins are set by Asian and Middle Eastern capacity additions.
  • High prices invite windfall taxes.
  • Shell's position. Its most robust profit pool, flexibility and trading, is the one that gains from volatility, a partial natural hedge. Its upstream relies on the cartel's support, and its downstream on continued disruption.
  • For the model. Base the mid-cycle on a disciplined industry. Carry an explicit OPEC+ market-share scenario (Brent USD 55-60 for 12-18 months) as the main structural tail.

C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

F5513th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: substitution was overestimated in 2020-23 and is weaker than feared over the model horizon, though high 2026 prices are re-accelerating it. It is a moderate threat to Marketing volumes and European gas demand, and a low threat to upstream and LNG to 2030.

  • The substitutes.
  • EVs, for road fuels and lubricants.
  • Renewables plus storage, for gas in European power; the IEA sees European gas demand down 8-10% over 2024-30.
  • Coal, which replaces LNG in Asia when LNG is expensive.
  • Efficiency.
  • Not substituting at scale yet. Hydrogen (projects cancelled), synthetic fuels (technology readiness level 6, so funds will not invest until the 2030s) and biofuels (Rotterdam cancelled).
  • The direction of change. High prices accelerate substitution. Bernstein flags structural fuel switching in developing Asia; US coal falls to 15% of generation by 2050 while gas, nuclear and renewables more than double.
  • For the model.
  • Marketing fuel volumes drift down about 1-2% a year (my assumption) and are offset by convenience and premium products.
  • No substitution haircut on upstream or LNG volumes before 2030.

4a

Business quality

C608th of 198 answers
Judgement60C-4166C-4266C-4352C-4464C-4542C-4646C-4758
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

C608th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement: Shell's competitive advantage is concentrated and genuine.

  • The strong cluster. The world's leading integrated LNG portfolio, destination flexibility and physically backed trading, integrated from its own gas resources. It has proved non-inferior, and in fact superior, utility to customers in the 2026 supply crisis, and it widens with volatility.
  • The supporting advantages. Scale, balance sheet, branded retail and lubricants, and deepwater execution.
  • What it lacks. Unit-cost leadership, pricing power in the usual sense, and above all reserve longevity, its clearest competitive disadvantage against TotalEnergies, ExxonMobil and Eni.
  • The per-share mechanics (buybacks, cost ratchet) carry the improvement in financial outputs over the model horizon.

For the Consumer: model Shell's above-benchmark earnings capacity as a trading and optimisation layer whose size scales with market volatility, and assume no pricing premium elsewhere. Expect the advantage to defend margins in shocks better than peers, not to grow volumes faster.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

C667th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Shell's real, sustainable advantages sit in one cluster: LNG scale plus destination flexibility plus physically backed trading plus wellhead-to-water integration. Volatility is widening it, as 2026 proved. Its brand and scale advantages in retail and lubricants are real but narrowing. Its upstream cost position is middling. Its resource-access advantage is concentrated and politically fragile. It holds no reserve-longevity advantage; it holds a deficit.

  1. LNG scale and flexibility (production- and scale-based): strong and widening. - The largest listed LNG portfolio, handling about 20% of global demand. - Destination-flexible offtake plus real-time analytics let Shell divert cargoes to premium regions. Regional competitors cannot replicate this. - Proof under stress: in Q2 2026 Integrated Gas beat consensus by 11% while its production fell 31% sequentially. - The moat widens when markets dislocate and narrows in a calm, liquid, oversupplied market (2027-30).
  2. Physically backed trading (scale and know-how): strong. - Trading added an estimated USD 3bn to Q1 2026 earnings. - Supply sourced from more than 10 countries. - Experts describe it as "virtually impossible for regional players to replicate". - It makes up the bulk of the beats in both 2026 quarters.
  3. Wellhead-to-water integration: strong. Equity gas backfills Shell's liquefaction (QGC, Prelude, LNG Canada fed by ARC's Montney), avoiding third-party feedgas costs and margin leakage.
  4. Retail and lubricants brand (customer-related): moderate and narrowing. - 44,109 stations; number one in lubricants for 19 years; loyalty programmes. - But premium fuels earn no premium margin, unbranded markets have zero switching costs, Shell is divesting the network, and EVs erode the base.
  5. Upstream cost and scale: moderate. - Unit cost of USD 8.54/boe, down 47% since 2014: in line with Chevron, above TotalEnergies' under USD 5. - Deepwater clusters in the Gulf of Mexico and Brazil; breakevens under USD 60/bbl; best-in-class deepwater drilling per experts.
  6. Sovereign concession access (regulatory or political): strong but fragile. Qatar (Pearl GTL, 140,000 boe/d), Oman Block 10 and Brunei. These are exactly the assets hit by the 2026 conflict.
  7. Refining complexity and capture: moderate. A 55% capture rate in H1 2026, against 35% at TotalEnergies and 33% at Eni, but behind BP's 64%. Throughput of only 51% of production limits it.

Missing or negative: reserve longevity (bottom of the peer group) and organic growth (1% a year).

C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

B665th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Shell is very large: the largest European energy company by revenue and the second-largest Western integrated company after ExxonMobil by market value. Its scale confers optionality, bargaining power and resilience, not unit-cost leadership.

  • Size.
  • Revenue USD 266.9bn in 2025 on the brokers' basis, about USD 352bn expected for 2026.
  • Market capitalisation USD 309.6bn, against ExxonMobil's USD 621bn and BP's USD 115bn; enterprise value about USD 294bn.
  • 85,000 employees.
  • Production of 2.7-2.8 mboe/d, about 3.4 mboe/d in 2027.
  • LNG sales of about 70 Mt a year; 44,109 retail sites; 5,400 km of pipelines and 1.5bn barrels shipped a year by Shell Pipeline.
  • What scale gives Shell. 1. The ability to absorb a USD 16.5bn acquisition without straining the balance sheet. 2. A diversified supply portfolio that kept customers whole through Qatar's force majeure. 3. Bargaining power with contractors and access to the largest projects. 4. Funding capacity to sustain buybacks through downturns. 5. Trading that monetises the information and optionality of a large physical book.
  • What scale does not give Shell. Lower unit cost than TotalEnergies; faster decisions (experts describe Shell as "culturally slow" and bureaucratic, the Wisdom Library's "Big Company Disease"); or growth.
  • The test of "scale needs a bounded market". It is satisfied only in LNG portfolio and logistics, where minimum efficient scale is high and the gap to the next integrated player is about twice. It is not satisfied in upstream oil, where national oil companies and US majors are larger.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

C648th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Shell's surplus customer utility is demonstrably not inferior, and in LNG supply reliability and flexibility it is demonstrably leading. 2026 was a live test that Shell passed when a sovereign competitor (QatarEnergy) declared force majeure.

  • LNG.
  • Through QatarEnergy's force majeure from 2-Mar-26, Shell "safeguarded LNG deliveries" with record third-party sourcing and LNG Canada at full capacity, so customers were not exposed to shortages.
  • Destination flexibility, tolling agreements and portfolio optimisation are valued by buyers: the Ecopetrol trader cites secondary-trading benefits.
  • Long-term contracts of about 15 years show that buyers value security of supply from Shell.
  • The counter-example: the arbitration loss against Venture Global shows Shell as a buyer exposed to an unreliable supplier, not as an unreliable supplier.
  • Retail and lubricants.
  • The number one lubricants position for 19 years, OEM relationships, premium fuels (13.5% of volume) and Shell Fleet Solutions (more than 8 million customers) show utility.
  • Limits: unbranded customers show no loyalty, and the Pearl outage forced Shell to ration high-value base oils.
  • Products. Record 102% refinery utilisation kept jet and diesel supply flowing in tight markets.
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

D4211th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: demonstrable pricing power is limited. Shell takes benchmark prices on almost everything, and the evidence of applied pricing is weak in Marketing. Its genuine "pricing" capability is earning a premium for flexibility and reliability during dislocations. That is evidenced but episodic, so it should be modelled as a volatility rent that decays, not as pricing power.

  • Evidence for.
  • LNG contracts are extended "without structural margin deterioration" even when sovereign buyers push for renegotiation after spot slumps.
  • In 2026 Shell captured premium spot margins with its uncontracted volumes and trading.
  • A 55% refining capture rate against peers' 33-35%.
  • Jefferies calls Shell the "prime name to capture energy volatility".
  • Evidence against.
  • Premium fuels earn no gross-margin premium over regular fuel.
  • Lubricant margins fell in Q2 2026.
  • Shell is a price-taking reseller in unbranded retail.
  • The chemicals business cannot pass costs through: negative adjusted earnings of USD -117m in Q1 2026.
  • Windfall taxes truncate upstream price capture (the UK's 78% marginal rate).
  • For the model. No price above benchmark except two separate lines:
  • a refining-capture assumption of about 50-55% of indicator margins (Jefferies uses 50% from 2027);
  • a trading and optimisation line that is normal in calm markets and elevated only in dislocated ones.
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

D4610th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Shell does not share its scale economies with customers, by stated strategy ("value over volume") or in practice. Cost savings go to shareholders and the balance sheet, not to lower prices. For a commodity producer that is normal and not a weakness in itself. In its customer-facing businesses, though, it limits share defence, and Shell's deliberate retreat from unbranded retail and lower-margin sites is the visible consequence.

  • Messaging and culture.
  • "Deliver more value with less emissions."
  • Every investment is benchmarked against buybacks.
  • Retail high-grading sells about 500 lower-margin stations a year.
  • Where sharing exists de facto: reliability. Absorbing the Qatar shortfall through third-party sourcing is a form of sharing scale, since only a portfolio of Shell's size could do it. Supply-agreement continuity after divestments (Jiffy Lube, lubricants) is another.
  • What it means. Shell will not defend volume share in contested downstream markets. Its competitive model is to concentrate on the layers where scale cannot be matched (LNG portfolio, trading) rather than to undercut.
  • For the model. Marketing volumes are flat to falling while unit margins hold.
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

C-589th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: gravitational forces work both ways at Shell, and in the model horizon the per-share forces win.

  • The mechanical forces that improve per-share outputs each period. 1. Buybacks shrink the share count by about 3.5-4.5% a year at today's price. 2. The cost-programme ratchet: savings, once embedded, persist. 3. Portfolio high-grading removes low-return capital. 4. The LNG contract book rolls onto current terms, a tailwind in tight markets and a headwind in loose ones. 5. Debt repaid in a windfall does not come back (Industry Digest), so the equity keeps a larger share of enterprise value.
  • The mechanical forces that erode it each period. 1. Natural field decline. Maintaining flat liquids at 1.4 mb/d "demands massive capital", and reserve life is only 7.4-7.8 years. 2. Rising carbon costs (USD 1bn in 2026 toward USD 4bn by 2035). 3. Service-cost inflation. 4. Erosion of Marketing volumes from EVs.
  • Net.
  • Within the 18-month to four-year horizon, per-share forces beat asset-level erosion, because ARC and the Qatar restoration temporarily offset decline.
  • After about 2030 the balance shifts unless the liquids gap is filled.
  • Heroic dynamism is not needed for the model's per-share growth; buybacks and costs deliver it.
  • Restoring reserve longevity does need heroic effort, or more M&A.
  • Against peers. TotalEnergies' gravitational pull is stronger (low unit cost plus organic growth plus long reserve life). Shell's is per-share-mechanical rather than asset-driven.

Judgement: Shell is run by a high-quality, disciplined, shareholder-aligned management team that acts decisively and communicates its framework clearly.

  • The limits.
  • Its ambition is deliberately narrow (value over volume).
  • Its team is insular.

It does not support assuming growth beyond the announced levers, and it argues for a modest operational-risk allowance in 2027-28.
C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

D5411th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Shell's ambition is large but narrow. It is to be the best per-share value creator and capital returner among the majors, not to be the biggest or fastest-growing. Next to TotalEnergies (growth plus power), Eni (4-5% growth, satellites) or ExxonMobil (volume), Shell's ambition is lower on growth and innovation and higher on discipline.

  • Evidence of drive.
  • Sawan's "we are not at the full potential of this company" (October 2025).
  • An expert notes he takes "heavy strategic inspiration" from ExxonMobil's high-return upstream focus.

  • A willingness to act decisively: ARC; LNG Canada Phase 2 fast-tracked.
  • Evidence of limits.
  • Organic growth of about 1%.
  • LNG growth below market, which an expert calls "insufficient".
  • Exploration caution (Namibia single-well).
  • "Strategic patience", resisting acquisitions unless terms are very favourable.
  • For the model. Do not expect management to chase volume or to launch large new growth platforms. Expect further discipline-led actions: costs, exits, buybacks, bolt-ons.
C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

C-628th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: the strategy is smart, coherent and dynamic in its capital framework. Its weakest point is resource longevity.

  • Its strengths.
  • It is designed to perform across a wide range of environments. Sawan: the energy system is "inherently becoming more volatile", so Shell controls asset performance rather than predicting prices.
  • It widens the moat where Shell is strongest: the LNG portfolio, the Canadian gas-to-LNG hub, trading.
  • Its distribution framework (a share of CFFO, with a buyback floor at USD 50) flexes rather than breaks.
  • The 2023 pivot away from low-return renewables was bold and has been vindicated by peers following (BP, Equinor).
  • Its weaknesses.
  • Growth is explicitly not a priority.
  • The answer to the post-2030 liquids gap is "M&A or exploration", which is a dependency rather than a plan.
  • It relies on trading returns that are partly cyclical.
  • For the model. The strategy is highly modellable: fixed capex bands, distribution rules, a cost target. Its behaviour in a downturn is predictable: maintain the buyback, use the balance sheet, keep the dividend growing.
C-57

How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

B704th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: judged on actions, Wael Sawan is an excellent operator-allocator and an enabler, not an ossifier. He has done what he said, fast. The qualifications: his record is so far in a mostly favourable price environment; his growth ambition is low by design; and the share price has lagged peers for the last 12 months.

  • Actions since January 2023. 1. Structural costs out by USD 5.83bn, about 2.5 years ahead of the original schedule. 2. Low-carbon spending cut; Rotterdam biofuels halted and then cancelled despite sunk costs; exits from offshore wind, Sprng and the European onshore renewables. 3. Distributions raised to 40-50% of CFFO; dividend +15% at CMD23; buybacks of at least USD 3bn for 19 quarters. 4. Portfolio surgery: Nigeria onshore, Singapore, Jiffy Lube, South Africa, Na Kika, Adura. 5. Rejected a BP bid, accepting the M&A head's resignation. 6. Bought ARC and took Phase 2 of LNG Canada to final investment decision. 7. Simplified the organisation: Presidents, an Executive Committee of eight, the technical functions folded in.
  • Outcomes.
  • TSR of 30.2% from January 2023 to May 2025, ahead of BP and TotalEnergies.
  • But the last 12 months: Shell +32.6% against a peer average of 47.3% to September 2026 (TotalEnergies +52.3%); dashboard 12-month +41.5% against TotalEnergies +64% and Eni +83.6%.
  • The market has recently rewarded growth and leverage over Shell's defensiveness.
  • Pre-CEO record. Integrated Gas earnings went from USD 8.06bn to USD 22.21bn (2021-22), heavily price-aided. The Appomattox start-up came early and under budget; the BG integration in Brazil was led successfully.
  • Style. A "harsher" performance culture, decisive, and benchmarking every investment against buybacks.

How high a priority is hiring well for the chief executive, and how great is the leadership team the chief executive has built (an enabler)?

Judgement: Sawan prioritises performance and simplicity over breadth of talent. He has built a competent, cohesive, experienced but insular leadership team.

C-58

How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

D4614th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Shell is very well resourced financially but lean, and increasingly thin, in human capital, exploration and maintenance. The "ant colony" was built for financial resilience, not for resource or capability resilience.

  • Exploration right-sized; the Namibia rig handed back.

How clear, straightforward and helpful is the company's communication, including on the strategy and the driver transmission mechanism?

  • A record 102% refinery utilisation; Q2 capex underspend.
  • Mid-term.
  • FY2024 capex of USD 21bn against USD 22-25bn guided.
  • Q3 2024 upstream volumes 110 kb/d above the guided range.
  • 2025 distributions at 52% of CFFO, above the 40-50% range.
  • Project delivery at 99.8% of budget.
  • Misses. Q2 2023 adjusted earnings (-10% against consensus) and Q4 2025 adjusted earnings (chemicals and gas), both trading- or price-driven rather than execution misses.
  • In a tougher environment. In 2025 (Brent about USD 69, weak chemicals) management still delivered costs and buybacks, while ROACE fell to 9.4%.
  • The pre-Sawan counter-example. The 2020 dividend cut by about two-thirds (FactSet DPS USD 1.87 to USD 0.63), the reason the current framework is designed to flex.
C-62

To what extent does the company hold outlier standards, driving higher, faster and better, with a record of out-executing competitors?

C-609th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Shell holds outlier standards in some measures and is a strong but not outlier performer in others. It out-executes its European peers on reliability, refining capture, buyback consistency and trading in a crisis. It does not out-execute TotalEnergies on unit cost or organic growth, or ExxonMobil on development speed.

  • Where it is an outlier.
  • Refining capture of 55% against 35% at TotalEnergies and 33% at Eni.
  • Refinery utilisation of 102% against 87% at TotalEnergies.
  • The 19-quarter buyback streak, unmatched.
  • A share-count reduction of 19% in three years, against 15% at BP; only Equinor's 21% is higher.
  • Cost-programme speed.
  • In Q2 2026 Integrated Gas beat consensus by 11% while TotalEnergies' Integrated LNG missed by 10% in the same market.
  • Where it is not.
  • Unit opex of USD 8.54/boe against TotalEnergies' under USD 5.
  • Organic growth of 1% against TotalEnergies' 3% and Eni's 3.7%.
  • Exploration: Eni's 80% success rate and USD 1/boe finding cost.
  • Development speed: ExxonMobil's Guyana; Eni's 4.4-year discovery-to-production.
  • BP's 64% refining capture.
C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

A662nd of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: the evidence shows Shell puts shareholders first in capital allocation and incentives, with prudent governance scaffolding largely in place. There are two blemishes: a real audit-governance lapse, and the choice to fund ARC with equity while buying back stock.

  • For shareholders first.
  • Every investment is benchmarked against buybacks; low-return projects are killed (Rotterdam); value-destroying M&A is rejected (BP).
  • 40-50% of CFFO returned.
  • CEO shareholding of 900% of salary and CFO of 550%.
  • LTIP on normalised FCF per share and relative TSR; three-year post-vesting holding; malus and clawback; no windfall adjustments.
  • Governance structure.
  • A single share class with one vote each and no takeover defences.
  • 12 of 14 directors independent; active refreshment with average tenure under five years.
  • Related-party dealings limited to ordinary joint ventures.
  • A refreshed Code of Conduct (January 2026); 1,983 helpline cases in 2025, of which 67% of 497 closed cases were substantiated.
  • Against.
  • The EY partner-rotation breach led to amended 20-Fs for FY2023-24. No restatement was needed, but no peer had a comparable lapse.
  • 228m shares issued for ARC.
  • CEO pay of £13.8m in 2025, up from £8.6m as the 2023-25 award vested at 149% of target. That is near the median and price-aided.
  • No open-market insider buying; the Upstream President sold more than £1m.
  • Why. The board, and since 2021 the shareholder base, has hard-wired returns into pay and policy. The Third Point episode and the 2020 dividend cut created the institutional memory.
C-64

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

B+683rd of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Shell is an enlightened, disciplined capital allocator and the European leader in repurchasing its own shares. It is a programmatic rather than opportunistic repurchaser, and its returns on new capital are good rather than outstanding.

  • Repurchases.
  • About 27% of shares retired since 2019 (19%, or USD 43bn, in the last three years).
  • Mostly at forward P/E of about 5-9x, against 11.15x today: highly accretive, the single largest source of per-share value creation under Sawan.
  • Discipline.
  • Capex cut to USD 20-22bn; low-carbon below 10% of capital employed; hurdle rates of 15% and 11%; sunk costs written off (Rotterdam); BP rejected.
  • Allocation errors inherited. The impairments of about USD 16bn over 2023-25 mostly reflect pre-Sawan projects: the Monaca petrochemical plant (USD 14bn, labelled value-destroying by IEEFA), chemicals, renewables.
  • ARC. A defensible, strategic use of capital at about a 9-10.5% free-cash-flow yield on EV. Funding it with equity while buying back shows Shell treated its shares as fully valued at the time, which is sensible, and protected its AA metrics.
  • The limit. The buyback is steady at about USD 3bn a quarter whatever the valuation, not dynamic. At today's multiple each dollar retires about half as many shares as in 2022-24, so the per-share engine is becoming less powerful.

Financial profile

Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

D5512th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement: Shell's economic strength is solid but not outstanding.

  • Where it is strong.
  • Cash conversion, with a counter-cyclical working-capital cushion.
  • A low and comfortable balance sheet.
  • Well-specified, per-share-driven compounding of about 5-8% a year at constant prices (my estimate).
  • Where it is only moderate.
  • Returns: about 11-12% ROACE through the cycle.
  • Margins: moderate and cyclical.
  • Earnings quality: excluded impairments of roughly USD 3-5bn a year and heavy derivative and working-capital noise.
  • Reinvestment opportunities: limited, which caps the fair multiple at a yield-stock level.
  • The dominant feature. Large price leverage on a fixed cost base, which makes 2026 earnings about 40-50% above a normalised USD 24-26bn.
  • Self-help left. About USD 1-2bn of further cost and exit benefits, plus volume restoration and ARC, plus about USD 4bn of capex relief in 2027. Enough to make 2027-28 decline more gently than the deck implies; not enough to grow through it.

For the Consumer, the economic engine to model:

  • adjusted earnings of about USD 24-27bn at USD 70-75 Brent by 2028, against FY2028 consensus of USD 25.7bn;
  • free cash flow, after leases, of about USD 22-27bn;
  • a share count falling about 3.5-4.5% a year.
C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

F4814th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Shell's earnings-per-share growth is overwhelmingly price-driven and not durable at the headline level: about +102% in 2026, then declines in 2027-29. Its underlying, durable per-share growth engine at constant prices is about 5-8% a year, from buybacks, the remaining cost savings, ARC and volume restoration. Consensus does not credit that engine after 2027.

  • History, brokers' basis.
  • EPS USD 4.20 (2023), USD 3.76 (2024, -10%), USD 3.15 (2025, -16%).
  • Revenue USD 316.6bn, 284.3bn (-10%), 266.9bn (-6%).
  • The longer view. On FactSet's adjusted diluted EPS, USD 1.90 (2019) rose to USD 3.41 (2025), about +10% a year, while revenue fell about 24%. The whole gain came from per-share and cost mechanics: shares down 27% and Viewforth-definition net income up from USD 15.8bn to USD 21.6bn.
  • Consensus.
  • EPS USD 6.37 (+102%), 5.28 (-17%), 4.83 (-8.5%), 4.62 (-4.4%, only 8 brokers).
  • Revenue +31.8%, -8.0%, -5.1%, +0.4%.
  • Durability.
  • 2026 is not repeatable: H1 2026 adjusted earnings of USD 16.75bn compare with USD 18.5bn for all of 2025.
  • The repeatable engine has five parts:
    • buybacks at about 3.5-4.5% a year;
    • the remaining structural savings of about USD 0.5-1.2bn to the top of the 2028 target, worth about 2-4% of normalised earnings;
    • ARC (about USD 1.5bn of free cash flow plus USD 250m of synergies);
    • restoring the Qatar and Pearl volumes;
    • removing chemical losses.
  • Against that sit about USD 0.3bn a year of extra carbon cost, inflation, and natural decline.
  • The net constant-price per-share growth is my estimate of 5-8% a year, and is plausibly the basis of management's "FCF per share above 10% a year to 2030", helped by a low 2025 base.
  • For the model. Build EPS as normalised earnings plus a decaying windfall layer, divided by a falling share count. The FY2028-29 consensus decline (-8.5%, -4.4%) implies brokers assume further price normalisation and do not credit per-share growth. That is conservative if the deck stabilises at about USD 70-75.
C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

D4613th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: the immediate scope to deploy capital at high returns is moderate in quality and limited in quantity, and the limit is deliberate. Shell is a harvest-and-return model: reinvestment is about 35-46% of cash flow and returns go to shareholders. The fair multiple is therefore a yield multiple (about 7-10x P/E; Redburn's EV/DACF floor of 6.0x), not a growth multiple. The mid-term potential depends on refilling the resource base.

  • High-return opportunities.
  • Deepwater: Gulf of Mexico and Brazil; breakevens under USD 60/bbl; the 15% Upstream hurdle; Whale built on Vito's design.
  • LNG backfill, described as "high-return backfill projects".
  • LNG Canada Phase 2: AECO-indexed feedgas that is "highly competitive".
  • Montney via ARC: USD 1.3bn a year of organic capex.
  • Nigerian deepwater (Bonga North).
  • Namibia: unproven.
  • Constraints.
  • Capex is capped at USD 20-22bn, and ARC's spend is absorbed inside it, which crowds out legacy reinvestment.
  • The upstream reinvestment rate is 46% against 59% at BP.
  • Morgan Stanley says Shell lags global peers on portfolio duration.
  • Low-carbon capital is held under 10% of capital employed at a hurdle of at least 10%.
  • The implication over three to five years. Return on new invested capital on incremental projects is plausibly 11-15%+, but the volume of such projects is not enough to grow organic production. Production growth therefore needs M&A.
  • For the model. Capex at USD 20-22bn a year for 2027-28. Production flat organically after the 2027 step-up from ARC and the restoration. Free cash flow allocated to distributions and debt.
C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

D5213th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: returns are mid-pack and cyclical: about 10-12% ROACE through the cycle, peaking near 12-13%.

  • The record.
  • ROACE 12.8-13.4% (2023), 11.3% (2024), 9.4% adjusted or 9.8% (2025).
  • 9.9% in Q1 2026 and 12.4% annualised in Q2 2026, which leads the European integrated peers this quarter.
  • The peers. TotalEnergies 12.6% (2025) and 13.9% on a trailing basis at June 2026; Equinor 14.5% (2025); Aramco 20.7%; BP about 14% on a price-adjusted basis.
  • What drags it. Underperforming capital: Jefferies counts about USD 45bn, mainly chemicals; Redburn counted USD 15bn in underperforming low-carbon capital. Recurring impairments (C-54) show the same drag.
  • Incremental returns (ROIC on new capital).
  • ARC: about 9% free-cash-flow yield on EV before synergies and about 10.5% with them (my calculation).
  • LNG projects at an 11% hurdle; deepwater at 15%+.
  • Equity and tangible returns.
  • Consensus ROE about 19% in 2026 (USD 36.1bn over USD 189bn of equity) and about 12.6% in 2028 (USD 25.7bn over USD 204bn).
  • Price to book about 1.6x (USD 54 against a consensus book value of USD 33.9 a share for 2026).
  • For the model. Mid-cycle ROACE of about 11-12% (adjusted, post-tax), rising only as the chemicals and renewables capital is exited.
C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

C627th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: underlying cash conversion is strong, about 1.0-1.1x of adjusted earnings into lease-adjusted free cash flow at normal prices. The reported numbers are extremely noisy because working capital swings by up to USD 11bn a quarter.

  • History (FactSet free cash flow over reported net income).
  • 84% (2022), 106% (2023), 151% (2024), 75% (2025).
  • Free cash flow USD 35.6bn, 20.5bn, 24.3bn and 13.3bn respectively.
  • 2025 operating cash flow fell to USD 40.7bn, hit by working capital.
  • Consensus free cash flow: USD 38.8bn, 32.5bn, 29.6bn, 27.3bn (about 1.1x net income).
  • Leases. FactSet free cash flow excludes lease repayments (USD 1.34bn in Q1 2026; about USD 5bn+ a year), so lease-adjusted free cash flow is about USD 5bn lower each year.
  • Working capital.
  • Q1 2026: -USD 11.2bn, driven by price (inventory price effect -USD 6.2bn; receivables).
  • Q2 2026: +USD 3.4bn.
  • Net working capital was USD 18.8bn at Q2 2026 (6.4% of trailing revenue), against -USD 5.5bn at Q4 2025.
  • Underlying conversion. Q1 CFFO before working capital was USD 17.2bn against USD 6.9bn of adjusted earnings; Q2 CFFO excluding working capital was USD 17.2bn against USD 9.8bn.
  • Why this matters for the model: a counter-cyclical cash cushion.
  • Working capital absorbs cash as prices rise and releases it as they fall. On Shell's own cash-flow measure H1 2026 shows a net build of about USD 7.8bn (-11.2bn then +3.4bn). FactSet's balance-sheet net working capital rose from -USD 5.5bn (Q4 2025) to +USD 18.8bn (Q2 2026); its Q4 readings turn negative every year (also -USD 6.2bn at Q4 2024), so treat that series as directional only.
  • As prices normalise, much of it should come back. That supports distributions in exactly the year earnings fall, and it means 2027 free cash flow falls less than 2027 earnings.
  • Jefferies expected the Q1 outflow of "up to USD 15bn" to unwind upon normalisation.
  • For the model. Model working capital as a function of the price level, roughly symmetric. Deduct lease payments explicitly. Expect reported conversion above 1x in down-years and below 1x in up-years.
C-53

How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

D5014th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: margins are moderate and cyclical, and ratios on revenue mislead because revenue includes large traded volumes passed straight through (purchases of about USD 177bn). Consensus already takes margins back to 2025 levels by 2028. Per-boe and ROACE measures are better guides.

  • Gross margin (FactSet). 12.2% (2023), 13.3% (2024), 12.8% (2025); 19-20% in the 2026 quarters.
  • EBITDA margin.
  • Brokers' basis: 21.2% (2025), with consensus at 23.2% (2026), 22.9% (2027), 21.0% (2028) and 22.8% (2029, only 7 brokers).
  • FactSet operating basis: 15.2-17.0% over 2023-25.
  • EBIT margin. FactSet adjusted 7.3-8.3% over 2023-25. Consensus on the brokers' basis is 16% for 2026; the two bases are not comparable.
  • Peers, brokers' EBITDA margin for 2026. TotalEnergies 25%, Equinor 44%, Eni 21%, BP 23%.
  • The trend. 2026 is up; 2027-28 back down. Brokers do not expect the windfall to persist in margins beyond 2027.
  • For the model. Model segment earnings bottom-up on volumes times unit margins and the price deck, rather than as group margin on revenue. The margin on revenue should come out about 21-23% (brokers' EBITDA basis) at mid-cycle.
C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

D4611th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: the earnings base is moderately clean on a cash basis but systematically flattered on an economic basis.

  • Adjusted versus reported gaps are frequent and large.
  • Q1 2026: identified items -USD 2.4bn.
  • Q2 2026: reported net income USD 10.82bn against USD 9.84bn adjusted, helped by +USD 0.4bn of identified items (derivative fair-value gains) despite USD 665m of impairments.
  • H1 2026: a USD 648m drag from commodity-derivative fair-value accounting.
  • Recurring impairments excluded from adjusted earnings.
  • USD 8.3bn (2023), USD 4.4bn (2024), USD 3.7bn (2025), USD 0.67bn in Q2 2026.
  • The causes: Rotterdam biofuels (USD 1.4bn), renewables, chemicals.
  • They average roughly USD 3-5bn a year: real, recurring costs of past capital misallocation that adjusted earnings never show.
  • Complexity.
  • USD 9.5bn of FY2025 revenue came from fair-value accounting of commodity derivatives (Level 3 inputs).
  • Inventory write-downs USD 783m; receivable provisions USD 1.47bn; a USD 902m expected credit loss on trading loans.
  • Tax leakage.
  • Reported tax rates of 44.9%, 49.8% and 41.4% (2023-25), driven by the UK's 78% marginal rate and Brazilian levies (USD 242m in Q2 2026).
  • Deferred-tax gains from currency moves flatter earnings: Brazil USD 106m and Argentina USD 191m in H1 2026.
  • Basis differences. Viewforth-definition net income (USD 21.6bn in 2025) exceeds reported (USD 17.8bn).
  • Governance overlay. The auditor rotation breach (no restatement) and the PwC transition in FY2027.
  • For the model.
  • Forecast on Shell's adjusted (current cost of supplies, excluding identified items) basis, consistent with consensus.
  • Carry an "expected impairments" line of USD 1-3bn a year for reported EPS; that range is my assumption, falling as the clean-up ends.
  • Keep tax at 41-45% reported.
  • Treat derivative swings as noise with zero expected value.
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

C+725th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: financial leverage is low, comfortable and strategic. It barely amplifies EPS and greatly reduces risk.

  • The numbers.
  • Gearing 18.7% at Q2 2026 (23.2% in Q1; 20.7% at end-2025).
  • Net debt USD 41.75bn, of which about USD 29.7bn is lease liabilities, so net debt excluding leases is only about USD 12.1bn.
  • FactSet's trailing net debt to EBITDA is 0.66x.
  • Net interest about USD 3.7-3.9bn a year, including lease interest; a 1% rise in rates improves pre-tax income by USD 148m.
  • Ratings A+ (S&P) and AA- (Fitch); management targets AA metrics.
  • ARC adds about USD 2.5-2.8bn of net debt and leases.
  • What it does to EPS. Interest is about 7-10% of pre-tax profit at mid-cycle (my calculation), so financial leverage hardly changes the EPS profile.
  • What it does to risk. It underwrites the USD 3bn-a-quarter buyback down to USD 50 Brent and gives counter-cyclical capacity: buying back or acquiring in a downturn.
  • The tripwire. Jefferies notes that gearing above 25% would put sequential pressure on the buyback. Q1 2026 at peak working capital reached 23.2%.
  • Lease-heavy debt. Shipping and LNG vessels cannot be repaid quickly and are freight-sensitive (+USD 3bn in Q1 2026).
  • The trade-off. Shell chose equity, not debt, for ARC. That signals conservatism that protects the AA metrics at the cost of dilution.
  • For the model. Net debt falls toward about USD 32-38bn by end-2027 at mid-cycle prices (Morgan Stanley USD 31.9bn). Interest is broadly flat. The balance sheet is not an EPS lever.

Pricing power and operating leverage (and others)

5a

Pricing power

3 answers
C-1747C-4542P-0440
C-17To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management?D4711th of 19full answer under 2b Internal corporate change forces
C-45To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?D4211th of 19full answer under 4a Business quality
P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

F4013th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: price will be the largest single contributor to Shell's revenue and margin over the horizon, and it contributes negatively after 2026: about +39% of revenue in FY2026, then about -17% in FY2027 and -9% in FY2028. On profit, price (including the trading rent) added about USD 19bn of net income from FY2025 to FY2026 and gives back about USD 10bn in FY2027 and about USD 6.5bn more by FY2028. Shell's own pricing power softens the timing, not the direction: the three-to-six-month lag on oil-indexed LNG holds Integrated Gas up into H1 2027; Marketing and lubricants keep a real premium; trading earns a volatility rent that decays. From 2028 the LNG buyer's market starts to erode contract slopes on renewals.

How each price line behaves, and what I assume.

  1. Crude (Upstream; part of Integrated Gas). Pure benchmark. Brent about USD 95 in FY2026, 78 in FY2027 (quarterly path 88, 80, 74, 72), 72 in FY2028, 70 thereafter. That is a gradual Hormuz reopening through H1 2027, close to Morgan Stanley (USD 81.2 for 2027) and Redburn (75 base, 70 long term).
  2. Oil-indexed LNG (about 80% of LNG volumes contracted to 2029, average tenor about 15 years, slopes about 13-14% of Brent, lag of three to six months). The lag moves FY2027 Integrated Gas earnings up by about USD 1.0-1.3bn against an unlagged calculation, because Q1 2027 LNG prices reflect Q3-Q4 2026 Brent of about USD 100-105. From 2028 the lag is neutral.
  3. LNG contract slopes and renewals. History says buyers renegotiate when spot falls below contract: Petronet cut its RasGas price from USD 14 to USD 8-9/mmbtu in 2015; Chinese buyers pushed in 2015; the Brent-JKM slope fell to about 10% against a legacy 13.5% in 2020. Reviews come every four to five years (Hogan Lovells, 14-Oct-26). The 2027-30 supply wave (about 200 mtpa over two to three years, Systematix; JKM at USD 8-12 by 2029-30, Huatai) and QatarEnergy's Henry Hub-plus-fee SPA with Cheniere (October 2026) point to lower slopes on new and reviewed volumes. I assume realised slopes drift down about 0.3-0.5 points by 2029-30 on the volumes that roll or are reviewed, worth about USD 0.2-0.4bn of earnings a year. Shell has so far "prolonged legacy contracts without structural margin deterioration", which is why I do not assume more.
  4. Spot gas and the uncontracted 20%. TTF about USD 17 (2026), 12, 10, 9, 8.5. European storage entered winter at a record-low 74% (Morgan Stanley, 27-Oct-26), so Q4 2026 and Q1 2027 stay firm.
  5. Refining. Shell's capture rose to 55% in H1 2026 from 50% in FY2025 (BP 64%, TotalEnergies 35%), helped by about 100% utilisation. I assume capture returns to about 50% as margins normalise and the indicative margin averages about USD 28 (2026), 16.5, 12, 10.5 and 10.
  6. Chemicals. No pricing power: Chinese integrated producers price ethylene at USD 1,330/t against USD 2,060/t for Western standalone crackers (Jefferies, 4-Oct-26). The European chemicals PMI is 49.8. I assume chemicals margins stay near the cyclical low until the US exit.
  7. Marketing and lubricants: genuine but small pricing power. Premium fuels are 13.5% of volume and 26% of gross mobility margin; lubricants are under 4% of volume with about USD 70-84/bbl of EBITDA against USD 6.44 for other fuels (Bernstein); Shell passes additive cost increases on (expert call, 16-Feb-26). In Q2-Q3 2026 the Pearl base-oil outage squeezed lubricant margins; Train 2 repairs run to Q1 2027. I hold Marketing at about USD 5.2bn (2026) rising to about USD 5.4-5.5bn, helped by retail margins that widen as crude falls.
  8. Trading and optimisation: a volatility rent. The normal baseline (2-4 points of ROACE, about USD 7bn of net operating profit a year, Bernstein and Morgan Stanley) is in every year of my forecast. The exceptional layer of 2026, which I put at USD 3-5bn for the year, fades by half in 2027 and is gone by 2028. The decay is likely because the 2027-30 LNG wave adds liquidity and entrants (Indian and other national oil companies building desks and fleets; traders) while volatility falls. TotalEnergies' USD 800m sequential gas-trading swing in Q2 2026 shows the rent can turn negative in a falling market.
  9. Windfall taxes cap the upside, not the base. The UK Energy Profits Levy (78% marginal rate to March 2030) now sits inside the equity-accounted Adura venture; Brazil's 12% crude export levy (USD 242m in Q2 2026) is suspended by a reversible court ruling. In my base the Brazil levy lapses in 2027. In the prolonged-disruption scenario I deduct about USD 2bn a year for new European or UK levies.

What it means for revenue and margin.

  • Revenue: USD 357.4bn (+38.9%) in FY2026; 296.0bn (-17.2%); 268.5bn (-9.3%); 262.0bn (-2.4%); 262.5bn (+0.2%). Revenue moves with product prices at an elasticity of about 0.7 (FY2025 to FY2026: product prices up about 58%, revenue up about 39%).
  • Consensus revenue is stale. FY2027 consensus (USD 323.6bn, -8.0%) implies product prices that do not match its own EBITDA, which I match. Do not read the gap as an earnings view.
  • Margin: price is behind the EBITDA path in P-01. The pricing-power lines (LNG lag, Marketing, trading baseline) are what keep FY2028-30 EBITDA at about USD 65-67bn rather than about USD 60bn.

Skew. From today's spot the price skew is down for 2027 (Hormuz reopening, the OPEC+ supply response with spare capacity at about 0.9% of demand, and demand destruction: global LNG demand fell 5.4% in H1 2026). It is mixed for 2028-30: the LNG supply wave against Asian data-centre demand (about 20 mtpa of additional LNG imports by 2030, Morgan Stanley).

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7544P-0142
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

D4413th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Shell's leverage is to price rather than to fixed costs, and it is large. Operating costs (about USD 35.7bn) and depreciation (about USD 24bn) are largely fixed against volumes, so changes in realised prices fall almost straight to pre-tax profit. The result is a very wide range of earnings outcomes, which windfall taxes truncate on the upside and working capital and production-sharing contracts cushion on the downside.

  • Published sensitivities (annual).
  • Brent USD 10/bbl: about USD 3.7bn of CFFO (Upstream USD 2.6bn, Integrated Gas USD 1.1bn). Earnings move about USD 370m per USD 1 (Bernstein); ING uses USD 600m of cash flow per USD 1.
  • TTF/JKM USD 1/mmbtu: USD 500m of CFFO.
  • Henry Hub USD 1/mmbtu: -USD 500m. Shell is net short US gas through its US LNG offtake; ARC now adds a long position in Canadian AECO gas, which partly offsets it.
  • Refining margin USD 1/bbl: USD 320m (Annual Report) or USD 400m (Bernstein).
  • Chemicals margin USD 30/tonne: USD 575m.
  • Explaining the 2026 gap with those sensitivities. Moving from a mid-cycle deck (USD 75 Brent, USD 10 refining, TTF about USD 10) to a 2026-like deck (about USD 95 Brent, USD 25-30 refining, TTF USD 20+) adds:
  • about USD 7bn from Brent;
  • about USD 5-6bn from refining;
  • several billion from gas and trading.
  • That explains most of the gap between USD 24-26bn of normalised and USD 36bn of FY2026 consensus adjusted earnings. The calculation is mine.
  • The downside. At USD 60 Brent and USD 8 refining, adjusted earnings would be about USD 18-21bn (my estimate). 2025 delivered USD 18.5bn at about USD 69 Brent with weak refining and chemicals.
  • The cone of outcomes.
  • Individual broker FY2027 EPS ranges from USD 3.95 (ING) to USD 7.71 (Barclays).
  • FY2028 ranges from USD 3.27 to USD 6.44.
  • That is a range of about 2x driven by price decks.
  • For the model. Run every forecast year on at least three decks. The fixed cost base means the recurring cost savings (about USD 5.8bn) lowered breakevens permanently; the payout Brent floor is about USD 50 for the buyback.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

F4215th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Shell's operating leverage is leverage to price on a semi-fixed cost base, not leverage to volume, and over the next four years it works in reverse. Adjusted EBITDA falls from about USD 85bn (FY2026) to about USD 74bn (FY2027) and USD 67bn (FY2028), then holds near USD 65bn at a flat USD 70 Brent. That mid-cycle level matches FY2024 (USD 64.4bn) at a Brent about USD 8 lower, which is the true structural gain of the last four years: about USD 6-8bn of EBITDA from costs, ARC, LNG Canada and exits. The nominal cost base will not fall; savings and inflation roughly cancel.

What the leverage actually is.

  • Costs are fixed against volume and price. Operating expenses were USD 35.7bn in 2025 (Marketing 10.6, Upstream 9.0, Chemicals & Products 8.4, Integrated Gas 4.2, Renewables & Energy Solutions 2.6) and D&A about USD 24bn. Neither moves with Brent, so price changes fall almost straight to profit.
  • The price leverage (post-tax, a year): about USD 3.3-3.7bn per USD 10 of Brent (Bernstein USD 370m per USD 1); USD 320-400m per USD 1/bbl of refining margin; about USD 300m of earnings (USD 500m of cash flow) per USD 1/mmbtu of TTF or JKM; minus USD 500m of cash flow per USD 1 of Henry Hub; USD 575m per USD 30/t of chemicals margin.
  • The volume leverage is weak because the new barrels are low-margin. ARC adds about 370 kboe/d for about USD 1.5bn of free cash flow (about USD 11/boe) and, after the higher depreciation of acquired assets, about USD 1bn of earnings including synergies. The Qatar and Pearl restoration adds about USD 1.5-2bn (Pearl alone was about 6% of group cash flow, USD 2.0-2.5bn of free cash flow unconstrained). Total: about USD 2.6bn of earnings at full rate, so a 15-20% rise in production lifts mid-cycle earnings by only about 11%.
  • The cost leverage is spent. Structural savings reached USD 5.83bn against 2022, but "other opex changes, including inflation" added USD 1.1bn in H1 2026 alone. Opex in dollars will be flat to slightly up to 2030 (P-03).

Where the leverage is highest: refining and chemicals, carried by trading. Chemicals & Products has the heaviest fixed-cost base (USD 8.4bn of opex). Its adjusted earnings went from USD 1.93bn (Q1 2026) to USD 2.88bn (Q2) and, on my estimate, about USD 3.9bn in Q3 on the USD 42/bbl indicative margin. Trading and optimisation was 126% of Products adjusted earnings in 2025 (Annual Report 2025): the manufacturing assets lost money at mid-cycle and trading earned the segment's profit. That is the key structural point for the forecast: when trading and refining normalise together, this segment falls from about USD 11.5bn (FY2026) to about USD 3.7bn (FY2028). It is the single largest line in the decline.

Against the last four to six years.

  • The pattern repeats: a spike, then a normalisation of 40-50% in earnings. FactSet operating EBIT went USD 25.8bn (2021), 47.2bn (2022), 21.9bn (2023), 22.8bn (2024), 19.5bn (2025). Brokers'-basis EBITDA went 69.4bn (2023), 64.4bn (2024), 56.6bn (2025). My path, 85.4 to 74.0 to 66.9bn, is the 2022-24 shape again.
  • The floor is higher. In 2020 at about USD 42 Brent Shell lost money on FactSet's operating EBIT (USD -9.1bn). Today, after USD 5.8bn of gross savings and the exits of loss-making assets, I estimate adjusted earnings of about USD 18-20bn at USD 60 Brent and USD 8 refining.
  • D&A is rising from about USD 24bn to USD 26bn by 2030 (ARC's stepped-up assets, the Whale and Mero ramps). EBIT leverage is therefore slightly worse than EBITDA leverage: a mid-cycle EBIT of about USD 38-41bn against USD 41bn in FY2028 consensus.

The two natural hedges inside the portfolio narrow the cone but do not make it narrow.

  1. Hormuz price-volume hedge. If the Strait reopens, Brent and refining margins fall (about USD 8-10bn of earnings lost) while about 185 kboe/d of Qatari volumes return (about USD 1.5-2bn gained). If it stays shut, the reverse. Shell is long the disruption by about four to five times its volume exposure to it.
  2. Product margins lag crude down. Bernstein (26-Oct-26) finds crack spreads fell more slowly than crude in three past price collapses, and Morgan Stanley (20-Oct-26) sees planned refinery outages rising 3.4 mb/d from Q4 2026 to Q2 2027 while forward margins for late 2027 trade at about three times their historical average. This is why I hold refining at USD 16.5/bbl for 2027 rather than snapping it to USD 10.

Peers. TotalEnergies has the same shape with lower unit cost (under USD 5/boe against Shell's USD 8.54) and smaller refining leverage (35% capture). Equinor carries more spot-gas leverage (USD 120m of free cash flow per USD 1 of Brent against Shell's USD 400m, Redburn, but far more per USD of TTF). BP has more refining leverage (64% capture, USD 450m per USD 1). Shell's distinctive exposure is that its refining-plus-trading layer, not upstream, is the most leveraged part.

For the model. EBITDA margin about 23.9% (FY2026), then 25.0%, 24.9%, 24.8%, 24.6% on falling revenue. The margin rises as revenue falls because revenue is mainly a pass-through of product prices; read the EBITDA in dollars, not the margin.

5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7672P-0264
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?C+725th of 19full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

B+643rd of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: financial leverage is low, falling and almost irrelevant to EPS: net interest of USD 3.1-3.8bn a year is 7-9% of pre-tax profit, and each USD 10bn of net debt is worth only about USD 0.06 a share. The balance sheet matters for two other reasons. It guarantees the buyback through the price downturn, and on my base case Shell will hold USD 10-15bn of unused balance-sheet capacity by end-2028. How that capacity is used, buybacks, faster dividends or acquisitions, is the largest controllable swing in per-share outcomes after 2027.

The numbers.

  • Net debt USD 41.75bn at Q2 2026 including about USD 29.7bn of leases; net debt excluding leases about USD 12bn; gearing 18.7%; FactSet net debt to trailing EBITDA 0.66x; ratings A+ (S&P) and AA- (Fitch).
  • H2 2026: ARC adds about USD 2.6bn of assumed net debt and USD 3.3bn of cash consideration; H2 free cash flow after leases of about USD 19bn covers that, the dividend and USD 6.9bn of buybacks. Year-end 2026 net debt about USD 40bn.
  • 2027: USD 33.4bn of free cash flow after leases (including about USD 6.7bn of working-capital release) less USD 9.0bn of dividends and USD 14.5bn of buybacks, plus about USD 1.5bn of net disposals, cuts net debt to about USD 31bn (Morgan Stanley USD 31.9bn). Gearing falls to about 14%.
  • 2028: the sale of the US chemicals business (bids from ExxonMobil and LyondellBasell reported in August 2026; "up to USD 8bn" in press reports; I assume about USD 5.5bn closing in early 2028) takes net debt to about USD 26bn, of which nearly all is leases. Net debt excluding leases would be close to zero.
  • 2029-30: I let net debt drift back up to about USD 30-33bn through USD 13.5bn a year of buybacks and about USD 2bn a year of net bolt-on acquisitions, which keeps gearing near 13-14%.

Why EPS barely notices. A 1% rise in rates improves pre-tax income by USD 148m (Shell's cash exceeds its floating debt). Lowering net debt from USD 40bn to USD 26bn saves about USD 0.6bn of interest a year pre-tax, about USD 0.4bn post-tax, or USD 0.08 a share. My net financial items run USD -3.8bn (FY2026), -3.5bn, -3.2bn, -3.1bn, -3.1bn. About USD 1.4-1.5bn of that is lease interest, which does not fall with deleveraging.

What the balance sheet does instead.

  1. It underwrites the buyback through the downturn. Management says the buyback of at least USD 3bn a quarter holds down to USD 50 Brent; Morgan Stanley puts the dividend breakeven at about USD 40. Only Shell, TotalEnergies and Eni cover their dividend organically at USD 50 Brent (Redburn, 6-Jul-26). Jefferies' tripwire, gearing above 25%, would need Brent near USD 50 for a year or a USD 15bn acquisition paid in cash.
  2. It builds optionality. "Debt repaid in a windfall does not come back" (Industry Digest). By end-2028 my base leaves Shell at about 11-12% gearing against a 15-25% comfort range. That is USD 10-15bn of capacity. Spent on buybacks it would add about 1.5-2% a year to per-share accretion for two years; spent on resource acquisitions it would dilute per-share growth, as ARC did.
  3. The leases are the rigid part. About USD 29.7bn of mostly shipping and LNG-vessel leases, whose variable components move with freight (USD 3.9bn added by index-linked remeasurement in Q1 2026). Gearing can rise with no cash spent; lease payments of about USD 5.2-5.5bn a year are a fixed cash call that FactSet free cash flow ignores.

Which way management will lean. The evidence points to caution. ARC was paid 75% in shares (228m) at 19% gearing, which ING called puzzling; Gorman describes banked cash as "flexibility to accelerate buybacks during cycle downturns". So I expect the capacity to be used counter-cyclically: higher buybacks if the share price falls in 2027-28, and bolt-on acquisitions to address the 200-350 kb/d post-2030 liquids gap. I do not expect leverage to be used to lift EPS.

Peers. TotalEnergies is heading below 10% gearing by end-2026; Eni is at 10%; BP reached its USD 14-18bn net debt target a year early but paused buybacks; Equinor's net debt to capital employed is 10.4%. Shell is not the least levered, but it is the one whose leverage most clearly funds per-share returns.

For the model. Net financial items as above; no change in share count from leverage; buybacks at USD 13.5-14.5bn a year (P-08). Model gearing as a constraint (15-25%), not as a driver.

Cost-out scope NTM
