C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

C557th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: the large, safe cost cuts are mostly done. The remaining cuttable scope is about USD 1-2bn by 2028, roughly 4-7% of normalised earnings (my estimate), and it comes mainly from exits (chemicals, loss-making renewables activities) rather than further efficiency. Cuts much beyond that would start to damage reliability, project delivery and the reserve base.

  • Delivered.
  • USD 5.83bn of structural reductions against 2022 by mid-2026 (USD 5.1bn at end-2025; USD 0.7bn in H1 2026).
  • Composition: 57% is non-portfolio (operations, maintenance and the corporate centre, USD 3.3bn; of which operations, maintenance and supply chain USD 1.5bn); more than 60% comes from "corporate simplification and faster decisions".
  • The Risks report says "nearly half" came from divestments. The true efficiency share is therefore about USD 3-3.3bn.
  • Remaining by line. 1. Corporate centre and administration. Largely done: the Executive Committee went from 9 to 8, layers removed, USD 700m in 2026 from "a leaner corporate centre". 2. Operations and maintenance. Risky. Q2 2026 deferred non-essential maintenance, and talent loss (turnover 11%) argues for no further cuts. 3. Chemicals. The largest remaining prize, through the US sale and European closures (about 15% of capacity). It removes losses, so it is cost removal by exit. 4. Renewables & Energy Solutions. Most activities are loss-making (Q2 2026), so they can be cut further. 5. Capex. Already cut to USD 20-22bn from USD 22-25bn. Further cuts would accelerate decline. 6. Digital and AI. Incremental opex savings and reliability gains of up to 2%.
  • Offsets. Inflation (BP saw about USD 3bn of gross savings offset) and rising carbon costs (about USD 0.3bn a year).
  • For the model. Cumulative structural savings of about USD 6.5-7bn by 2028. Net of inflation, the effect on adjusted earnings is about USD 0.5-1.0bn above the 2026 run-rate by 2028. Do not double-count portfolio-related savings with divestment dilution.
P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

D4711th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: very little of the remaining cost programme will reach the profit and loss account. Shell has USD 5.83bn of the USD 5-7bn target in hand, about 43% of it from divestments. The last USD 0.9-1.2bn of gross savings to 2028 is roughly matched by inflation and rising carbon costs, so the net benefit to adjusted earnings is about USD 0.3-0.6bn post-tax by 2028 against the 2026 run-rate, most of it in H2 2026 and 2027. The larger "cost" gain is exit, not efficiency: removing chemicals losses adds about USD 0.3-0.5bn by 2028-29. Further cuts beyond the target would start to consume reliability.

What has been delivered, and what part is real margin.

  • USD 5.82-5.83bn of cumulative pre-tax structural reductions against 2022 by Q2 2026 (USD 5.1bn at end-2025; USD 0.7bn in H1 2026).
  • Composition (Q2 2026 presentation): USD 3.3bn (57%) non-portfolio efficiency, of which USD 1.5bn maintenance and supply chain and USD 1.2bn leaner corporate systems; USD 2.5bn (43%) from portfolio high-grading and asset sales (Singapore, Aera). The 43% removed revenue and earnings with the cost, so it is not a margin gain.
  • Offsets: "other opex changes, including inflation and activity" added USD 711m over 2022-25 and USD 1,117m in H1 2026 alone. On that evidence inflation is now outrunning savings in nominal opex.

What is left, line by line, and when it lands.

  1. Corporate centre. USD 0.7bn delivered in 2026 from "a leaner corporate centre"; the Executive Committee went from nine to eight. Perhaps USD 0.2-0.3bn more, in 2027.
  2. Operations and maintenance. I assume nothing more. Q2 2026 deferred non-essential maintenance; Tier 1 and 2 process-safety events rose from 63 (2023) to 90 (2024); voluntary turnover was 11.2% in 2025 against 7.6%; headcount fell from 96,000 to 85,000. Midstream availability fell in 2025.
  3. Chemicals. The largest remaining prize, and it is an exit. The Q3 2026 indicative chemicals margin of USD 208/t (USD 270/t in Q2) puts the business back into losses (Jefferies). The US sale and the European closures remove those losses: about USD 0.3-0.5bn of earnings by 2028-29, timed by the sale.
  4. Renewables & Energy Solutions. Most activities lose money; further shrinkage adds perhaps USD 0.1-0.2bn, with impairments outside adjusted earnings.
  5. Digital and AI. Bernstein cites 20% less downtime and 15-25% lower maintenance spend industry-wide; Shell cites up to 2% LNG reliability. I treat this as the source that keeps the target on track, not as upside to it.

Against what is cuttable at peers. Chevron delivered USD 3bn six months early with more than 70% from efficiency; ExxonMobil USD 16.3bn since 2019, on its way to USD 20bn by 2030; BP USD 6.5-7.5bn by end-2027 with documented inflation offsets (about USD 3bn) and a plan to cut absolute costs from USD 22bn to USD 18bn. TotalEnergies cut technical costs from USD 28.3/bbl to USD 23.0 in 2015. Shell's programme is fast, but it is half-portfolio, and BP and ExxonMobil both show the industry pattern: gross savings of this size absorb inflation rather than lift margins.

The capability question. The evidence of damage is suggestive but not conclusive: rising process-safety events, the deferred maintenance, the turnover and "triple-hatting" reported by experts. Against it: the 2024 Pearl Train 2 turnaround (16,000 workers, on schedule, no significant incidents), record 102% refinery utilisation and record Brazilian output in Q2 2026, and projects delivered at 99.8% of budget in 2025. Precedents (BP before Macondo; the Norwegian regulator's 2020 notice to Equinor that efficiency programmes were squeezing safety time) show the cost arrives as an incident, not as a trend. I therefore add no savings beyond the target and keep an unplanned-downtime allowance of about USD 0.2-0.3bn a year in 2027-28.

When and how much reaches the line items (pre-tax, against the 2026 run-rate).

Item FY2027 FY2028 FY2029-30
Remaining structural savings (gross) +0.6 +1.0 +1.1
Inflation and activity (net of AI benefits) -0.4 -0.6 -0.8 a year further
Carbon compliance (about USD 1bn in 2026, +0.3bn a year) -0.3 -0.6 -0.9 to -1.2
Chemicals exit (losses removed) +0.2 +0.5 +0.6
Net, pre-tax +0.1 +0.3 about -0.1 to -0.3

So net cost-and-exit benefit is close to zero by 2029-30; the line items will not show a falling cost base. Any new upside needs a new target, most likely at a Capital Markets Day in 2027, and I do not model one.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4758P-0550
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?C-589th of 19full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

D508th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: five loops will shape the line items. One balancing loop dominates the profit and loss account: high prices lead to demand destruction, supply response and windfall taxes, which bring lower prices. Two counter-cyclical loops dominate the cash flow and per-share lines: working capital releases as prices fall, and buybacks retire more shares as the share price falls. The result is that EPS falls less than net income, and free cash flow less than EPS, in 2027-28. From 2029 two slower loops take over: the LNG liquidity loop, which erodes the trading rent, and the depletion loop, in which a short reserve life forces dearer acquisitions that dilute buyback accretion.

  1. High price, then demand destruction, supply response and taxes (balancing; dominant in 2027-28). - How it works: prices above USD 100 cut demand; buyers switch fuel; governments tax windfalls; OPEC+ and stranded Gulf barrels return after a settlement; prices fall. - Evidence now: global LNG demand -5.4% in H1 2026 (China -10%, Japan -7%); gas-to-coal switching in Asia; jet-demand destruction; six EU states lobbying for a permanent windfall tax; non-OPEC supply growth peaking by early 2027. - Effect on the line items: the deck path in P-04. It removes about USD 15bn of net income from FY2026 to FY2028.
  2. Working capital (balancing; cash only; 2027). - How it works: rising prices lock cash in inventories and receivables, and falling prices release it. Q1 2026 -USD 11.2bn (inventory price effect -USD 6.2bn); Q2 +USD 3.4bn. Chevron (+USD 2.9bn) and TotalEnergies (+USD 1.2bn) released working capital in Q2 too; BP built USD 1.0bn. - My inputs: a FY2026 build of about USD 8.4bn, a FY2027 release of about USD 6.7bn and a FY2028 release of about USD 2.1bn. - Limits: the loop is a timing bridge. It reverses when prices recover, and in 2020 it did not prevent Shell's two-thirds dividend cut. It cushions the first year of a downturn, not a prolonged one.
  3. Buybacks at a falling share price (reinforcing per-share accretion; stabilises EPS). - How it works: if the share price falls with earnings, a fixed dollar buyback retires more shares. At USD 54 a USD 14bn buyback retires about 4.6% of shares; at USD 42 about 5.9%. - Evidence: management bought 25% of the shares over three years at about 20% below the price of February 2026; the loop is now weaker at a 99th-percentile multiple. - Effect: in my base (USD 52-53) the share count falls about 5% a year; in the fast-reopening scenario (share price about USD 42) about 6%. That partly offsets the scenario's lower earnings, so EPS outcomes are less dispersed than net income outcomes.
  4. Windfall cash repays debt (reinforcing, slowly). Net debt falls from about USD 45bn (end-2025) to about USD 26bn (end-2028) in my base. Interest falls about USD 0.7bn, and the capacity built supports distributions in the next downturn (P-02).
  5. Volatility, trading profit, then entrants and liquidity (balancing; 2028-30). 2026 volatility produced record trading results at Shell and BP. The response, more portfolio players and national oil company desks offtaking LNG without downstream contracts (Jefferies, 10-Jun-26), plus 2027-30 liquidity, compresses arbitrage. Vitol's net earnings fell from USD 15.1bn (2022) to 8.7bn (2024); Trafigura's profit fell by two-thirds in a half-year as markets calmed. Effect: the exceptional trading layer goes to zero by 2028, and I hold the baseline flat rather than growing it with LNG volumes.
  6. Depletion, acquisitions, equity issuance, slower accretion (reinforcing negative; mostly beyond 2028). Proved reserve life is 7.4-7.8 years against 12.2 at TotalEnergies; the liquids gap after 2030 is 200-350 kb/d; conventional discoveries are below 5bn boe a year, and the resource race raises acquisition premiums (about 20% for ARC). ARC issued 228m shares, reversing about one year of buybacks; Redburn puts its free cash flow per share accretion at 7.5% on average over 2027-29. I include USD 2bn a year of net acquisitions in 2029-30; a second ARC-sized share issue would cut about 4% from per-share figures.
  7. Capital discipline, service scarcity, inflation (balancing; capex). Flat industry budgets keep rigs, subsea and turbines tight (subsea awards about USD 30bn in 2027; turbines expected at USD 1.3-1.4m per MW for 2028 deliveries). The fixed USD 20-22bn envelope buys 5-10% less activity a year, which feeds loop 6.
  8. Cash-tax timing (minor at Shell). Cash tax paid lags the charge in a profit surge; Shell's H1 2026 cash flow before working capital (USD 34.4bn) ran about USD 6bn ahead of adjusted earnings plus D&A. I reverse about USD 1-3bn of that in 2027. Equinor's version of this loop (ten tax instalments; a 2015 cash flow of NOK -18bn from lagged taxes) is far larger.

Which loops dominate which line. EBITDA and net income: loop 1 in 2027-28, loops 5 and 7 after. Free cash flow: loops 2 and 8 in 2027 (so FCF falls about 5% against about 23% for net income). EPS: loop 3 throughout, loop 6 as a tail risk. Net: the per-share and cash lines are much more stable than the profit lines, the opposite of the market's habit of capitalising profit.

Current guidance and SS management, simple case and no SHMLP risks (and comparable track record)

6a

Guidance conservatism

B635th of 193 answers

Group judgement

Guidance

B635th of 19

Layer 1 · Guidance

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement: Shell's guidance is specific, framework-based and conservative.

  • It comes from a management that under-promises on controllables, raises targets only at CMDs and reports windfalls without upgrading frameworks.
  • Formal guidance is in line with or slightly below consensus where comparable (capex, DPS). Informal commentary points to a strong H2 2026 but does not endorse a higher price deck for 2027.
  • The capital-return frameworks are set for a USD 60-70 world, so they are a floor in today's environment.

For the Consumer:

  • Model capex at the guided bands (ceiling).
  • Model distributions at no less than 40% of modelled CFFO (floor), with a step-up option.
  • Model dividend growth of 4-5% (base) with upside.
  • Use the quarterly outlooks and sensitivities for segment builds.
  • Treat trading, the Q1 2027 Pearl restart and the Qatar restoration as the main points where guidance is vague and the forecast risk sits.
C-68

To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

C586th of 19

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: Shell does not guide earnings, so there is no direct earnings comparison. On the items it does guide (capex, distribution policy, dividend growth, volumes, costs), guidance is in line with or slightly below consensus. The gap that matters is between consensus and the price deck, not between guidance and consensus. Shell guides frameworks; the sell-side forecasts outcomes.

Capex.

  • FY2026 guidance is USD 24-26bn of cash capex, including about USD 4bn related to ARC (much of it the USD 3.3bn cash consideration, since Shell's cash capex includes acquisitions). FactSet consensus is USD 20.9bn on a fixed-asset basis. The two are consistent.
  • H1 2026 actual was USD 7.8bn on FactSet's basis (Q1 USD 4.2bn and Q2 USD 4.2bn on Shell's), which points to the low end of the range excluding ARC.
  • FY2027 guidance is USD 20-22bn against consensus of USD 21.0bn: in line.

Distributions.

  • Policy is 40-50% of CFFO and a progressive dividend of about 4% a year.
  • Consensus DPS is USD 1.58 (FY2026), USD 1.64 (FY2027, +4%) and USD 1.71 (FY2028): policy-consistent.
  • Brokers assume buybacks of about USD 3bn+ a quarter.

Volumes.

  • The 4% CAGR for 2025-30 includes ARC.
  • Quarterly ranges are given at each update note. The Q3 2026 note put the refining margin at USD 42/bbl against about USD 32 expected, effectively informal guidance above consensus for Q3.

Costs. USD 5-7bn by 2028: consensus presumably embeds it.

Why the gap with the deck matters.

  • FY2026 consensus adjusted net income of USD 36.1bn implies about USD 19.4bn in H2. Brent was about USD 100+ in Q3 and refining margins stood at USD 42, so H2 looks beatable.
  • FY2027 consensus of USD 29.5bn implies a deck still well above mid-cycle.
C-71

How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

C+665th of 19

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: current guidance is conservative against the last one to five years, and more conservative still once the input change forces are taken into account. Realised prices sit far above the levels the frameworks were set at.

  • Capex. USD 20-22bn for 2027-28 is unchanged even after adding ARC (about USD 1.3bn a year of ARC organic capex absorbed). Legacy capex is therefore effectively cut by about USD 1.3bn, which is conservative on spend. It also means less legacy reinvestment, a mild longevity cost.
  • Costs. USD 5.83bn achieved against a target of USD 5-7bn.
  • Production. A 4% CAGR is mechanically supported by ARC; organic 1% is a low bar.
  • FCF per share above 10% a year to 2030. Easy from the low 2025 base (USD 69 Brent, chemicals losses). Harder from the 2026 windfall base, which is not the stated base.
  • Distributions.
  • A 40% floor on CFFO.
  • The buyback floor holds down to USD 50 Brent while Brent is above USD 100.
  • Dividend growth of 4% when DPS (USD 1.58) is only about 25% of FY2026 EPS (USD 6.37).
  • All very conservative.
  • Against the input forces. The Sector Digest's rule applies: when realised prices sit far above planning prices, consensus is a floor, not a ceiling. Shell's frameworks were set for a USD 60-70 world.
  • Against 2021-25. The 2023-25 period delivered within or above every framework.
  • For the model. Take guidance as a floor for capital returns and a ceiling for capex.
6b

Judgement: the sell-side has correctly framed Shell's earnings as a windfall year followed by normalisation.

  • By year. FY2026 consensus is likely still a floor; FY2027 is the most exposed year, embedding an elevated deck; FY2028 sits near a credible mid-cycle level.
  • Shell's record. It beats in volatile markets and never misses on controllables.
  • The bar. Upward revisions have been broad and steep for 2026-27, so the bar is high. The very wide dispersion between brokers shows the debate is about the price deck, not the company.

For the Consumer: anchor FY2028 on mid-cycle fundamentals (it matches consensus at about USD 25-26bn). Treat FY2027 as the key judgement year: model it explicitly on two or three decks rather than accepting a consensus that is a midpoint of polar views. Check consensus' implied tax rate and lease treatment.

C-72

How specific is the guidance: built from stated building blocks, or vague about how results come about?

A722nd of 19

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: guidance is highly specific and built from stated building blocks. Shell gives modellers more structure than most peers. The vagueness is concentrated in trading and renewables.

  • The building blocks.
  • Capex bands, including about USD 13bn a year for Upstream and Integrated Gas.
  • The distribution formula; dividend growth; the buyback floor.
  • The cost target, tracked by category (non-portfolio share; operations, maintenance and corporate centre).
  • Hurdle rates by segment.
  • Published sensitivities: Brent, gas, Henry Hub, refining and chemicals margins, interest rates.
  • Quarterly outlooks: Integrated Gas production and liquefaction volumes, Upstream production, refinery and chemicals utilisation, Marketing volumes, corporate costs.
  • Pre-close update notes with indicative margins.
  • The October 2025 "Financial Modelling Guidance".
  • The LNG portfolio outlook (a 4-5% sales CAGR).
  • The gaps.
  • Trading and optimisation contribution (only qualitative: "significantly higher").
  • Renewables margins.
  • The LNG realised-price formula (slopes are not disclosed).
  • ARC's standalone contribution after integration.
  • The tax rate by jurisdiction.
  • For the model. Build segment by segment from the outlooks and sensitivities, and treat trading as the residual explanatory line.
C-85

To what extent is informal/qualitative company commentary above or below formal guidance and sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

C+605th of 19

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: informal commentary sits slightly above formal guidance on near-term operations and on flexibility in distributions, and below it (cautious) on the commodity outlook. Against consensus it is broadly aligned for FY2026 and gives no support to FY2027 estimates rising further on price.

  • Above formal guidance.
  • The Q3 update note: a USD 42 refining margin and strong trading.
  • Sawan's "very strong operational delivery" and confidence in maintaining refinery performance.
  • "We are not at the full potential of this company."
  • Gorman: 40-50% is "sacrosanct", and holding cash gives "flexibility to accelerate buybacks during downturns".
  • Expediting Namibian appraisal.
  • Integrating ARC "ahead of plan".
  • Jefferies hears a rebalancing toward dividends.
  • Below or cautious.
  • Management presents "a highly cautious outlook on commodity prices".
  • Q3 lubricant headwinds from the Pearl outage.
  • Chemical spreads "softening" as new capacity arrives.
  • Pearl Train 2 offline until Q1 2027.
  • A refusal to "predict volatile market directions".
  • Why. Management manages expectations on what it cannot control and talks up what it can. The tone supports a beat in Q3 and Q4 2026 and gives no comfort that FY2027 consensus (+21% in three months) is conservative.
C-73

What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

A601st of 19

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: several features of Shell's guidance and investor relations are material for modelling and uncommon.

  1. Pre-close quarterly "update notes" about three weeks before results, with indicative margins and segment ranges. They reduce surprises; most beats in 2026 were partly flagged.
  2. "Cash capex" includes acquisitions, so 2026 guidance includes ARC's cash consideration. Do not read USD 24-26bn as organic spend.
  3. Net debt includes about USD 29.7bn of leases whose variable components move with freight rates (+USD 3bn in Q1 2026). Reported gearing can rise without any cash spent.
  4. Adjusted earnings are on a current-cost-of-supplies basis excluding identified items. Large identified items (derivatives, impairments) make reported earnings noisy.
  5. The auditor transition to PwC from FY2027, after the EY rotation breach.
  6. Segment restatements. Q1 2026 segment figures differ between the two earnings reviews (Integrated Gas USD 2.115bn against USD 1.819bn), so check against the latest tables.
  7. The incentive metric "normalised FCF per share" (2026 policy). Management may start to communicate in that metric.
  8. The "sacrosanct" language on distributions: unusually strong commitment wording, which raises the cost of any future cut.
  9. The listings. London plus a US ADR. Morgan Stanley's compilation of ADR targets gives a consensus mean of USD 95.63, a high of USD 120.60 and a low of USD 47.30, so compare targets in the correct line.
  10. Next results on 29-Oct-26 (Q3 2026), the day after the UK budget of 28-Oct-26, which could affect the levy.

To what extent have sell-side estimates been cut deeply and fully across all analysts, and has company guidance recently been rebased lower, or more aggressively kitchen-sinked or sandbagged?

D4214th of 19

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: estimates have not been cut. They have been raised steeply and broadly for FY2026-27, while FY2028 is essentially unchanged. There has been no kitchen-sinking or rebasing of guidance; the only rebase was the modest buyback trim. The bar is now high, not low; the near-term "easy beat" setup is fading as estimates catch up with spot, though Q3 2026 may still beat.

  • FactSet mean EPS revisions (1 / 3 / 6 / 12 months).
  • FY2026: +12.2% / +23.5% / +29.4% / +92.9%.
  • FY2027: +15.0% / +21.0% / +17.5% / +40.4%.
  • FY2028: +1.6% / +3.5% / -1.5% / +2.9%.
  • FY2027 revisions on the dashboard's data.
  • EPS +11 / +21 / +18 / +39%; EBITDA +6 / +13 / +17 / +27%; sales +4 / +10 / +8 / +11%.
  • Target price +2 / +7 / +5%.
  • Buy share 39%: unchanged over three months, +9 points over six.
  • Revision-momentum score 13, in line with the peers (11-15).
  • Breadth. Most brokers revised up (Morgan Stanley FY2026 +11%, then +17.3% / +31.8% / +17.3% for FY2026-28; Jefferies +9% / +16%; Redburn +5% for FY2026 and -3% for FY2027).
  • The rebases.
  • The buyback cut from USD 3.5bn to USD 3.0bn a quarter (Q1 2026).
  • ING downgraded in December 2025 on lower buyback expectations; Redburn downgraded to Neutral in April 2026 on longevity.
  • Neither was a profit rebase.
  • Ratings and targets. Mean rating Overweight (1.59), median Hold. The mean target is USD 53.03 against a price of USD 53.98, so the stock trades at the consensus target. That is consistent with a pinned sell-side momentum B and valuation D.
  • What it means. Earnings momentum is strong but concentrated in the windfall years. Brokers refuse to capitalise it into FY2028, which supports the case that the market is not over-extrapolating, but also means there is no low bar for 2027.
6c

Case simplicity

7 answers
Judgement50C-7950D-0266D-0344D-0436D-0562D-0656
Group judgement

Risks

–50

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement: the predictability of Shell's financial forecasts over the next 18 months is bifurcated, and the memo should say so plainly. Everything below EBITDA and everything in cash is predictable to a degree unusual for a company of this size: capex, structural costs, lease payments, the dividend, the buyback floor, the share count and the ex-lease net debt are set by frameworks management has met every year since 2023, and the Q3 2026 print confirmed the second set's model to the decimal. EBITDA and EPS are not predictable within about plus or minus 25% for FY2027, because about 85% of the forecast change comes from four price lines that are all readings of one geopolitical state, the Strait of Hormuz, and the single most likely outcome (a gradual reopening) is also the one in which consensus has the furthest to travel. The earlier sets are congruent on all of this; the thesis asks almost nothing of Shell that it has not demonstrated; disclosure and the external transmission are good exactly where predictability already exists (volumes, capex, distributions, sensitivities) and poor where it does not (trading, tax, unit margins). The share price is three-quarters sector-driven, and the sector trades on the same variable as the earnings, so the multiple adds correlated risk rather than diversification. The investable conclusion for the memo: underwrite the cash and per-share lines and the resolution of the Hormuz state; do not underwrite an FY2027 EPS or a peak multiple.

The seven answers fit together as follows.

  • D-01 and D-02 establish that the forecast is internally coherent and undemanding of management: the earnings fall is a deck call, the offsets rest on demonstrated capabilities or on an external event, and only ARC's drilling synergies and the post-2030 liquids answer need capabilities Shell has not shown at scale.
  • D-03 and D-04 establish that the forecast is concentrated on one variable with a three-peaked cone of about USD 3.6-7.5 for FY2027 EPS, a probability-weighted mean (about USD 5.4) above the base and consensus, a positive skew in earnings and a negative skew in the share price, and a narrow cone for cash (free cash flow after leases of about USD 27-38bn in FY2027 in every state).
  • D-05 and D-06 establish that the case can be tracked (weekly transits, quarterly update notes, segment ranges, published sensitivities, a dated Capital Markets Update) and that the per-share mechanics can be audited exactly, while the EBITDA line will keep surprising by about 10% a quarter on trading and tax.
  • D-07 establishes that the price and the multiple move with the European majors (R-squared 71%, beta 0.83), that the multiple's elevation is a sector phenomenon, and that the model's guideline multiple is a bet on the sector's regime.

For the memo's topics this means: #9 (riskiness and simplicity) should be written as "simple and binary", not "complex"; #6 (forecast financials) should present FY2027 as three states with the cash lines common to all; #11 and #12 should treat the multiple as the largest single risk to the return, larger than the earnings risk, because the two are correlated.

C-79

How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

D5012th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: the perimeter is still wide (five reporting segments, upstream across many countries, global trading, global retail), but it is narrowing steadily.

  • How it has narrowed.
  • Refineries cut from 15 to 7.
  • Exits from Nigeria onshore, Singapore, Indian power, South African retail, Jiffy Lube, US offshore wind and European onshore renewables.
  • UK upstream deconsolidated into Adura.
  • Chemicals under review.
  • What still limits predictability. Earnings depend on at least six independently volatile prices (Brent, TTF/JKM, Henry Hub and AECO, refining margins, chemical margins, freight), plus trading results that cause roughly 10% earnings surprises (Redburn), plus Level 3 derivatives and working-capital swings.
  • Geographic concentration adds discontinuity. About 20% of production is in the Middle East, about 10% of it through Hormuz.
  • For the model. Simplification helps the cost base and capital allocation more than it helps the predictability of earnings. Quarterly forecasting error will stay high (about ±10%) even as the annual framework (capex, distributions) stays very predictable.
D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

–66

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the change the forecasts expect over the next 18 months rests almost entirely on strengths Shell has demonstrated, or on events outside its control; it rests very little on strengths Shell would need to acquire. The forecast earnings fall of about USD 13bn from FY2026 to FY2028 is a price-deck call that requires no capability. The USD 3-4bn of offsets (Middle East volumes, ARC, LNG Canada, costs, the chemicals exit) break down as follows: about half depends on the Strait of Hormuz reopening, which is external; about a third rests on demonstrated capabilities (project restarts, cost delivery, portfolio exits, the trading baseline); and the remainder, chiefly ARC's drilling synergies and the post-2030 volume answer, depends on a shale-manufacturing capability Shell has not demonstrated at scale. The one thing the thesis asks Shell to do that it has not done before is to run a large North American unconventional business well, and in the 18-month window that is worth about USD 1bn of earnings, not the case.

Component by component, tested against the record and the precedents.

  1. Middle East volume restoration (about USD 1.5-2bn of earnings at full rate; the second set assumes 70% restored on average in 2027). Shell's demonstrated part is the repair: Pearl Train 2 is on schedule for late Q1 2027, consistent with the 2017 precedent (a five-month unplanned shutdown, restored in July 2017, with lubricant supply maintained from inventory) and the 2024 planned turnaround (16,000 workers, 56 days, no significant incident). The external part is the export: Train 1 is restarted but filling inventory because product cannot leave; Shell's Qatargas 4 train is undamaged on the sources' description and QatarEnergy says undamaged trains restart within weeks of a reopening. So the restoration is plausible as soon as the Strait reopens and implausible before; the capability is demonstrated, the timing is not Shell's. Precedents for the timing are poor: Suez transits were still 50% below baseline three years after the 2023 Red Sea attacks (Jefferies), and insurance at 5% of hull value and ship-to-ship transfers at USD 1m a day for supertankers show that shipping normalises slowly even after a political settlement. The second set's "gradual" base case is the right shape; the risk is that 70% average restoration in 2027 is generous if a settlement arrives only mid-year.
  2. ARC integration and USD 250m of synergies by about September 2027 (about USD 1bn of earnings including synergies). Of the USD 250-260m, about USD 160m (pipeline tolls, trading optimisation, G&A) is mechanical and low-risk; Shell's trading division has demonstrated the premiumisation of molecules, and the toll savings come from contiguous acreage. The USD 100m of drilling and completion savings is the part that needs a capability Shell has not shown at scale: Shell exited the Permian in 2021 and Groundbirch is small; shale is a manufacturing business, not a mega-project business. ARC's own record is mixed: standalone opex of CAD 5.39/boe in 2025 (against Tourmaline's 4.48) rose to 6.98 in Q2 2026 on turnarounds, G&A doubled with transaction costs, and Attachie, the growth engine, underperformed badly enough for guidance to be withdrawn. The precedents are favourable for scale synergies (Tourmaline-Crew about 30% opex synergies; Canadian Natural's asset swap with Shell saved CAD 30m a year; Shell's own BG integration under Sawan's Brazil leadership) and unfavourable for pace (BG took years and USD 30bn of disposals; ExxonMobil-Pioneer and Chevron-Hess are described by experts as multi-year digestions). Redburn's arithmetic (7.5% average FCF-per-share accretion with synergies, 3.3% without) says the synergies are the accretion. Verdict: plausible for the mechanical USD 160m, unproven for the drilling USD 100m; the 18-month earnings effect of failure is about USD 0.1bn, small.
  3. Structural costs to the top of USD 5-7bn (the second set gives this almost no net earnings value). Demonstrated: USD 5.83bn delivered two and a half years early, with the CFO guiding to the top of the range. The caveat is demonstrated too: nominal opex is not falling (nine months USD 26.41bn against 26.12bn), BP's CFO reports USD 4bn of savings offset by USD 1.5bn of inflation since 2023, and an expert attributes much of Shell's delivery to divestments. Reliability has not deteriorated on the published measure (Tier 1 and 2 events 62 in 2025). The forecast asks nothing here that the record does not support.
  4. Buybacks of at least USD 3bn a quarter through falling prices. Demonstrated through 19 quarters including 2025 at USD 69 Brent and a 9.4% ROACE. The balance sheet is stronger than at any point in that record (ex-lease gearing 2.7%). Peers show the limits: Chevron cut its buyback run-rate 37% in 2025 when prices softened; BP suspended buybacks in February 2026 to deleverage; DNB Carnegie notes European majors need about USD 80 Brent to fund capex and distributions from CFFO, so below that Shell either leans on the balance sheet, as its policy allows, or trims. The floor is demonstrable at USD 60; the Q3 pace of USD 5bn is not, and the model does not assume it persists.
  5. Refining capture of 50% from 2027 (the second set assumes a fall from 55%). Shell's capture is structurally high (complex refineries, 96-102% utilisation, trading support) against TotalEnergies' 35% and Eni's 33%; Jefferies models 50% from 2027 and notes freight is already eroding realised margins. The assumption asks for less than the record, not more.
  6. The trading baseline of about USD 7bn a year, with the exceptional layer gone by 2028. The baseline is demonstrated across cycles (Bernstein: 2-4 points of ROACE uplift; BP reports a 4-point uplift for six consecutive years). The decay of the exceptional layer is the historical norm, not a risk: Vitol's net income fell from USD 15.1bn (2022) to 4.2bn (2025); Trafigura's from 7.4bn (FY2023) to 2.7bn; Eni's gas-portfolio EBIT from EUR 3.4bn (2023) to 1.05bn (2025); Shell's own Integrated Gas from USD 22.2bn (2022) to 7.1bn (2023). The forecast relies on a strength Shell has and assumes the part that depends on the environment goes away.
  7. The chemicals exit (about USD 0.3-0.5bn of losses removed by 2028-29; USD 5.5bn of proceeds assumed in early 2028). Exiting is demonstrated (Singapore in 2025, several hundred million dollars a year of earnings improvement). Selling a large US petrochemicals business at a non-distressed price is not demonstrated by anyone in this cycle: SABIC sold its European business at EUR 0.5bn (0.13x sales) with an SAR 11bn write-down; LyondellBasell paid to exit Europe; Eni's Versalis breakeven slipped to 2029. The one favourable US precedent is Dow's sale of 49% of its Gulf Coast infrastructure to Macquarie at about 10x EBITDA, which is the "capital-markets transaction" Sawan mentioned. Four bidders at up to USD 8bn make a deal plausible; a book loss (USD 24bn of chemicals assets) is near-certain and sits outside adjusted earnings. The adjusted-earnings benefit is small and plausible; the timing is the uncertainty.
  8. LNG sales growth of 4-5% a year. Supported by capacity Shell already owns or has contracted (LNG Canada Phase 1 at full rate, past 100 cargoes; Phase 2 sanctioned for the early 2030s; North Field East 6.25%, now delayed to late 2027 or 2028; North Field South 9.375%, to 2029; Ruwais 10%) plus third-party offtake, which is the portfolio model Shell has run for two decades. Plausible.

What the forecasts do not ask of Shell. They do not ask for organic growth (flat liquids at 1.4 mb/d), pricing power, a new cost programme, a re-rating of the renewables business or an acquisition. The second set's mid-cycle earnings of USD 23-24bn by 2028-30 are USD 5-6bn above 2025, of which about USD 3.5bn is the banked self-help, ARC and LNG Canada. Everything else is the deck. In that sense the thesis is unusually undemanding of management.

Where it relies on strengths Shell would need to acquire. Two places, both beyond 18 months: running ARC's shale inventory to deliver the 4% production CAGR (Attachie's problems and Tourmaline's pause show the basin is not easy), and filling the 200-350 kb/d post-2030 liquids gap, for which Shell's demonstrated answer is acquisition, with the attendant dilution (228m shares for ARC). These shape the 2029-30 share count and the multiple, not the 18-month earnings.

Peers. TotalEnergies' expected change rests on demonstrated organic growth (3% a year, 12.2-year reserve life) and needs no Hormuz; BP's rests on deleveraging it has now achieved (net debt USD 13.9bn) and a trading organisation like Shell's; Eni's on a satellite model it has run for years. Shell's change is the most dependent on an external event and the least dependent on new capability of the four.

D-03In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?–44full answer under 2a Internal risks
D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

–36

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the cone of plausible outcomes for FY2027 adjusted EPS runs from about USD 3.6 to about USD 7.5, with three peaks at about 4.2, 5.3 and 7.0; it is about plus or minus 35% around the base, three times the width normal for a company of Shell's size. I weight the peaks 30%, 45% and 25% (the second set had 35/45/20), which puts the probability-weighted mean at about USD 5.4, above the base (5.27 after the buyback change) and above consensus (5.28 at 25-Oct-26, and rising). In earnings the skew is to the upside; in share-price terms it is to the downside, because the upside state brings windfall taxes, demand destruction and the certainty of a later de-rating, while the downside state de-rates the sector at the same moment earnings fall. The cone for cash and distributions is narrow in every state. The case is therefore poorly predictable on 18-month EPS, well predictable on 18-month cash, and highly trackable, because one weekly data series decides most of it.

The three states for FY2027 (adjusted; my figures build on the second set's).

State Probability (second set) Defining assumptions FY2027 net income / EPS FY2028 EPS Free cash flow after leases, FY2027
Fast reopening by Q1 2027, OPEC+ pushes volume 30% (35%) Brent about 70 (a harsher variant 60-65), refining about 11, TTF about 9, trading normal by mid-2027, Qatar and Pearl largely back by H2, buybacks retire more shares at a lower price about USD 23bn / about 4.2 (3.6-3.8 in the harsh variant) about 4.0 about USD 27-29bn: the working-capital release and lower cash taxes cushion
Gradual reopening through H1 2027 (base, the mode) 45% (45%) Brent 88, 80, 74, 72 by quarter; refining 16.5; TTF 12; Qatar and Pearl about 70% restored on average 28.9bn / 5.27 4.65 about USD 32-33bn
Disruption through 2027 25% (20%) Brent about 100, refining 25-28, TTF 15-18, Qatar volumes out, Pearl exporting little, new European or UK levies of about USD 2bn about USD 39bn / about 7.0 (Morgan Stanley's 6.86 sits here) about 5.8 about USD 36-38bn: higher earnings but working capital builds and levies bite

Why I move five points from the first state to the third: the evidence since 29-Oct-26 points to a longer closure (Gulf states planning for hostilities of up to ten years, US carrier groups assembling, Iranian drone capacity restored, a toll regime being set up, QatarEnergy buying 25 years of US LNG as a hedge, Edison's force majeure extended to end-2026, transits at ten a day), against weaker evidence of a near-term corridor (the Oman-Iran talks of August; the World Bank's Q1 2027 partial recovery). The mode does not move; the mean does.

Width. The 30th to 70th percentile of my distribution is roughly USD 4.4 to 6.5; the full plausible range USD 3.6 to 7.5. The broker range at 25-Oct-26 (USD 3.95 to 7.71) is the same cone. For comparison, a mega-cap with this balance sheet would normally carry a one-year EPS cone of about plus or minus 10-15%.

Skew, and why it points the opposite way in earnings and in the share price.

  • In earnings: the upside peak is about 33% above the base and the downside about 20% below, because the downside is cushioned by three mechanisms the second set identified: the three-to-six-month LNG lag (Q1 2027 Integrated Gas prices reflect Q3-Q4 2026 Brent of about USD 100-105), product cracks lagging crude (Bernstein: in three past collapses cracks fell more slowly than crude), and the volume hedge (the Qatar return is worth about USD 1.5-2bn against about USD 8-10bn of lost price). Historical precedent cuts the other way: forecasts made at price peaks overshot in 2008, 2011-14 and 2022 (ExxonMobil's 2024 earnings were 40% below 2022; TotalEnergies' 2024 was 21% below 2023; BP's consensus was cut for seven straight quarters), because analysts' decks adjusted with a lag. That precedent argues that when the reopening comes, consensus will have to travel further than it expects.
  • In the share price: the upside state carries its own penalties. Six EU states lobby for a permanent windfall tax, the ECB's report of 3-Nov-26 discusses one, and the 2022-23 levies took 5-10% of the European majors' free cash flow (Morgan Stanley, 1-Apr-26); global LNG demand already fell 5.4% in H1 2026 on price; and every prolonged-disruption path ends in a sharper reopening later, so the multiple, now at the 99th percentile of five years, is at risk in both states. In the downside state, EPS falls 20%, the energy hedge unwinds (systematic commodity positioning at the 91st percentile in the first set's data) and the multiple falls together with earnings; the first set's trough arithmetic (8x on USD 4.8 gives about USD 39, 28% below the 25-Oct price) applies. The share-price distribution is therefore negatively skewed around a base that already assumes a peak multiple.

The narrow cone: cash and distributions. In all three states free cash flow after leases lands between about USD 27bn and 38bn in FY2027, the dividend costs about USD 9bn, the buyback is at least USD 12bn (floor) and at most about USD 18bn (Morgan Stanley's capacity figure), and ex-lease net debt stays near zero. The USD 50 Brent buyback floor and the sub-USD 40 dividend breakeven are not tested in any state. This is what the second set meant by "the forecast to trust is cash and distributions"; nothing since has weakened it, and the Q3 cash print strengthened it.

FY2028 is a narrower cone (about USD 4.0 to 5.8) because in most paths Qatar is back, Pearl exports, refining is near USD 10-12, the exceptional trading layer is gone and the OPEC+ response has played out; the remaining dispersion is the long-run Brent assumption (65-80) and the buyback pace. Consensus FY2028 of USD 4.83 (dispersion 3.27-6.44 across brokers) sits a little above my 4.65; the gap is USD 72 against USD 75 Brent.

How predictable, then. Over 18 months the EPS outcome is not predictable within about plus or minus 25% from company information; it is predictable from the Hormuz state, which is observable weekly (Morgan Stanley's tracker, Kpler), and the cone narrows month by month as that state resolves: by mid-2027 roughly 70% of the FY2027 outcome will be known. The trackability is unusually good for an unusually wide cone. The investable conclusion: do not underwrite an FY2027 EPS; underwrite the cash lines and the position of the multiple when the state resolves.

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

–62

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: Shell's disclosure is among the most useful in the sector for monitoring the operational building blocks over the next six months, and it is weakest on the two lines that will generate the surprises: the trading contribution and the tax charge. For the six months to about May 2027 the Consumer has three scheduled company checkpoints (the Q4 update note in early January, the Q4 results and Capital Markets Update on 4-Feb-27, the Q1 update note in early April and results in early May), one dated operational milestone (Pearl Train 2 ready by late Q1 2027), one dated transaction (the US chemicals agreement, expected within one to two quarters of September 2026) and one external series that matters more than all of them (Hormuz transits). The case can be monitored well; its most important variable is monitored outside the company's disclosure.

What Shell gives, and how it maps to the thesis drivers.

Thesis driver over six months What Shell discloses How useful What is missing
Price deck and refining margin Indicative refining and chemicals margins in the quarterly update note three weeks before results (Q3: USD 42/bbl flagged on 7-Oct-26, so the beat was 5%, not 20%); published sensitivities (USD 320m of earnings per USD 1/bbl of refining margin; USD 3.7bn of CFFO per USD 10 Brent) High: the margin can also be replicated from forward curves Realised capture, which freight is eroding (Jefferies), is visible only ex post
Integrated Gas volumes and the Middle East return Quarterly production and liquefaction ranges (Q4 2026: 1,080-1,180 kboe/d); Pearl Train 2 "ready by late Q1 2027"; Train 1 restarted but filling inventory High on volumes; medium on timing, which is gated by the Strait Pearl's export schedule; Qatari equity volumes by train; realised LNG price, slopes and the oil-indexed share
Trading and optimisation Qualitative wording only ("significantly higher", "lower than Q2") Low; this is where the 10% quarterly surprises come from (Redburn) Any quantification; the split between physical optimisation and derivative timing
Upstream volumes and costs Quarterly production ranges; opex by segment; structural savings by category High Unit margins by asset; ARC's standalone contribution and Attachie's well results after integration
Distributions and balance sheet Buyback announced each quarter with a completion date; the 40-50% CFFO policy, now stated as "sacrosanct"; net debt and gearing with and without leases High; the 4-Feb-27 update is the decision point for dividend growth, the cost target and any new ambitions The leverage floor at which management stops banking cash; the lease remeasurement, which moves gearing without cash
Cash conversion CFFO before working capital, working capital and cash taxes shown separately each quarter; the CFO explains Q1 and Q4 tax seasonality and the Q4 German certificate and biofuel outflows (about USD 1.5bn) Medium: explained after the fact, not guided Any forward indication of the working-capital release or the cash-tax schedule, which drive the second set's FY2027 cash view
Reported earnings noise Identified items explained each quarter; the USD 6.4bn Q4 2026 non-cash pension derecognition pre-announced High: no reported-EPS shock in Q4 The size and quarter of the US chemicals write-down
Tax rate Not guided; moves with mix and Q4 deferred-tax true-ups (47% in Q4 2025 against 37% for nine months) Low Any jurisdictional guidance; Redburn models a flat 33% and acknowledges the simplification
Carbon cost Annual Report scenario (about USD 1bn in 2026 toward 4bn by 2035) Medium Nothing quarterly

Relative to peers. TotalEnergies guides its average LNG selling price for the coming quarter (above USD 14/mmbtu for Q4 2026); Shell gives no realised-price guidance for Integrated Gas. BP's trading opacity matches Shell's. Equinor's cash-tax schedule is fixed by the Norwegian system and known. ExxonMobil and Chevron have no trading layer of this size to disclose. Shell's pre-close update notes are the sector's best device for reducing the within-quarter surprise, and the first set was right to call them unusual.

What this means for the six months. The Consumer can monitor the volume restoration, the refining margin, the buyback and dividend decisions and the chemicals transaction from company disclosure with high confidence, and will learn the trading result and the tax rate only when they are reported. The variable that decides the case, the Strait, is monitored from shipping trackers, and the second-order variables that decide FY2027 cash (the working-capital release, cash-tax timing) will only be confirmed with the Q1 and Q2 2027 cash-flow statements. One practical rule follows from the Q3 pattern: read the update note's trading wording against the prior quarter and expect the result to land within about 5% of the consensus that forms after the note, not before it.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

–56

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: what Shell says it will achieve traces cleanly onto the lines it controls and poorly onto the lines that make the money. The chain from the capital framework to EPS per share is the clearest in the sector: capex to D&A and free cash flow, distributions to the share count, the dividend to DPS, structural costs to opex by category. The chain from the volume and growth targets to earnings is broken at the unit margin, which Shell does not disclose and which differs by a factor of three between ARC's barrels and the legacy portfolio. The external transmission from the value chain to EPS is clear in direction and in rough magnitude, because Shell publishes sensitivities, but two of the mechanisms that produced the 2026 result, trading and refining capture, have no published transmission function at all, and a third, the tax rate, moves with the earnings mix in a way the company does not explain in advance. The result is a forecast whose per-share mechanics can be audited line by line and whose EBITDA cannot.

The internal trace, item by item.

What this means. An investor can reconstruct Shell's EPS from the deck with about plus or minus 10% error once the quarter's trading result is known, and only within about plus or minus 10% a quarter before it is known (Redburn's surprise record). The per-share denominator can be reconstructed exactly. The company's stated ambitions translate into per-share value through the buyback, not through earnings growth, and that is the honest reading of "FCF per share above 10% a year": at a flat deck, net income drifts down about 1% a year after 2028 and only the share count makes the per-share line rise. Where the first set (C-60, C-72) rated communication highly, it was right about the framework and right to confine the praise to it; where it worried about trading opacity, Q3 confirmed the worry in a benign direction (a 5% beat), and the same opacity will work the other way when volatility fades.

C-82

What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

B384th of 19

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: Shell carries material sentiment-driven risks that could hit the shares sharply whatever its own fundamentals. The largest is a sector-wide de-rating triggered by de-escalation or a risk-on rotation, because the regime treats energy as its hedge and systematic positioning is crowded.

  1. De-escalation headlines. A ceasefire or US-Iran deal: energy has run for two months, and a de-escalation would cut oil and yields together. The Fitness for Fighting digest says "if the market's bull case arrives, this week's leaders fund it", meaning energy.
  2. Rotation back into AI and growth as bonds rally (the US 10-year falling below 4.5%).
  3. Crowded positioning. Energy at the 69th percentile; systematic commodity allocations at the 91st percentile; the Fitness for Fighting Momentum tilt turned Underweight on 23-Oct-26.
  4. Contagion from peers. - A trading blow-up elsewhere (TotalEnergies' USD 800m gas-trading swing hit its quarter). - Windfall-tax announcements aimed at all the European majors. - Read-across from the US majors' results.
  5. The LNG oversupply narrative spreading (Equinor commentary) and hitting Integrated Gas multiples.
  6. M&A speculation. Any sign that Shell would bid for BP would hit Shell's shares.
  7. A technically extended chart. The price is at its 52-week high (4,033p against 4,035.5p), 24% above the 200-day average, with relative strength at the 92nd percentile. A sharp mean reversion is possible.

Magnitude. 10-20% over weeks (the 1990 Gulf War analogue: equities -20% in three months). Shell's low beta and its yield would make it fall less than the higher-beta peers.

C-61How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?C+704th of 19full answer under 4b Management quality
C-70

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

C646th of 19

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: the company's style is to under-promise and over-deliver on controllables, and to raise targets only once they have been met, usually at Capital Markets Days rather than mid-year. Sawan is a steady, formula-driven upgrader ("honest tracker or sandbagger"), not a headline-maker.

  • Cost target. USD 2-3bn by 2025 (CMD23), then USD 5-7bn by 2028 (CMD25), with the midpoint reached about 2.5 years early.
  • Distributions. Raised in steps from 20-30% to 30%+ and then to 40-50% of CFFO; 2025 came in at 52%, above the range.
  • Dividend. +15% (2023), about +4% (2024 and 2025), +5% (Q1 2026, above the 4% policy).
  • Buybacks. USD 3.5bn a quarter sustained for several quarters, trimmed to USD 3.0bn when ARC and working capital required it, with the USD 1.2bn suspended balance then made up.
  • Volumes and capex. Volume outlooks beaten (Q3 2024 +110 kb/d over the range); capex at the bottom of the range (2024).
  • The pattern. Shell does not raise quarterly guidance for windfalls; it reports them. It changes frameworks only at CMDs: March 2025, and probably 2027.
  • What to expect. Q3 and Q4 2026 beats delivered quietly. Any upgrade to the distribution policy or cost target held for a CMD or for the FY2026 results (February 2027).
C-69

What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

B664th of 19

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: Shell's record against guidance is near-perfect on controllables. Against estimates it is a beat in most quarters, with occasional misses, because trading creates roughly ±10% surprises. In comparable high-volatility conditions (2022, 2026), Shell has beaten through trading.

  • Last year.
  • Q4 2025 missed adjusted net income (USD 3.87bn; chemicals and gas).
  • Q1 2026 beat by 8% (USD 6.915bn against USD 6.40bn): Chemicals & Products +55%, Integrated Gas -11%.
  • Q2 2026 beat by 10% (USD 9.836bn against USD 8.92bn): Chemicals & Products +23%, Integrated Gas +11%, Upstream -1%.
  • Capex below consensus both quarters (USD 4.2bn against USD 5.0bn and USD 4.9bn).
  • CFFO before working capital beat in Q1 (USD 17.2bn against USD 14.8bn) and matched in Q2.
  • Through 2025 the trailing net income beats were "high single digit" (Jefferies).
  • Three years.
  • Q2 2023 missed by 10% (Deutsche Bank).
  • FY2024 beat capex and cost guidance; Q3 2024 volumes beat.
  • 2025 distributions came in above range.
  • No guidance misses on capex, costs or buybacks.
  • Five years. The 2022 Integrated Gas windfall (USD 22.2bn of earnings) was a beat-driven year.
  • Comparable conditions. Shell beats in volatile markets (trading) and is more likely to miss in calm or falling ones: Q4 2025 when chemicals and gas trading were weak.
  • For the model. Expect beats to continue in Q3 and Q4 2026. As volatility normalises in 2027, expect trading to become a source of misses.
P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

C605th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the record splits cleanly in two, and the forecasts should be weighted the same way. On the controllables (capex, structural costs, buybacks, the dividend, project delivery) Shell under Sawan has met or beaten every commitment since 2023, so those forecasts deserve about 85-90% weight and should lean slightly favourable: capex at the low end of guidance, distributions at or above the floor. On earnings, Shell does not guide, and its record against consensus is about plus or minus 10% a quarter, driven by trading. Those forecasts deserve about 70% weight for FY2026 (two quarters are banked), about 40% for FY2027 and about 30% for FY2028 onward, because they are deck calls. Lean FY2026 up, FY2027 neutral with the downside heavier, and FY2028 slightly below consensus. Two items deserve a deliberate lean toward delay: the Qatar and Pearl restoration, and LNG Canada Phase 2.

The record, item by item.

Item Commitment Delivery Lean for the forecast
Cash capex USD 22-25bn (2024); USD 20-22bn (2025-28) USD 21bn in 2024; Q1 and Q2 2026 USD 4.2bn each against USD 5.0bn and 4.9bn consensus Low half of the band; partly deferred maintenance, so not all of it is saving
Structural costs USD 2-3bn by 2025, then USD 5-7bn by 2028 USD 5.83bn by mid-2026, about 2.5 years early Target met; no credit beyond it (P-03)

Distributions 40-50% of CFFO 52% in 2025; about 36-37% in 2026 so far Catch-up likely at Q4 2026 or FY2026 results

Why the earnings record earns little weight beyond 2026.

  1. The beats are environmental. In 2026 the beats came from Chemicals & Products (+55% in Q1, +23% in Q2) and Integrated Gas trading (+11% in Q2); Upstream was in line (-1%). That is the volatility rent. In calm or falling markets (Q4 2025, Q2 2023) the same lines produced the misses.
  2. The record under Sawan has not been tested by a real price collapse. 2025 at about USD 69 Brent, with weak chemicals and a ROACE of 9.4%, is the toughest year, and management still delivered costs, capex and buybacks. The 2020 dividend cut (FactSet DPS from USD 1.87 to USD 0.63) belongs to the previous leadership and a fixed-payout policy that has since been replaced.
  3. The pre-close update notes reduce surprises within a quarter but not across quarters. The Q3 2026 note (7-Oct-26) flagged the USD 42/bbl refining margin against about USD 32 expected. These notes make the next print predictable, not FY2027.

Direction of lean, by year.

  • FY2026: up. Q3 and Q4 are largely visible: the USD 42 refining margin, Brent above USD 100, ARC from 2-Sep-26 and oil-indexed LNG priced on Q2-Q3 Brent. My FY2026 net income is USD 37.5bn against USD 36.1bn consensus (+4%). I expect Q3 adjusted earnings of about USD 10.8bn, against a consensus-implied H2 average of about USD 9.7bn a quarter.
  • FY2027: neutral on the mean, downside-heavy on the mode. Consensus has risen 21% in three months (FactSet mean EPS +15% in one month) as brokers moved toward spot. My base is 2% below consensus net income; the reopening scenario is 22% below it.
  • FY2028: slightly below. Consensus rests on about USD 75 Brent; I use USD 72 and USD 12 refining, giving net income 6% below and EPS 5% below.

The integration risks to watch. ARC's USD 250m synergies are due by about September 2027 (management says integration is "ahead of plan"). The BG precedent (2016, led by Sawan) is favourable. AECO below USD 2/mcf is the economic risk, not execution.

F4713th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement: Shell's forecasts over the next four years are dominated by one uncontrollable variable, the commodity deck, which the company's controllable variables cushion but do not offset. From the FY2026 peak (adjusted net income about USD 37.5bn, EPS about 6.63), earnings normalise over two years to a mid-cycle of about USD 23-24bn and EPS of about 4.6. That mid-cycle is better than 2025 by about USD 5.7bn of earnings (about USD 3.5bn of it structural) and about 45% per share, the result of banked self-help, ARC and LNG Canada, and the share count. It does not grow after 2028 except through buybacks.

The pieces join up as follows.

  • Operating leverage is price leverage (P-01, P-04). About USD 15bn of net income leaves with the deck between FY2026 and FY2028. Volume (P-01), cost (P-03) and pricing power (P-04) give back only about USD 3bn.
  • The cash and per-share lines are much steadier than profit (P-02, P-05, P-08). Working-capital release, cash-tax timing, falling capex and a buyback that retires about 5% a year make FY2027 free cash flow fall about 5% and EPS fall about 21%, against a 23% fall in net income. This is the part of the forecast with high confidence.
  • The record supports the controllables and not the profit line (P-06). Model capex, buybacks and dividends at guidance or better; give FY2027-28 earnings low weight; build them on decks.
  • Consensus already carries the right shape (P-09). My differences from it are timing and deck: FY2026 about 4% higher, FY2027 about level, FY2028 about 5% lower in EPS. The broker range for FY2027 (USD 3.95-7.71) is the cone of Hormuz outcomes, and consensus is its average, not its mode.

For the Consumer judging predictability over 18 months: the distributions, capex, share count and net debt are highly predictable. Q4 2026 and Q1 2027 earnings are moderately predictable, because oil-indexed LNG lags and the update notes give visibility. FY2027 earnings are not predictable within about plus or minus 25%, and the deciding variable is geopolitical, not operational.

P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

B603rd of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: below operating profit, the items that move are tax mix, inventory and impairment noise in reported earnings, and the share count; interest barely moves. On my base, adjusted net income falls about 39% from FY2026 to FY2030 while EPS falls only about 28%, because the share count falls about 5% a year once ARC's 228m new shares are absorbed in 2026. Reported net income will run USD 2-4bn below adjusted in FY2027-28 because falling prices create inventory holding losses and the chemicals exit is likely to carry an impairment. Free cash flow after leases holds above net income in every year because D&A (USD 24-26bn) exceeds capex (USD 20.5-22bn) and working capital releases in 2027-28.

Tax.

  • Reported rates were 44.9% (2023), 49.8% (2024) and 41.4% (2025); Shell's Tax Contribution Report gives a 44.8% global effective rate for 2024 (USD 12.5bn on USD 29.9bn).
  • On the model's adjusted, joint-venture-inclusive basis (joint-venture results enter EBITDA post-tax; minority interests of about USD 0.3-0.4bn are folded in), the rate that reproduces adjusted earnings was about 35.5% in FY2025. I use 35.0% (FY2026), 36.0%, 36.5%, 36.5% and 36.0%. FY2026 is lowest because refining and trading, which are taxed at lower rates, are an unusually large share of profit. The mix moves back to Upstream and Integrated Gas from 2027. ARC's Canadian profits (about 25%) lower the rate a little, and the UK levy ends in March 2030 inside Adura.
  • Levies: Brazil's 12% export levy cost USD 242m in Q2 2026 and is suspended pending appeal; I assume it lapses in 2027 (risk: about USD 0.5-1bn a year if reinstated at high prices). UK budget of 28-Oct-26: outcome not in the files; Shell's UK exposure is now inside the equity-accounted Adura venture, so the effect on Shell's line items is diluted.
  • Carbon compliance is an operating cost, not tax: USD 398m in 2025, about USD 1bn in 2026, rising toward about USD 4bn by 2035.

Interest. Net interest USD -3.7bn to -3.9bn a year over 2023-25. I use USD -3.8bn (FY2026), -3.5bn, -3.2bn, -3.1bn and -3.1bn. About USD 1.4-1.5bn a year is lease interest, which deleveraging does not remove.

Identified items, inventory effects and impairments (reported earnings only).

  • History: reported net income was USD 8.9bn below adjusted in 2023 and USD 7.6bn below in 2024. In 2025 it was only USD 0.7bn below, helped by the USD 2.8bn Adura disposal gain. Impairments were USD 8.3bn (2023), 4.4bn (2024), 3.7bn (2025) and USD 0.67bn in Q2 2026.
  • Forecast: FY2026 reported close to adjusted (inventory holding gains as prices rose offset impairments and derivative losses; Q1 -USD 2.4bn, Q2 +USD 0.4bn). FY2027 reported about USD 4bn below adjusted: inventory holding losses of about USD 2-3bn as Brent falls from about USD 100 to about USD 72 during the year, plus USD 1-2bn of impairments. FY2028 reported USD 1-3bn below adjusted on the likely loss or write-down on the US chemicals sale (Monaca alone cost about USD 14bn). FY2029-30 about USD 1bn below.
  • The auditor change to PwC for FY2027 could revisit Level 3 commodity-derivative valuations (USD 9.5bn of FY2025 revenue came from fair-value accounting). I treat this as noise with zero expected value.

Joint ventures and associates. These are worth about USD 28bn in FactSet's enterprise value. Adura is self-funded and expected to pay more than USD 1bn of dividends in total to its two parents over 2026-27. Raizen (44%) has filed a restructuring that contemplates a USD 685m capital injection by Shell; I put that in acquisitions for 2026. Joint-venture income is in my EBITDA; joint-venture cash arrives as dividends, which over 2026-27 I treat as roughly equal to the income.

Buybacks and the share count, against the last six years.

  • 2019-25: 7,857m to 5,697m shares (-27%; about -5.1% a year, about -6.5% a year in 2022-25), at forward P/E multiples of mostly 5-9x. My 2027-30 pace (about 5% a year) is therefore close to the 2019-25 average, not a slowdown from it, once the ARC issue is absorbed.
  • 2026: about 295m retired (USD 13.5bn) and 228m issued for ARC, so the year-end count falls only about 1% (to about 5,625m). The issue cost about one year of accretion.
  • 2027-30: USD 14.5bn, 14.0bn, 13.5bn and 13.5bn at an assumed USD 52-53. That retires 260-275m a year, about 4.9-5.4% of a shrinking base. Year-end counts: about 5,351m, 5,082m, 4,822m and 4,562m. Average diluted counts: 5,523m, 5,252m, 4,987m and 4,727m (about 35m of dilution added).
  • Why my pace is faster than the first set's 3.5-4.5% and consensus's implied 3.5%: policy headroom. My FY2027 distributions (USD 9.0bn of dividends plus USD 14.5bn of buybacks) are about 39% of CFFO including the working-capital release and 44% excluding it, inside 40-50%; consensus distributions look nearer USD 12bn of buybacks. Sensitivity: every USD 5 lower share price adds about 0.5 points a year to accretion; buybacks of USD 12bn instead of 14bn cut FY2028 EPS by about USD 0.10.

P-10

Pulling together all the above answers, in all sets so far, what is your carefully considered base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

F4713th of 16

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: my base case is a gradual Hormuz reopening through H1 2027 and a two-year normalisation of the deck to USD 72 Brent and USD 12 refining by 2028. Production steps up to about 3.05 mboe/d in 2027 and 3.2 mboe/d from 2028; LNG sales grow 4-5% a year; costs are flat in dollars; capex is USD 20.5-22bn; buybacks are USD 13.5-14.5bn a year. For the next 18 months the forecast to trust is cash and distributions; the forecast to doubt is FY2027 earnings.

7b

Margins/growth vs. past

6 answers
C-5350C-7862C-8052C-6755P-0440P-0948
C-53How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?D5014th of 19full answer under 4c Financial profile
C-78

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

D628th of 19

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: beyond costs there is meaningful, plausible scope to improve financial outputs over the mid term. It is concentrated in a few big, dated, largely controllable areas: volume restoration, ARC, lower capex in 2027 and the chemicals exit. Peer precedents show these are achievable, and they fit the stated strategy.

The big areas.

  1. Integrated Gas volume restoration. - Qatar is back from 78 toward 263 kboe/d. - Pearl GTL Train 2 returns in Q1 2027; Morgan Stanley cites up to USD 2.5bn of post-tax free cash flow from Pearl when unconstrained. - This is plausibly worth USD 1.5-3bn a year of earnings when restored; the range is my estimate from the cited figures.
  2. ARC. About USD 1.5bn a year of free cash flow; USD 250m of synergies by the end of year one.
  3. Capex down. From USD 24-26bn (2026, including acquisition cash) to USD 20-22bn (2027-28): about USD 4bn more free cash flow a year.
  4. Chemicals. Removing chronic losses and freeing about USD 25bn of low-return capital. Sale proceeds fund buybacks or debt reduction.

The smaller areas.

  • LNG Canada at full rate, with Phase 2 later.
  • Deepwater start-ups (Whale ramp, Bonga North).
  • Trading at normal levels.
  • The end of the UK levy (2030) and Brazilian relief.
  • Divestment proceeds of about USD 3-4bn a year.

Peer precedents.

  • Chevron achieved USD 3bn of structural savings ahead of schedule.
  • ExxonMobil targets USD 7bn more by 2030.
  • TotalEnergies' asset recycling (KKR and others).
  • Eni's satellite model crystallised more than EUR 30bn.
  • BP is copying the playbook.

The offsets.

  • The windfall layer fades.
  • Divestments dilute earnings.
  • The gas-weighted mix lowers realised dollars per boe.

For the model. These levers make 2027 adjusted earnings fall far less than the price deck alone implies, and they support FY2028 consensus (USD 25.7bn) as achievable at about USD 70-75 Brent.

C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

D5212th of 19

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: the core growth engine has not decelerated over the last twelve months. Revenue growth accelerated sharply, driven by price. Shell's "normal" revenue growth rate is close to zero over a cycle (2019-25 CAGR about -4%), and its volume engine is weak organically (about 1%). The input change force, the commodity price, has not slowed yet, but its second derivative is likely near its peak.

  • Quarterly revenue growth year on year (FactSet).
  • -10.3% (Q2 2025), -4.1% (Q3 2025), -7.0% (Q4 2025), +0.7% (Q1 2026), +44.7% (Q2 2026).
  • Q3 2026 will be strong on the USD 42 refining margin and Brent above USD 100.
  • Volumes.
  • Integrated Gas production fell 31% sequentially in Q2 2026 on the Middle East shut-ins, a deceleration that was then masked by trading.
  • It re-accelerates from Q4 2026 with ARC and from Q1 2027 with Pearl.
  • The input force.
  • Brent at USD 111 in late October; European refining margins expected to peak in Q3 2026 (Redburn).
  • LNG spot prices expected to fall to about USD 9/mmbtu over 2026-28 once the supply wave lands (Bernstein).
  • What it means. The deceleration is ahead, not behind: price-driven in 2027 and partly offset by volume.
C-67

How do the sell-side's forecasts for the next one to three years compare with the last one to five years, for the main financial statement line items in particular and the granular working up to them, and how credible is the gap given the change forces and foundations found above?

C-559th of 19

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: the sell-side forecasts a single windfall year (FY2026) followed by a two-year normalisation that already brings FY2028 back to roughly mid-cycle. FY2026 looks conservative given Q3 margins. FY2027 is the most exposed year, because it embeds a still-elevated deck. FY2028 is credible to slightly conservative, given the volume and self-help levers.

Line by line (brokers' basis; history FY2023/24/25, consensus FY2026/27/28/29).

  • Revenue. USD 316.6bn / 284.3bn / 266.9bn; consensus 351.8bn / 323.6bn / 307.1bn / 308.2bn (+31.8%, -8.0%, -5.1%, +0.4%). FY2026 sits below FY2022 (USD 369.6bn on FactSet's restated basis).
  • EBITDA. USD 69.4bn / 64.4bn / 56.6bn; consensus 81.65bn / 74.0bn / 64.5bn / 70.4bn. The FY2029 rise rests on only 7 brokers and is a coverage artefact.
  • EBITDA margin. 21.2% (2025) to 23.2%, 22.9%, 21.0%.
  • EBIT. Consensus USD 57.7bn / 47.9bn / 41.1bn / 44.2bn, with D&A of USD 23-24bn rising to USD 26.9bn in FY2029 as ARC is added.
  • Adjusted net income.
  • Shell's own history: USD 28.25bn (2023), 23.7bn (2024), 18.5bn (2025).
  • Consensus: 36.1bn / 29.5bn / 25.7bn / 24.5bn.
  • EPS. USD 4.20 / 3.76 / 3.15 to USD 6.37 / 5.28 / 4.83 / 4.62.
  • Implied share count. About 5.67bn (2026), 5.59bn (2027), 5.32bn (2028), 5.30bn (2029). About 3.5% a year to 2028, then flat in 2029, an artefact of thin coverage.
  • DPS. USD 1.28 / 1.38 / 1.42 to USD 1.58 / 1.64 / 1.71 / 1.78 (about +4% a year).
  • Operating cash flow. USD 51.9bn / 52.5bn / 40.7bn to 61.5bn / 54.6bn / 51.7bn / 56.2bn.
  • Capex. About USD 21bn flat.
  • Free cash flow (excluding leases). USD 20.5bn / 24.3bn / 13.3bn to 38.8bn / 32.5bn / 29.6bn / 27.3bn.
  • Implied tax. Net income over pre-tax profit gives about 69% (2026), 67% (2027) and 73% (2028). That implies leakage of about 27-33%, below the 41-50% reported historically. Part of the gap is post-tax joint-venture income inside pre-tax profit. This is a check item for the Consumer.

Credibility, given the forces.

  • FY2026 needs about USD 19.4bn in H2, against H1's USD 16.75bn. With a Q3 refining margin of USD 42, Brent above USD 100 and ARC consolidated from September, it looks beatable.
  • FY2027 (USD 29.5bn) implies roughly USD 80-85 Brent plus elevated refining (my inference, from the sensitivities in C-75), plus the volume step-up. It is credible only if a meaningful part of the geopolitical premium persists through 2027. It rose 21% in three months as brokers moved toward spot.
  • FY2028 (USD 25.7bn) is close to Morgan Stanley's USD 24-25bn mid-cycle (USD 70 Brent, USD 10 refining) plus ARC, Qatar and costs. Credible.

The dispersion is the key fact. Broker FY2027 EPS runs from USD 3.95 (ING) to 7.71 (Barclays), and FY2028 from USD 3.27 (GERDES) to 6.44 (Wells Fargo or Redburn). "Consensus" is a midpoint between opposing price decks, not a shared view.

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: net, the forecasts return Shell to a mid-cycle that is structurally better than 2023-25 but lower than 2026. By FY2028-30 revenue is back at 2024-25 levels (about USD 262-269bn); adjusted EBITDA is about USD 65-67bn, equal to 2024 at about USD 8 lower Brent; adjusted net income is about USD 23-24bn, 25-30% above 2025's USD 18.5bn; and EPS is about USD 4.6-4.8, about 45-50% above 2025's USD 3.15. Of the per-share gain over FY2025, about 45% is Shell's own earnings structure (costs, ARC, LNG Canada, exits, the absence of 2025's weak quarters), about 30% is the lower share count and about 25% is a slightly better deck. What does not improve is growth: at a flat deck, net income drifts down about 1% a year after 2028 and only buybacks keep EPS rising.

Where the net impact comes from, FY2025 to FY2028 (adjusted net income, USDbn, my estimates). 18.5 (FY2025) plus about 1.9 from the deck (Brent +3, refining +3, gas -2) plus about 1.0 from ARC including synergies plus about 0.5 from LNG Canada at full capacity plus about 0.4 from costs net of inflation plus about 0.3 from chemicals plus about 1.0 from the absence of 2025's weak trading and chemicals quarters, less about 0.9 of carbon cost, less about 0.4 of divestment dilution, plus about 0.3 of interest and other items, plus about 1.6 from Upstream and LNG volume growth (Whale, Mero, LNG sales growth of 4-5% a year) and other items: about 24.2. The last item is the balancing figure in my build and the least certain; without it FY2028 would be about USD 22.6bn, which is my bear-leaning check on the base.

The judgements the Consumer should carry.

  1. The structural improvement is real but modest and mostly banked. About USD 3.5bn of the USD 5.7bn gain from FY2025 to FY2028 is Shell's own doing; the rest is deck and the recovery from 2025's weak trading and chemicals quarters. After 2028 there is no further self-help in my base.
  2. The windfall years do not change the mid-cycle, except through the balance sheet. FY2026-27 add about USD 18bn of net income above mid-cycle. That cash ends up as about USD 15bn of lower net debt and about 5% fewer shares, worth about USD 0.25-0.30 of mid-cycle EPS. It is the most durable legacy of 2026.
  3. Consensus already has this shape. Brokers take EPS from 6.37 to 4.62 by FY2029. My differences are timing (FY2026 higher) and the deck (FY2028 lower), not the shape.
