Forecasts and upgrades

8

Probably upgrades this financial year to

–not yet rated

Memo topic 7, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

–48

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement: the gap between our assessment and what guidance, consensus and the narrative already reflect is small, and it is favourable only in the near term and in one event. Guidance will be met and probably raised at the 4-Feb-27 Capital Markets Update (a dividend reset to 6-8%, a higher cost target, a buyback of USD 4-5bn), and the market half-expects it (consensus dividend growth about 5.5%; Morgan Stanley's 10% is one house). Consensus goes up into February, on Q4 prices, the LNG lag and the share count, and then comes down from mid-2027 in the two most likely states; its probability-weighted end-point for FY2027 is roughly where it stands. The narrative knows the two big things, that the windfall fades and that the dividend may be reset, and misses only small things, most of them mildly negative. Our model after the fixes is in line with consensus on FY2027 EPS (5.27 against 5.28), above it on FY2026 cash and the share count, below it on FY2028 by 4%, and well below the market's implied multiple, since the shares sit at the 99th percentile of their five-year forward P/E and the model's own guideline multiple is at the ten-year 90th percentile. The investable gap is therefore a timing gap: the three months to the February update are the window in which guidance, estimates and narrative are all moving in the case's favour; after that the case depends on the Strait, and the market already prices the hedge.

  • From I-01: the frameworks will be met; the raise is likely and dated; the only reduction path is a fast reopening with an OPEC+ volume push, and even that returns distributions to the floors, not below them. Favourable, modest, concentrated in one event.
  • From I-02: upgrades for two prints, cuts thereafter in 70% of states; FY2028 anchored near our figure. Neutral over 18 months, favourable for three months.
  • From I-03: the sell-side has the shape right; the omissions are small and mostly negative; investor commentary is not in the files. Slightly unfavourable.
  • The link to the model: after the revenue-basis fix the model's EPS equals the second set's plus about 1% for the faster buyback (USD 6.63, 5.27, 4.65, 4.64, 4.85), its FY2026 free cash flow about USD 35bn, and its 18-month return at the 11.58x guideline about +5-7% rather than +10%. That return requires the sector's regime to hold for 14 months while EPS falls about 30% and the Strait reopens; D-07 showed the multiple is three-quarters the sector's. For the memo's #11 and #12 the favourable gap in guidance and near-term estimates is smaller than the unfavourable gap between the guideline multiple and what a normalised-earnings market will pay.
8a

Guidance

I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

–58

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: Shell will meet every item of formal guidance over the next 18 months with high probability, because its guidance consists of frameworks set for a USD 60-70 world and it does not guide earnings. The live question is not meeting but raising, and the date is 4-Feb-27, when the Q4 2026 results and a Capital Markets Update coincide. My probabilities for that event: a dividend growth policy above 4% (most likely 6-8%, possibly Morgan Stanley's 10%) about 55%; a structural cost target above USD 7bn about 50%; a post-2030 volume or resource plan about 60%; a change to the 40-50% distribution range about 15%; a change to the USD 20-22bn capex range about 15%, more likely up than down. The buyback for the quarter after 4-Feb-27 is likely to be USD 4-5bn again (about 65%) and then to step down toward USD 3.5bn through 2027 as the deck normalises; the probability it falls to the USD 3bn floor within 18 months is about 30%, and the probability of a cut below the floor is under 5%. Guidance is reduced in only one state, a fast reopening followed by an OPEC+ volume push, and even there the reduction is a return to the floors, not a breach of them. For 18 months to three years, the targets most likely to be raised are LNG sales growth and the cost programme; the ones at risk are the 4% production CAGR (Attachie) and the timing of LNG Canada Phase 2 (early 2030s).

The events and what each is likely to bring.

Event Date What is decided or disclosed Most likely outcome What would make it a raise What would make it a reduction
Q4 2026 update note early January 2027 Q4 ranges; indicative refining margin (Q4 Brent about 105, TTF above EUR 100); trading wording; working capital and tax signposts Strong Q4, in line with the second set's USD 9.95bn A refining margin above USD 30 and "significantly higher" trading Trading "lower", Rhine or hurricane downtime
Q4 2026 results and Capital Markets Update 4-Feb-27 Buyback for the next quarter; dividend policy; cost target; capex 2027-28; production and LNG growth; post-2030 plan; 2026 payout at about 39-40% of CFFO Buyback USD 4-5bn; dividend growth reset to 6-8% a year; cost target "above USD 7bn" or re-based; capex held at 20-22bn; a resource and longevity narrative built on ARC, Phase 2, Bonga North and Orca Brent still above USD 90, Q4 CFFO above USD 15bn, gearing near 15%, a chemicals agreement signed Brent below USD 70 in January after a settlement; management then keeps 4% and USD 3-3.5bn and banks cash, as Gorman has said it would
20-F March 2027 Reserves and reserve life after ARC; carbon-cost scenario; tax by country; Attachie disclosure Reserve life up with ARC's 2bn boe of 2P A reserves write-down at Attachie
Q1 2027 update note and results early April and early May 2027 Dividend increase for the year (5% in 2026 against a 4% policy); buyback; Pearl Train 2 status; Q1 Integrated Gas still carrying Q4 2026 oil prices through the lag Dividend up 6% or more (about 70%); buyback USD 4bn-plus if Brent above 80 Pearl exporting; Qatar undamaged trains restarting A fast reopening with Brent in the 60s and the buyback back at USD 3-3.5bn
Q2 2027 (July or August 2027) The first clean post-reopening quarter in the base case; trading normalising; working-capital release visible Earnings down about 35% year on year; cash resilient; buyback USD 3.5bn An earnings miss on trading, as in Q4 2025
Q3 2027 and Q4 2027 (October or November 2027; February 2028) Capex 2028 confirmed inside 20-22bn; ARC synergies at the twelve-month mark (September 2027); the chemicals exit's accounting; the 2028 buyback pace Guidance met; buyback at or near the floor if Brent is near 70 Chemicals proceeds near USD 8bn; AECO above CAD 3 A second ARC-style resource acquisition funded with shares

Why "met" is near-certain on the frameworks. Capex has come in at or below guidance every year under this management; the 2026 organic spend of about USD 20.5bn was below the ex-ARC range. Costs stand at USD 5.83bn against USD 5-7bn with the CFO guiding to the top. Buybacks have run for 19 quarters including 2025 at USD 69 Brent and a 9.4% ROACE, and the balance sheet (2.7% ex-lease gearing) has never been stronger; the second set's base case has free cash flow after leases of USD 32-33bn in FY2027 against distributions of about USD 25bn. Only the harsh variant of the fast-reopening state (Brent at 60-65 for most of 2027) pushes distributions to the 40% floor at the USD 3bn buyback; nothing in the cone pushes them below it.

Why "raised" is the live question, and what drives it. Three facts make a raise at the 4-Feb-27 update more likely than not. First, the 2026 payout will land at about 39-40% of CFFO even after a USD 5bn quarter, at the bottom of a range management calls sacrosanct through the cycle, and Jefferies notes the payout ran at 50% of the range before the crisis; a progressive dividend is the cheapest way to lift the ratio without committing to a buyback pace that falls with Brent. Second, the dividend grew at 2.1% a year under Sawan before 2026 (Morgan Stanley), below inflation, and the 5% rise of 2026 broke that pattern; Morgan Stanley's thesis (10% a year to 2030, the catalyst for closing the valuation gap) is the most-cited sell-side call on the stock, Redburn assumes 6% with the Q4 results and consensus implies about 5.5%. Third, the cost programme has reached its original range two and a half years early and Redburn says there is room to raise it. Against a raise: management's demonstrated preference to bank windfall cash for "flexibility to accelerate buybacks during downturns" (Gorman), the pattern of changing frameworks only at Capital Markets Days, and the possibility that Brent has fallen sharply by February. My reading of the balance is a 6-8% dividend growth policy (55%), a 10% policy (15% of that), no change (30%); I model 6%, which is where the probability-weighted outcome sits.

The buyback path, and the reason I lift the model's FY2027 figure to USD 16bn from 14.5bn. The Q3 programme of USD 5bn (against 3.0bn in Q2 and 4.2bn in the Q3 period including the ARC catch-up) is a step change announced when Brent was above USD 100 and gearing was falling toward 15%. In the base case Brent is about 88 in Q1 2027 and 80 in Q2, so the programmes announced in February and May are likely to be USD 4-5bn and USD 4bn, fading to 3.5bn in the second half. Cash executed in calendar 2027 of about USD 16bn gives total distributions of about USD 25bn, about 42% of my FY2027 CFFO, inside the range and above the 2026 ratio. In the fast-reopening state the figure is about USD 14bn; in the prolonged state USD 18bn (Morgan Stanley's capacity estimate). The second set's 14.5bn was set before the USD 5bn signal; it is now the low end, not the centre.

What would make guidance be reduced. Only the combination of a reopening before February and an OPEC+ decision to recover market share (Brent to the low 60s) would bring the buyback back to USD 3-3.5bn and the dividend growth to 4%, and that is a return to the floors, not a breach. Two company-specific events could do the same: a large acquisition funded in cash (management has shown it would rather issue shares and keep the buyback), or damage to Shell's own Gulf assets in an escalation (Pearl was hit once). Neither is in my base case. The 2027 capex range could be raised to accommodate LNG Canada Phase 2 and Coastal GasLink Phase 2 overruns, which fall on the LNG Canada partners; that would be a "raise" the market would read negatively.

Secondary: 18 months to three years. LNG sales growth of 4-5% a year is more likely to be raised than cut (Phase 2 sanctioned; Ruwais; North Field East and South delayed but coming; third-party offtake). The structural cost target is likely to be raised or replaced. The 4% production CAGR to 2030 is at risk from Attachie's underperformance and from Tourmaline-style basin discipline; management's likely answer is bolt-on resource, which protects the volume target at the cost of per-share accretion. The "FCF per share above 10% a year to 2030" target was set on a 2025 base at USD 69 Brent and will be met on that base; management may re-base it to the LTIP's normalised measure. LNG Canada Phase 2's early-2030s start carries about a 40% probability of slipping on turbines, labour and the pipeline compression stations. Carbon and transition targets will be softened further rather than raised.

Against peers in the same season. Five of the six European majors held distributions at the bottom of their frameworks in Q3 (Morgan Stanley); TotalEnergies reaffirmed its framework at its September Capital Markets Day with little room to re-rate; BP has reached its net-debt target early and will restart buybacks, competing for the same capital; Eni raised its buyback and paid a special dividend. Shell is the one whose framework has the most unused headroom and the one with a dated event to use it.

How favourable the gap is. The market expects guidance to be met and partly expects a dividend reset (consensus DPS growth about 5.5%; Morgan Stanley's 10% is one house). Our view (6% dividend growth, USD 16bn of 2027 buybacks against a consensus-implied share count that assumes about USD 12bn, a cost target raise) sits a little above what is reflected. The gap is favourable but modest, and it is concentrated in one event.

I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

–50

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: consensus will go up before it comes down. Over the next two reporting events (the Q4 2026 update note in January and the results and Capital Markets Update of 4-Feb-27) FY2026 and FY2027 estimates are more likely to be upgraded than cut (about 75%), because Q4 is being priced off Brent near USD 105 and TTF above EUR 100, the oil-indexed LNG lag carries those prices into Q1 2027, Morgan Stanley has already lifted its 2027 refining margin to USD 25/bbl, and a buyback above the floor lifts per-share figures even at flat net income. From about mid-2027, in 70% of my distribution (the fast-reopening and gradual states), FY2027 consensus is cut back to roughly where it stands today or below, as the deck normalises and trading turns from a source of beats into a source of misses; the net breadth of revisions turns negative in the second half of 2027. Over the full 18 months the FY2027 consensus EPS ends near USD 5.0-5.3 in the base case (today 5.28), near 4.2-4.5 in a fast reopening and near 6.5-7.0 if the disruption persists. FY2028-29 consensus (USD 4.83 and 4.62) is anchored on about USD 75 Brent and will be held or trimmed by about 5% toward my 4.65; it moves materially only if the long-run deck moves. The gap between our view and consensus is favourable for the next two prints and unfavourable thereafter, and the two roughly cancel.

Where consensus stands and how it has moved. At 25-Oct-26 the FactSet mean EPS for FY2026 was USD 6.33 (+12.2% in a month, +23.5% in three, +92.9% in twelve), for FY2027 5.37 (+15.0%, +21.0%, +40.4%) and for FY2028 4.72 (+1.6%, +3.5%, +2.9%). The dashboard's FY2027 series shows EPS +11%, +21%, +18% over one, three and six months, EBITDA +6%, +13%, +17%, target price +2%, +7%, +5%, a revision-momentum score of 13 (peers 11-15), and a buy share of 39%, unchanged over three months and up nine points over six; TotalEnergies' buy share is 58% and BP's 52%. The mean rating is Overweight (1.59), the median Hold, and the mean target (USD 53.03) sat below the price (53.98): the stock trades at its target. After the Q3 results Redburn raised 2026-28 by 5% (FY2027 USD 5.30) and Morgan Stanley moved to USD 6.86 for FY2027; the published range runs from ING's 3.95 (Brent in the 60s) to Barclays' 7.71. The dispersion is the cone of D-04, not disagreement about Shell.

The path, event by event, in the base case.

  1. November 2026 to 4-Feb-27: upgrades. The pre-Q3 FY2026 consensus of USD 36.1bn implied only about USD 8.5bn for Q4; the second set's Q4 is USD 9.95bn and Morgan Stanley's full year is 38bn, so FY2026 moves to about 37.5-38bn. FY2027 moves up 5-10% (to about USD 5.6-5.9) as brokers mark Q1 2027 to the forward curve and to the LNG lag, lift refining toward Morgan Stanley's USD 25, and add the USD 5bn buyback to their share counts (consensus implies about 5.59bn average shares in 2027 against my 5.49bn, a 2% per-share difference on its own). Breadth stays positive. The Q4 print itself (4-Feb-27) is likely a beat of the pre-note consensus by low single digits, as Q3 was.
  2. February to May 2027: upgrades slow; the mix of revisions shifts to per-share. The Capital Markets Update (a dividend reset at 6-8%, a higher cost target, a buyback of USD 4-5bn) raises DPS and lowers the share count in models without changing net income. Q1 2027 (early May) still carries Q4 2026 oil prices through the Integrated Gas lag and a Brent average near 88 in the base case, so it beats or meets; the first visible trading normalisation appears in the Q1 update note's wording.
  3. June to December 2027: cuts. Brent at 74-72 in the second half, the indicative refining margin falling toward 12-15, chemicals near breakeven and trading at its baseline bring FY2027 consensus back toward USD 5.0-5.3 and trim FY2028 toward my 4.65. The precedent is strong: after the 2008, 2011-14 and 2022 peaks consensus was cut for several quarters because decks lagged the price (BP's was cut for seven straight quarters after 2022; ExxonMobil's 2024 earnings were 40% below 2022), and Shell's own misses cluster in calm or falling quarters (Q2 2023, Q4 2025). Net breadth turns negative; the buy share, which rose nine points in six months, is more likely to fall than rise in this leg.
  4. Into 2028: FY2028 becomes the "current year"; consensus of about USD 4.7-4.8 meets a model at 4.65. The share count and the chemicals exit are the upside items; a long-run Brent of 70 rather than 75, AECO weakness and the carbon cost the downside. Held, with a slight downward drift.

In the other states. Fast reopening by Q1 2027: the up-leg is cut short in January or February; FY2027 falls to about USD 4.2-4.5 by mid-2027, FY2028 to about 4.0-4.3, and the breadth of cuts is broad because almost every broker's 2027 deck is above USD 70. This is the state in which the sell-side has the furthest to travel and the sector de-rates at the same time. Prolonged disruption: FY2027 rises toward USD 6.5-7.0 through the year; FY2028 rises toward 5.5; buy ratings increase; the market begins to debate windfall taxes and to price the eventual reopening, so the shares respond less than the estimates.

Why the probability-weighted change for FY2027 is small and the path is what matters. Weighting the end-points (about 4.3 at 30%, 5.1 at 45%, 6.8 at 25%) gives about USD 5.3, which is where consensus already stands. The sell-side's blended number is a fair estimate of the mean; it is a poor estimate of any path. The memo's #7 and #8 topics should therefore be framed as "upgrades into February, cuts from mid-year in the two most likely states", with the February event as the point at which momentum is most favourable and the valuation most exposed.

Secondary: 18 months to three years. FY2028 and FY2029 consensus (USD 4.83 and 4.62; FY2029 on eight brokers) sit within 4% of my 4.65 and 4.64. The levers that would move them: the share count (consensus implies about 3.5% a year of reduction against my 5%, worth about +3% to FY2029 EPS if brokers converge on the faster pace), the chemicals exit (+USD 0.06-0.10), the Pearl and Qatar return at full rate (already in most models), the long-run deck (USD 5 of Brent is about USD 0.30), AECO, and carbon costs (about USD 0.04 a year of EPS, cumulative, which no broker in the files models). Held is the most likely outcome, with upgrades if the Capital Markets Update converts the buyback headroom into a stated pace and cuts if the long-run deck settles at USD 70.

Where I differ from the earlier sets. The first set (C-74) said the near-term "easy beat" setup was fading as estimates caught up with spot; Q3 showed there is still a beat in each quarter while the update note is read conservatively, and I expect two more. The second set leaned FY2026 up, FY2027 neutral and FY2028 slightly below consensus; I agree on all three, and add the timing: the FY2027 "neutral" is an average of an up-leg and a down-leg.

11d

Current multiple

–not yet ratedMemo topic 11, : the Rater's score1 answer
D-0746
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

–46

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: over the latest six months about 71% of Shell's daily share-price variance is explained by the peer basket (correlation 0.84, beta 0.83), and the share of idiosyncratic movement has fallen from 57% a year ago to 29% as the geopolitical regime made the sector move as one. Shell's return of +17.9% against the basket's +11.4% nevertheless contains about eight points of company-specific outperformance, earned on dated company events. The multiple is more industry-driven than the price: the whole sector sits at elevated percentiles of its own history, Shell's position within the peer set is ordinary (P/E 10.0x against TotalEnergies 8.7x, BP 10.8x, Eni 11.0x; a ten-year P/E percentile of 70 against a peer median above 80), and its 24-month-forward P/E at the 99th percentile of five years reflects the same forward earnings collapse every major shows. I judge the multiple to be about three-quarters industry-driven and one-quarter company-driven, the company part being a modest defensiveness premium (beta 0.83, LNG and trading, the strongest balance sheet) plus the option on a dividend reset. The consequence for the case is that Shell's own delivery cannot defend the multiple if the sector de-rates, and the guideline fair multiple of 11.58x will be reached or lost on the Strait, not on Shell.

The evidence.

  • Level and trend. The rolling six-month R-squared against the equal-weighted basket of 19 dashboard peers was 56% at end-2024, fell to 43% at end-2025 (the ARC speculation, the BP merger talk, the buyback trim and the Q4 2025 miss were Shell-specific), then rose through 2026 to 76% at end-September and 71% at 6-Nov-26. Beta fell from about 0.88 to about 0.80 over the same period: Shell moves with the sector but less than one for one, consistent with the first set's "low beta, yield" description.
  • Which peers. Highest co-movement with BP (0.86), TotalEnergies (0.85), Eni (0.81) and Equinor (0.77): the European integrated group. Low with the Romanian and Hungarian names, Energean and BlueNord. The relevant basket is therefore the four or five European majors, and against them Shell's R-squared is likely higher than 71%.
  • Idiosyncratic return. With beta 0.83 the basket's +11.4% implies about +9.5% for Shell; the actual +17.9% leaves about +8 points of company-specific return over six months. The dated causes are visible: the Q2 beat (30-Jul-26), ARC's completion (2-Sep-26), the LNG Canada Phase 2 decision (30-Sep-26), the Q3 update (7-Oct-26) and the Q3 beat with the USD 5bn buyback (29-Oct-26). The first set noted Shell had lagged peers over twelve months (+41.5% against TotalEnergies +64% and Eni +84%); the last six months reversed part of that.
  • The multiple against peers. On the dashboard's ten-year P/E percentiles, Shell sits at 70, TotalEnergies at 50, BP 64, Eni 66, Equinor 83, Repsol 78, Galp 62; most upstream names are above 85. Shell is neither the most nor the least re-rated. All but TotalEnergies carry the dashboard's D or F valuation rating; TotalEnergies carries C. The 24-month-forward multiple's 99th-percentile reading (11.15x) is the arithmetic of forward EPS falling from USD 6.37 to about 4.8 while the price sits at a 52-week high; every major whose forward earnings normalise shows the same effect. Morgan Stanley's statement that the energy sector's 42% rally this year leaves "much already in the price" is a sector statement, and it applies to Shell in full.
  • What the company adds or subtracts. The company-specific component of the multiple is small and identifiable: a defensiveness premium (lower beta, LNG and trading, 2.7% ex-lease gearing, a sub-USD 40 dividend breakeven shared only with TotalEnergies and Eni), a discount for trading opacity (Bernstein: 20-25% of the Europeans' earnings valued on low multiples), a discount for reserve life and the post-2030 gap (Redburn's Neutral rests on it), and an option on faster dividend growth (Morgan Stanley's Overweight rests on it). The 4-Feb-27 update could move the company part by perhaps 5-10% either way; the sector part can move 20% on a ceasefire headline (the first set's 1990 analogue).

What this means for predictability and for the case.

  1. The share price is predictable relative to the sector (R-squared 71%, beta 0.83) and the sector is not predictable, because it trades as the hedge against the same geopolitical state that drives Shell's earnings. Shell's price and Shell's EPS are therefore driven by the same variable in the same direction, which removes any diversification between the earnings forecast error and the multiple forecast error.
  2. A multiple that is three-quarters industry-driven cannot be underwritten on company delivery. The model's return at 11.58x assumes the sector's regime holds for 14 months while Shell's EPS falls about 30%; the first set's trough arithmetic (8x the mid-cycle EPS, 28% below the 25-Oct price) is what the industry-driven component does when the regime ends. The guideline multiple is a sector call dressed as a stock call.
  3. Within the sector, Shell's company-specific component has been positive for six months on execution (beats, ARC, Phase 2, the buyback) and is likely to stay mildly positive into the 4-Feb-27 update. That is worth a few points of relative return, not a defence against a de-rating.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

B565th of 19

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: history shows the sector outperforms when capital discipline and credible cash returns coincide with a doubted payout and a low multiple. It underperforms when the market capitalises a peak margin, when capex surges, when the price collapses or when windfall taxes arrive. Shell fits the outperformance template well on fundamentals, but today it sits in the late, already-rewarded phase of that template.

What made the sector outperform.

  1. Payout frameworks set as a share of cash flow on low gearing, a yield the market believes. Shell's payout Brent floor is about USD 50, among the lowest in the sector.
  2. Guiding low and beating, so consensus becomes a floor. Seven of seventeen Change Forces reports in the sector call management a sandbagger or honest tracker.
  3. A volume step-change around first gas or first oil (six to twelve months).
  4. Self-help and simplification.
  5. The removal of a "transition discount" when capital swings back to barrels.
  6. Starting from a depressed multiple: EV/DACF about 5x in the late 2010s; a five-year P/E low of 5.2x.

What made it underperform.

  1. Capex above 100% of cash flow (the 2000s), which de-rated oil to 5x EV/DACF.
  2. Price collapses (2014-16, 2020). FactSet's dividend line shows Shell's DPS fell from USD 1.87 (2019) to USD 0.63 (2020); the payout was fixed and too high.
  3. Windfall taxes (2022-23).
  4. Over-allocation to the transition (the 2019-22 discount).
  5. The "record-margin trap": the October 2026 sector memos all find a record margin being capitalised as a run rate, with every path normalising from 2027.
  6. Resource shares de-rate three to six months before earnings revisions peak.

How Shell fits.

  • It fits points 1, 2, 4 and 5 of the positive template. Points 1-2 of the template were the 2023-25 re-rating: a share count down 19% in three years and TSR ahead of peers to mid-2025.
  • The flexible CFFO-based payout addresses the 2020 lesson.
  • But the stock is now at a 24-month forward P/E of 11.15x (99th percentile of five years), 12-month EPS revisions are +93% for FY2026 and +40% for FY2027, and the price is +41.5% over 12 months at its 52-week high.
  • Precedent therefore says this is the window in which shares pre-empt the earnings peak. Upside requires the market to believe the multiple can stay high on normalised earnings.
  • In a bear market for equities, the cleanest large-cap yield case in a sector tends to win (Wisdom Library). Shell qualifies as large and liquid, but not as "clean": it has many moving parts.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

D2814th of 19

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: a fall to a fair trough multiple on normalised earnings implies a share price of roughly 2,650-3,000p, 25-35% below 4,033p. A severe but plausible trough (crisis multiple on bear-case earnings) implies about 2,200-2,400p, 40-45% below.

The anchors.

  • Own valuation history. 24-month forward P/E 11.15x today, at the 99th percentile of five years. Five-year range 5.20-11.34x, median 8.03x, 10th percentile 6.38x. The dashboard's ten-year bands: 10th percentile 7.0x ("crash"), 35th 8.3x, median 9.0x.
  • Normalised EPS.
  • Consensus FY2028 USD 4.83.
  • Morgan Stanley's mid-cycle USD 24-25bn of adjusted earnings over about 5.3bn shares gives about USD 4.5-4.7.
  • A bear deck (USD 60 Brent, weak refining) gives about USD 19-20bn, or about USD 3.6-3.8 a share (my estimate).

The arithmetic, on a price of USD 53.98.

  • 8.0x × USD 4.83 = USD 38.6 (-28%).
  • 7.0x × USD 4.6 = USD 32.2 (-40%).
  • 6.4x × USD 3.7 = USD 23.7 (-56%), the extreme.

Cross-checks.

  • Sell-side bear marks:
  • JPM 3,045p (-25%);
  • Bernstein 3,100p (-23%);
  • the lowest local target 3,044.92p;
  • Jefferies' severe downside £21.90 (-46%);
  • Morgan Stanley's extreme bear 1,501p (-63%, a dividend-discount case with 2% dividend growth).
  • The 52-week low of 2,553p (-37%).
  • Redburn's EV/DACF floor of 6.0x.
  • Options imply only a 1.5% risk-neutral probability of falling below 1,697p within 12 months (Morgan Stanley).

What softens a trough. The yield: a dividend of about USD 1.6, or 3-4% at trough prices, plus a buyback of about USD 13bn a year, which is 6-8% of the market value at trough. Both tend to put a floor under it.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

D5212th of 19

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Judgement: the narrative staircase is solid and dated but not novel.

  • What is ahead. A run of near-term beats and capital-return decisions (29-Oct-26; February 2027), the restoration of Middle East volumes in 2027 and ARC synergies. A possible 2027 CMD offers the one potentially novel step, a faster dividend-growth policy.
  • What weighs on it. The narrative has already been rewarded: the price is at its 52-week high and the multiple at its five-year peak. And the 2027-30 window brings well-flagged headwinds: the fading windfall, the LNG supply wave and the post-2030 gap.

For the Consumer the narrative supports the 18-month earnings path and the capital-return assumptions. It gives no grounds to assume a re-rating unless the dividend policy changes.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–70

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the answers of the first and second sets are highly congruent on the architecture of the case, and the Q3 2026 results, which neither set had, confirmed the second set's model almost to the decimal. Where the sets differ, the differences are factual conflicts about magnitudes (the trading share of the windfall, the pace of share-count reduction, reserve life) or about the price deck, not contradictions about how the business works. The one real jar is not between the two sets but between both sets and the guideline fair multiple the model uses, which sits above every valuation anchor the first set gave.

What is congruent, and why that matters for predictability.

  1. The core architecture. Both sets describe one engine: a normal earnings base of about USD 23-26bn at USD 70-75 Brent and USD 10-12 refining, a windfall layer in 2026 that decays through 2027, highly predictable controllables (capex USD 20-22bn, distributions 40-50% of CFFO, a buyback floor of USD 3bn a quarter, structural costs at the top of USD 5-7bn) and a share count falling 4-5% a year. The first set's C-49 (durable per-share growth of 5-8% at constant prices), C-75 (price leverage on a fixed cost base), C-52 (counter-cyclical working capital) and C-67 (FY2028 consensus near mid-cycle) are the same propositions the second set quantified in P-01, P-04, P-05 and P-09. The second set's FY2028 adjusted earnings of USD 24.2bn sits inside the first set's USD 24-27bn.
  2. The Q3 2026 test. The second set forecast Q3 adjusted earnings of USD 10.8bn with Chemicals & Products at about 3.9bn; Shell reported USD 10.81bn and 3.86bn. The second set's Integrated Gas thesis (trading offsets volume loss; the oil-indexed lag holds realisations up) was borne out by a USD 3.04bn Integrated Gas result on production 21% below a year earlier. This is the strongest evidence in the files that the second set's segment build is calibrated.
  3. Cash against earnings. Both sets say cash falls less than earnings in a downturn (working capital releases, capex falls, cash taxes lag). Q3 confirmed the mechanism in the other direction: nine-month CFFO of USD 48.5bn absorbed an USD 8.3bn working-capital build and USD 8.5bn of cash taxes, and still ran well ahead of a mechanical build. The second set's FY2026 free cash flow of USD 35.1bn, which the model summary flagged against the model's 28.5bn, is the figure consistent with the actuals (preface).
  4. The direction of the risk. The first set's Risks group (de-escalation as the dominant risk, 30-40% within 18 months in sharp form) and the second set's scenario table (fast reopening 35%) are the same view. Both say distributions survive a de-escalation and the share price does not.
  5. Management and disclosure. Both sets rate management's delivery on controllables as near-perfect and its disclosure as strong on frameworks and weak on trading; the Q3 beat of 5% after an update note that had flagged the USD 42 margin is exactly the pattern the first set's C-73 described (update notes reduce the surprise to single digits).

Where the sets differ, and how I resolve each.

  1. The size of the trading windfall. The first set (C-08, C-41) credited Shell with about USD 3bn of incremental trading profit in Q1 2026; the second set (P-04) showed the Morgan Stanley figure referred to the European majors together and put Shell's exceptional layer at USD 1-1.5bn a quarter, with more of the surplus in refining margin and price. Q3 settles this in the second set's favour: Products trading was lower than in Q2 and Integrated Gas trading was flat, yet Chemicals & Products earnings rose by a third, which only the USD 42 refining margin explains. The resolution matters for predictability: refining margins are replicable from forward curves (Morgan Stanley models Shell's indicative margin from the underlying components), so the larger part of the windfall is more forecastable than trading would be.
  2. The pace of share-count reduction. First set 3.5-4.5% a year; second set about 5%. The Q3 buyback of USD 5bn supports the second set and a little more (I-01).
  3. Marketing's pricing power. The first set saw none (premium fuels at equal gross margin); the second set, with Bernstein's data, saw a small real premium (26% of mobility gross margin from 13.5% of volume). Q3 Marketing of USD 1.16bn with lower lubricant margins is consistent with the second set's USD 5.2bn for FY2026. Immaterial to the case.
  4. Cost savings reaching earnings. The first set expected USD 0.5-1.0bn more by 2028; the second set about 0.3-0.6bn. The nine-month opex of USD 26.41bn against 26.12bn supports the second set: the nominal cost base is not falling.
  5. Reserve life. 7.4-7.8 years (first set, several sources) against 9.5 years (Redburn, October 2026). Unresolved; see the preface.
  6. Process safety. The first set cited the rise to 90 Tier 1 and 2 events in 2024 as early evidence that cost cuts were consuming reliability; the 2025 figure of 62 reverses it. I downgrade that concern from "evidence" to "watch item".

The one real jar: the fair multiple. The first set's C-50 calls Shell a harvest-and-return model deserving a yield multiple of about 7-10x P/E; C-83 measures today's 24-month-forward P/E (11.15x) at the 99th percentile of five years; C-84 places Shell in the late, already-rewarded phase of the sector template. The model's return is calculated at a guideline fair multiple of 11.58x, at the dashboard's ten-year 90th-percentile "mania" band. Both earlier sets would regard 11.58x as a peak multiple that can hold only if the market keeps treating energy as its hedge. This is not an inconsistency inside the research; it is the point at which the memo Analyst's guideline and the research disagree, and the Consumer should resolve it openly in topics #11 and #12 rather than carry both.

Smaller jars inside the sets, for completeness. The first set's C-13 and C-19 expected a step-up in distributions as a near-term option; the second set modelled buybacks slightly above the floor; Q3 delivered the step-up. The first set's C-22 expected the UK budget to be a possible positive; it was neutral. The second set's assumption that the Brazilian levy lapses in 2027 is supported by the court injunction and the measure's expiry, but it remains a USD 0.5-1bn a year risk at high prices.

What this means for predictability. Congruence is high where it counts: the mechanism that turns the deck into earnings, cash and per-share figures is the same in both sets and was validated by the first post-forecast print. The residual conflicts are small and factual. A memo built on these answers will not contradict itself; it will be exposed to one variable, and both sets say which.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

–45

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: the sell-side narrative on Shell is comprehensive on the dynamics that favour the case and incomplete on the dynamics that weigh on it, and the files contain no evidence of investor commentary (Twitter or similar) at all, so the second half of the question can be answered only from positioning data. Well known and in the numbers: that 2026 trading and refining are not repeatable (every house normalises them in 2027), the LNG oversupply from 2027-28, Shell's Hormuz exposure and its trading offset, the post-2030 liquids gap, the dividend reset as the re-rating catalyst, and the reported-against-adjusted earnings noise. Known to specialists but not in headline numbers: the three-to-six-month LNG lag, the working-capital release, the cash-tax lag, the buyback's dependence on the share price. Known to one or two houses or to none: the carbon-cost trajectory (only Shell's Annual Report and Carbon Tracker), the margin per barrel of ARC's volumes against the portfolio (no published comparison; the "volumes up 20%, earnings down" framing appears nowhere), the gap between Shell's reported tax rate and the flat rates in consensus models (acknowledged by Redburn, flagged by Bernstein), and the fact that Shell's Qatari equity LNG train is undamaged, so its volume loss is transit rather than repair. The items the narrative misses are mostly mildly negative for the case; the one it misses that is positive (a faster Qatari volume return) is small. The gap between our assessment and the narrative is therefore slightly unfavourable: the market knows the good news.

The coverage check, and what the files cannot answer. The AlphaSense narrative report credits views to Morgan Stanley, Jefferies, Bernstein and Rothschild & Co Redburn from their own documents, with supporting references to Deutsche Bank, DNB Carnegie, ING, Incred, Carbon Tracker and the company. It claims this is more than 80% of active analysts but names no other covering house and shows no document from Barclays (the USD 7.71 high), UBS, Goldman Sachs, JPMorgan, HSBC, Citi, BNP Paribas, Berenberg, Kepler or Santander, all of which FactSet or the first set's files show as covering Shell. The judgements below on "how widely" are therefore judgements about four to six houses. On investor commentary the report offers one expert call from June 2022; there is nothing from Twitter, fund letters or conference transcripts. I substitute the first set's positioning data (energy at the 69th percentile, systematic commodity allocations at the 91st, the momentum tilt turned Underweight on 23-Oct-26), the dashboard's buy share (39%) and the fact that the shares trade at the mean target, and I raise a research request to fill the gap.

Dynamic by dynamic.

Dynamic (our assessment) Who discusses it, since when, with what view How well known In the numbers?
2026 trading and refining are a windfall that halves in 2027 and is gone by 2028 Morgan Stanley (above-mid-cycle USD 38bn in 2026 to 31bn in 2027; refining replicated from forwards), Redburn (average trading from Q4 2026, since October 2026), Jefferies (50% capture from 2027, since August 2026), Bernstein (20-25% of the Europeans' earnings valued on low multiples because trading is seen as non-recurring, since January 2025) Very well known Yes, though Morgan Stanley's USD 25 refining margin for 2027 keeps part of the windfall in its 2027 number
Working capital releases and cash taxes lag as prices fall Morgan Stanley (Q1 build in nine of the last ten years, seasonality), Jefferies (cash-tax lag as the primary downside risk to cash, since October 2026), Bernstein (Q4 2025 tax and working capital), Redburn (Q2 release) Known to the specialists; not a headline Partly: Morgan Stanley's USD 18.5bn buyback capacity for 2027 implies it; no house publishes the release
ARC's margin per barrel against Shell's portfolio Morgan Stanley (USD 1.5bn of free cash flow, 4x price to cash flow), Redburn (5.5x EV/EBITDA, 7.5% accretion, 3.3% without synergies), Jefferies (USD 8.20/boe of 2P) The deal metrics are known; the per-barrel dilution of realised earnings is not discussed by anyone No: a 13% volume addition is not translated into its roughly 3-4% earnings addition in any note

Carbon compliance cost from about USD 1bn to 4bn by 2035 Shell's Annual Report; Carbon Tracker Effectively unknown in the sell-side narrative No
The three-to-six-month lag of oil-indexed LNG behind Brent Jefferies (80% Brent-linked by exposure), TotalEnergies' own guidance, Deutsche Bank historically, a Thai broker; Shell's own Q1 2026 release describes the three-month Japan Crude Cocktail lag Well known mechanically; its effect on Q1 2027 is not emphasised Partly
Qatari volumes and prices linked through the Strait (the natural hedge) Redburn ("Strait Talking", April 2026: Shell the most exposed major), Morgan Stanley (10.1% volume markdown; the trading offset), Jefferies (trading more than offsets) Well known as exposure and as offset; the netting (long the disruption by four to five times the volume exposure) is not published Partly
Post-2030 liquids gap and reserve life Redburn (200 kb/d, about USD 20bn to fill, its reason for Neutral, since May 2026), Morgan Stanley (portfolio duration), DNB Carnegie (seven-year reserve lives sector-wide) Very well known; the signature bear point Yes, in Redburn's 2% terminal decline
Faster dividend growth or a higher payout as gearing falls Morgan Stanley (10% a year to 2030, the Overweight thesis, since 2025), Redburn (6% with Q4 results), Jefferies (mix shift to dividends) Very well known; the signature bull point Partly: consensus DPS growth about 5.5%
Inventory holding effects and impairments in reported earnings All houses work on adjusted earnings; Shell pre-announced the USD 6.4bn Q4 pension item; Morgan Stanley flags a chemicals write-down against USD 24bn of book value Known mechanically Not relevant to adjusted consensus; the FY2027 reported shortfall of about USD 4bn is not discussed
Shell's Qatari equity LNG train is undamaged; the loss is transit Implicit in Incred's and Fitch's identification of the damaged trains as ExxonMobil-partnered; no house states it for Shell Not discussed No
The LNG oversupply of 2027-30, now pushed to 2028-30 by the Qatari damage Morgan Stanley, Redburn, Jefferies, Bernstein, Incred, the IEA Universal Yes
Windfall-tax escalation Morgan Stanley (5-10% of free cash flow in 2022-23; 1-Apr-26), Jefferies and Bird & Bird on the UK budget, the EU debate in the ECB report and the European Parliament Known as a risk; not in any base case No
Rotation out of energy if the conflict ends; energy as a crowded hedge Morgan Stanley ("much already in the price"; "longer disruption priced out", 5-Nov-26), the first set's positioning digests Known In the multiple, not the estimates

Peers and the business model. The same houses apply the same frame to TotalEnergies (quality premium, little room to re-rate), BP (deleveraging done, buybacks to restart), Eni (satellites, raised buyback) and Equinor (downgraded on a 2.7% 2028 free-cash-flow yield). Cheniere is the contracted, Henry Hub-linked counter-example; Vitol's earnings path (USD 15.1bn to 4.2bn) is cited as the trading-reversion benchmark. The business-model view that European majors deserve a trading discount against US majors is Bernstein's and Redburn's and is in the sector's relative multiple.

What investors are and are not focused on, from the evidence available. The buy share of 39% against 58% at TotalEnergies and 52% at BP, the shares at the mean target, and the dashboard's valuation D say that investors who own Shell own it as the sector's defensive hedge with a dividend option, not as a growth or earnings-momentum story. The positioning data say the sector trade is crowded in systematic hands and that the momentum signal turned down on 23-Oct-26. Nothing in the files tells us what the owning investors think about ARC's margin, the carbon cost or the tax rate; the absence of discussion in the sell-side suggests they are not thinking about them.

What this means for the case. The narrative has the right shape and prices the two large known items (the windfall fades; the dividend may be reset). The unknown items are small individually (about USD 0.04 of EPS a year for carbon; a few points of accretion for ARC; a point or two of tax rate), and three of the four are negative. The one positive unknown, that Shell's Qatari volumes need the Strait and not a three-year repair, is worth perhaps USD 0.5bn a year of earlier earnings. There is no large, well-founded positive that the market has missed, and that is the honest reading for the memo's #7 and #10: the stock is in the "reinforcing" phase of its narrative, and the next phase, when the Strait reopens, is "stalling" on earnings with the dividend reset as the only novel support.

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 26 October 2026, 02:51 to 03:15 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Shell plc (SHEL-GB): answers to the first set of questions (written 2026-10-25)

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Preface for the Consumer (orientation only; not an answer to a question)

Five judgements carry most of the weight in what follows. Each is argued in full in the answers named in brackets.

  1. 2026 is a windfall year sitting on a normal earnings engine; model the two separately. Adjusted earnings were USD 6.915bn in Q1 2026 and USD 9.836bn in Q2 2026 (H1 USD 16.75bn), against USD 18.5bn for the whole of 2025 at roughly USD 69 Brent. FactSet consensus for FY2026 adjusted net income is USD 36.1bn (EPS USD 6.37, +102%), which implies about USD 19.4bn in H2. The windfall has three sources, all geopolitical: crude above USD 100 (Brent USD 111 in the week to 23-Oct-26), refining margins (Shell's own Q3 update note, 7-Oct-26, gave an indicative refining margin of USD 42/bbl against about USD 32 expected), and trading and optimisation of LNG and product volatility. Morgan Stanley's mid-cycle reference is USD 24-25bn of adjusted earnings a year at USD 70 Brent and USD 10/bbl refining margins. Consensus for FY2028 (USD 25.7bn) and FY2029 (USD 24.5bn, only 6 brokers) already sits at that normalised level. Never extrapolate 2026 (C-06, C-49, C-67).
  2. Volumes rise in 2027 while prices fall. Q2 2026 production was depressed in Integrated Gas (631 kboe/d against 909 in Q1; Qatar fell from 263 to 78 kboe/d) while Upstream set a record (1,824 kboe/d). ARC Resources (completed 2-Sep-26) adds about 370 kboe/d; the Qatar restart and Pearl GTL Train 2 (Q1 2027) restore most of the lost Qatari volume; LNG Canada runs at full capacity. Redburn puts 2027 group production at 3.4 mboe/d against about 2.7-2.8 mboe/d in 2025. But ARC's barrels are low-margin Canadian gas and condensate: about USD 1.5bn of annual free cash flow on about 370 kboe/d, or roughly USD 11 per boe. A volume rise of about 20% therefore lifts earnings far less than 20% (C-06, C-16, C-18).
  3. The per-share engine is slowing. Shares fell from 7,857m (2019) to 5,697m (2025), down 27%, or about 6.5% a year in 2022-25. ARC issued 228m new shares, which reverses about one year of buybacks, and at USD 54 a share the same USD 13-14bn a year buys only about 4.3-4.5% of the company. Consensus implies about 5.32bn shares in 2028 (USD 25.7bn net income over USD 4.83 EPS), a reduction of about 3.5% a year. Model per-share accretion at 3.5-4.5% a year, not the historical 6.5% (C-19, C-47, C-64).
  4. Distributions are the best-protected line in the model. The policy is 40-50% of cash flow from operations (CFFO), with buybacks of at least USD 3bn a quarter that management says hold "down to USD 50 Brent". Gearing was 18.7% at Q2 2026, including about USD 29.7bn of leases; net debt excluding leases was about USD 12.1bn. There is also a counter-cyclical working-capital cushion: the USD 11.2bn Q1 2026 build reverses as prices fall. 2026 distributions are running at only about 36% of CFFO (Leadership Foundations report), so there is room under the formula (C-19, C-52, C-76).
  5. Valuation leaves little cushion. The 24-month forward P/E is 11.15x, the 99th percentile of its five-year history (median 8.03x) and close to the dashboard's ten-year 90th-percentile "mania" band of 11.60x. The price (USD 53.98) sits above the mean target (USD 53.03), and the pinned TSR/Valuation rating is D. Over 12 months consensus EPS rose 93% for FY2026 and 40% for FY2027 but only 2.9% for FY2028: the market treats the windfall as transient. That is a rational view and it is already in the price. The debate is how large and how long the windfall is, and whether the multiple holds as it fades (C-83, C-22, C-74).

Data conventions and conflicts the Consumer must carry.

  • Money is in USD; EPS is on the brokers' adjusted basis unless stated. Never compute growth from FactSet's history lines to consensus; use the block's like-for-like line (FactSet quirks digest).
  • FactSet debt and Shell's own net debt both include leases. FactSet free cash flow excludes lease payments (USD 1.34bn in Q1 2026 alone, roughly USD 5bn+ a year), so FactSet and consensus free cash flow overstate distributable free cash flow by about that amount.
  • Shell's "cash capex" includes acquisitions. The 2026 guidance of USD 24-26bn includes about USD 4bn related to ARC, including the USD 3.3bn cash consideration. FactSet's FY2026 capex consensus of USD 20.9bn is on a fixed-asset basis. The two are consistent.
  • Conflicts carried rather than reconciled:
  • Proved reserve life: 7.4 years in some sources, 7.8 years in others.
  • Post-2030 liquids gap: 200 kb/d or 350 kb/d.
  • ARC price: equity USD 13.6bn at announcement and USD 13.9bn at completion, EV USD 16.4-16.5bn. The Leadership Foundations report's "USD 22bn" is inconsistent; disregard it.
  • Share of the cost savings that came from divestments: "nearly half" (Risks report) against 57% or "60%+" from non-portfolio efficiency (Earnings Review and Business Foundations).
  • Q1 2026 Integrated Gas adjusted earnings: USD 2.115bn (Q1 Earnings Review) against USD 1.819bn (the Q2 Earnings Review's table). Use the later table.
  • 2025 ROACE: 9.4% adjusted against 9.8%.
  • Method caution. The Risks reports for Shell, TotalEnergies, Equinor, Eni and BP all conclude that the "False Friend" profile wins the analysis-of-competing-hypotheses matrix. A verdict every peer receives is a property of the method (Wisdom Library: "The Vaguest Explanation Wins A Fewest-Contradictions Contest"), not evidence about Shell. Use its components (reserve life, organic growth, dependence on trading) on their own merits.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Shell plc (SHEL-GB), the London-listed integrated energy major, reporting five segments: Integrated Gas (LNG liquefaction, gas production, gas-to-liquids and LNG trading), Upstream (oil and gas exploration and production, mainly deepwater), Marketing (retail mobility, lubricants and business fuels), Chemicals & Products (refining, chemicals and oil products trading) and Renewables & Energy Solutions (power, hydrogen and carbon capture).

Request: T-P1

Peers: TotalEnergies (closest European integrated peer with the lowest supermajor unit costs and a USD 12.5bn 2026-2030 savings programme), BP (structural cost programme of USD 6.5-7.5bn by end-2027 with documented inflation offsets), ExxonMobil (long-running structural cost-savings programme with an additional USD 7bn targeted by 2030), Chevron (USD 3bn structural cost reduction against a 2024 baseline, delivered early), Eni (downstream and chemicals restructuring at Versalis with permanent cracker closures), Equinor (Norwegian shelf standardisation and subsea tie-back cost model), QatarEnergy (private; benchmark for LNG liquefaction operating costs), Vitol and Trafigura (private; cost structure of independent trading houses as a benchmark for Shell's trading and supply organisation). Inputs: What share of Shell's operating expenses (total operating expenses of USD 35.7bn in 2025 per the Annual Report 2025) is fixed and what share varies with production, refinery utilisation and traded volumes, segment by segment? What is the composition of the USD 5.8bn of cumulative structural cost reductions against 2022 reported with the Q2 2026 results (30 July 2026): how much came from divestments versus operating, maintenance and corporate-centre efficiency, how much has been offset by inflation, and how much is permanent? How much further reduction is realistically available to the top of the USD 5-7bn by 2028 target and beyond, from chemicals capacity closures, the sale of the US chemicals business, the corporate centre, and digital and AI programmes? Is there evidence that cost-cutting is damaging capability: maintenance deferral, reliability and plant availability, staff turnover (Shell reports 11% voluntary turnover in 2025 against 7% in 2024) and headcount (96,000 to 85,000)? What dated precedents exist at oil majors where deep cost cuts were followed by reliability or project failures? How did Shell's and peers' segment earnings and margins move per unit of commodity price in past down-cycles (2014-2016, 2020) and up-cycles (2021-2022, 2026), and how did realised moves compare with published sensitivities? What are oilfield-service, rig, subsea EPCI, gas-turbine and LNG equipment inflation trends for 2026-2028, and what do they imply for physical activity bought by Shell's USD 20-22bn annual capex guidance for 2027-2028? How have peers' announced cost programmes compared with delivery, in amount and time taken? How do the Integrated Gas and trading cost bases behave when volumes fall, for example at Pearl GTL during the 2026 outage? Focus: How much of Shell's change in earnings over 2027-2030 will come from cost actions it controls rather than from commodity prices it does not, and how much further structural cost reduction is achievable without eroding reliability, project delivery and technical capability.

Request: T-P2

Peers: TotalEnergies (large LNG portfolio with Brent-indexed contracts and integrated refining; Q2 2026 gas trading underperformance gives a contrast), BP (integrated trading organisation and refining margin capture), ExxonMobil and Chevron (US majors with larger refining integration and LNG positions), Equinor (deliberately unhedged European spot gas seller), Eni (gas and LNG portfolio optimisation), QatarEnergy (private; the largest LNG supplier and a contract-pricing benchmark), Cheniere and Venture Global (US LNG developers with Henry Hub-linked and spot-selling models), Vitol and Trafigura (private; trading houses competing for LNG and products optimisation margins). Operating conditions: a geopolitical supply shock with the Strait of Hormuz closed and Qatari LNG under force majeure (2026); an LNG buyer's market as new US and Qatari liquefaction capacity arrives (2027-2030); an oil price collapse or OPEC+ market-share war (as in 2014-2016 and 2020); exceptionally high refining margins from product-export disruption and their normalisation; weak European industrial demand and price-driven demand destruction in Asian LNG; high inflation with rising interest rates. Inputs: LNG contract structures: what share of Shell's LNG sales is on long-term oil-indexed contracts (Shell indicates roughly 80% contracted to 2029, averaging about 15 years), typical slopes, the three-month lag to oil, price-review and reopener clauses, and dated evidence from 2015-2016 and 2020 of buyers renegotiating slopes. How have pricing terms on new LNG contracts signed in 2025-2026 compared with earlier contracts? Trading and optimisation: how did Shell's and peers' trading earnings behave in volatile years (2022, 2026) against calm years (2023, 2024), and is any volatility premium structural or cyclical as LNG markets become more liquid? Refining margin capture: Shell's reported 55% capture in H1 2026 (Jefferies) against peers, and how capture behaved when indicator margins normalised in the past. Marketing: the premium fuels share of volume and margin, lubricant pricing and pass-through (including base-oil supply after the Pearl GTL outage), and branded against unbranded retail margins. Chemicals price-cost spreads in downturns. Windfall taxes as a cap on realised upstream price capture: the UK Energy Profits Levy (78% marginal rate, extended to March 2030) and its successor levy, Brazil's crude export levy, and EU windfall-tax proposals. Focus: Whether Shell's ability to earn above benchmark prices, through LNG contract structure, trading optimisation and refining capture, persists when volatility falls and LNG becomes a buyer's market, and how much of its 2026 outperformance analysts and the company regard as repeatable.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Shell's earnings, cash flow and distributions are shaped by several self-reinforcing and self-correcting dynamics whose net effect will decide the shape of the 2027-2030 forecasts: buyback compounding, working-capital release when prices fall, volatility feeding trading earnings, high prices inviting windfall taxes and demand destruction, and reserve depletion forcing acquisitions. Peers: TotalEnergies (organic growth model and buybacks at about 2.5% of shares a year), BP (deleveraging and disposal-funded returns), ExxonMobil (long record of programmatic buybacks across cycles), Chevron (buybacks scaled to a Brent range), Equinor (Norwegian tax-lag cash dynamics), Eni (asset-sale-funded deleveraging through satellites), Vitol (private; trading earnings across volatility regimes). Inputs: Buyback compounding: Shell has cut its share count about 19% over three years with USD 43bn of buybacks (Jefferies). What happens to per-share accretion as the share price rises, and what precedents exist of programmatic buybacks continued at peak valuations? Working capital as a counter-cyclical cash buffer: Shell's USD 11.2bn outflow in Q1 2026 and USD 3.4bn inflow in Q2 2026, and dated releases in past price declines (2014-2015, 2020) at Shell and peers. Volatility feeding trading earnings, and whether growing LNG market liquidity and new trading entrants (US developers, national oil companies, trading houses) erode it. High prices leading to windfall taxes (UK levy history 2022-2030; the EU solidarity contribution 2022-2023; current EU proposals) and to demand destruction (global LNG demand down 5.4% year on year in H1 2026 per Bernstein), which then lower prices and earnings. Capital discipline across the majors leading to scarce oilfield-service capacity, service inflation and higher project costs. Reserve depletion (proved reserve life of 7.8 years per Redburn) leading to reliance on acquisitions, equity issuance (228m shares issued for ARC Resources in 2026) and dilution of buyback accretion. Distribution policies set as a share of cash flow from operations, and their behaviour across cycles. Focus: Which of these loops will dominate Shell's financial line items over 2027-2030, in particular whether working-capital release and buybacks cushion a commodity downturn, and whether the trading volatility premium decays as markets calm.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: The bridge from Shell's 2026 earnings to 2027-2029 depends on several specific, separately forecastable inputs (price decks, volumes, trading, tax, share count) on which brokers' published estimates diverge widely. Peers: TotalEnergies, BP, ExxonMobil, Chevron, Eni and Equinor (integrated peers whose consensus bridges face the same inputs), with ARC Resources (acquired by Shell in 2026; its last standalone disclosures as a guide to the acquired volumes, costs and gas price exposure). Inputs: Production volumes for 2026-2029: the recovery of Integrated Gas (Shell reported Qatar volumes falling from 263 kboe/d to 78 kboe/d in Q2 2026); the restart of Pearl GTL Train 2 (targeted for Q1 2027); ARC Resources' contribution (about 370 kboe/d per Shell's completion release of 2 September 2026) and its margin per boe against Shell's portfolio; the LNG Canada ramp and the Phase 2 schedule (final investment decision 30 September 2026); and the effect of divestments (Na Kika, Nigerian gas, South African retail). Realised price linkages: oil-indexed LNG slopes and lags; exposure to Canadian AECO gas prices; Shell's negative Henry Hub sensitivity. The refining margin path and Shell's capture rate. The trading and optimisation contribution in normal against exceptional years. The chemicals earnings trajectory after the restructuring and the planned US chemicals sale. The tax rate: UK Energy Profits Levy to March 2030 and its successor, Brazil's export levy, the group adjusted effective tax rate, and carbon compliance costs (Shell: USD 398m in 2025, rising toward about USD 1bn in 2026 and about USD 4bn by 2035, Annual Report 2025). Net interest including lease interest (lease liabilities of about USD 29.7bn at Q2 2026). The share of profit from joint ventures and associates, including the Adura UK joint venture. The frequency of identified items and impairments. The share count: buybacks of at least USD 3bn a quarter, 228m shares issued for ARC, the dividend policy of about 4% annual growth against the 5% increase declared with Q1 2026 results. Capex of USD 20-22bn a year for 2027-2028 and the depreciation trajectory. Broker estimate dispersion: FactSet individual broker FY2027 EPS estimates ranged from USD 3.95 to USD 7.71 as at 25 October 2026. Focus: Reconcile the brokers' wide range of 2027-2028 earnings-per-share estimates input by input, and identify which inputs (price deck, volumes, trading, tax, share count) explain most of the dispersion and which are most likely to move materially either way.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 30 October 2026, 10:51 to 11:14 UK, Opus, richie login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Five judgements carry most of the weight.

  1. The forecast is a commodity-deck call, not a company call. From FY2026 to FY2028 my adjusted net income falls by about USD 13bn (37.5 to 24.2). The deck alone (Brent, refining, gas) takes away about USD 15bn and the fading of exceptional trading about USD 2bn; volumes and self-help give back only about USD 3.3bn. Every line below EBITDA is predictable; EBITDA itself is not.
  2. Volumes rise about 15-20% in 2027 while earnings fall. The new barrels (ARC, the Qatar and Pearl restoration) are worth about USD 2.0bn of earnings in FY2027 and about USD 2.6bn a year at full rate, against about USD 9bn lost to the deck from FY2026 to FY2027. Because the Qatar volumes return only when the Strait of Hormuz reopens, which is also what collapses the price, Shell's price and volume move against each other: a partial natural hedge worth roughly one-fifth of the price effect.
  3. Per-share mechanics cushion the fall more than the first set assumed. At USD 13.5-14.5bn of buybacks a year and a share price near USD 52, the share count falls about 5% a year (the first set said 3.5-4.5%). EPS therefore falls 30% from FY2026 to FY2028 while net income falls 35%, and EPS rises again from FY2029 on a flat deck.
  4. Cash falls far less than earnings in 2027. The working-capital build of 2026 (about USD 8.4bn) reverses as prices fall, so FY2027 free cash flow after leases is only 5% below FY2026 while net income falls 23%. Distributions are the safest line in the model.
  5. The FY2027 cone is three-peaked, and consensus is a blend of the peaks. A fast Hormuz reopening gives about USD 23bn of net income (EPS about 4.2); my gradual base about USD 29bn (EPS 5.24); a disruption lasting through 2027 about USD 39bn after windfall taxes (EPS about 7.0). I weight them 35%, 45% and 20%; the weighted mean (EPS about 5.23) sits on consensus (5.28), but almost no single path ends near consensus. The broker range of USD 3.95 to 7.71 is these three states, not disagreement about Shell.

Where I disagree with the first set, and why (each is argued in the answer named).

  • Tax rate (P-08). The first set says hold the tax rate at 41-45%. That is the reported rate on IFRS pre-tax profit. On the adjusted, joint-venture-inclusive basis the model uses (joint-venture results arrive post-tax inside EBITDA; minority interests are folded in), the rate that reproduces Shell's adjusted earnings is 35-36.5%. FY2025 check: USD 56.6bn adjusted EBITDA, less USD 24.2bn D&A, less USD 3.7bn net interest, gives USD 28.7bn; USD 18.5bn of adjusted earnings implies 35.5%. Entering 41-45% in the model would understate EPS by about 10%.
  • Trading windfall size (P-04). The first set (C-08) attributes Morgan Stanley's USD 3bn of incremental Q1 2026 trading profit to Shell. The AlphaSense EPS-bridge report (Morgan Stanley, 12-May-26) attributes USD 3bn to the European majors together. I take Shell's share as about USD 1-1.5bn a quarter at the peak, so more of 2026's surplus is refining margin and price, which are more visible and more mean-reverting.
  • Premium fuels (P-04). The first set (C-17, C-45) says premium fuels earn no margin premium. Bernstein (29-Nov-25) shows premium fuels at 13.5% of 2024 volume but 26% of the USD 7.6bn gross mobility margin, and Shell's 2024 Marketing EBITDA at USD 8.90/bbl against USD 5.52 at BP and USD 5.00 at TotalEnergies. Marketing has real, if small, pricing power; I hold it at USD 5.2-5.5bn a year.
  • Cost savings reaching earnings (P-03). The first set expects USD 0.5-1.0bn above the 2026 run-rate by 2028. "Other opex changes, including inflation" added USD 1.1bn in H1 2026 alone, against USD 0.7bn over 2022-25, and carbon compliance rises from about USD 1bn (2026) by about USD 0.3bn a year. I expect only USD 0.3-0.6bn post-tax net.
  • FY2028 (P-10). The first set calls FY2028 consensus credible to slightly conservative. My FY2028 net income is 6% below consensus (USD 24.2bn against 25.7bn) because I use USD 72 Brent and USD 12 refining, below the USD 75 base that brokers such as Redburn use; my EPS is only 5% below because my share count is lower.

Basis cautions for the model. EBITDA margins in this set are on Shell's adjusted basis divided by FactSet fundamentals revenue; they are not comparable with FactSet's operating EBITDA history (17.0% in FY2025, 22.7% in Q2 2026), which excludes about USD 12-13bn of joint-venture and other income. Capital spending is Shell's organic cash capex (excluding the ARC consideration); free cash flow is after lease payments and before acquisitions and disposals. Q3 2026 results are due today (29-Oct-26) and are not in the files; my Q3 estimate is USD 10.8bn of adjusted earnings.

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Basis of every input (read first). Revenue growth is applied to FactSet's fundamentals revenue (FY2025 USD 257,331m; Q3 2025 USD 68,153m; Q4 2025 USD 64,072m; Q1-Q4 2026 bases as in the financials block). The resulting revenue is USD 357.4bn (FY2026), 296.0bn, 268.5bn, 262.0bn and 262.5bn; quarterly revenue USD 96.0bn (Q3 2026), 97.0bn, 82.0bn, 75.5bn, 70.5bn and 68.0bn. The EBITDA margin is Shell's adjusted EBITDA (current cost of supplies, excluding identified items, with the share of joint-venture and associate results included, as consensus EBITDA is) divided by that revenue. It is not comparable with FactSet's operating EBITDA history (17.0% in FY2025), which excludes about USD 12-13bn a year of joint-venture and other income. If the model applies growth to the estimates-basis revenue instead (FY2025 USD 266,886m), EBITDA and EPS would come out about 3.7% and 5% higher than intended. The tax rate is the rate on the model's pre-tax profit (EBITDA less D&A plus net financial items) that reproduces Shell's adjusted earnings, with minority interests (about USD 0.3-0.4bn a year) folded in. It is lower than the reported 41-45% because joint-venture results enter EBITDA after their own tax.

Integrated Gas holds up in FY2027 because oil-indexed LNG prices lag Brent by three to six months, ARC is consolidated for the full year and Qatar and Pearl volumes return (about 70% on average in 2027, fully in 2028); it falls in FY2028 as the lag unwinds and the trading premium fades. Upstream falls with Brent (about USD 2.4-2.6bn per USD 10). Chemicals & Products carries most of the decline: the indicative refining margin falls from about USD 28/bbl (FY2026; USD 42 in Q3) to USD 16.5 and then 12, the exceptional trading layer fades, and chemicals stay near breakeven until the US business is sold (assumed early 2028). Trading and optimisation was 126% of Products adjusted earnings in 2025. FactSet's segment consensus lines are inconsistent (Marketing consensus of USD 191.6bn for the next year exceeds any history) and should not be used.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Price deck. Brent about USD 95 (FY2026; Q3 about 100, Q4 about 105), 78 (FY2027; quarters 88, 80, 74, 72), 72, 70, 70. Indicative refining margin about USD 28, 16.5, 12, 10.5, 10. TTF about USD 17, 12, 10, 9, 8.5/mmbtu. Henry Hub about USD 4 throughout. The deck assumes a gradual reopening of the Strait of Hormuz through H1 2027.
  • Sensitivities used (annual, post-tax earnings). About USD 330m per USD 1 of Brent, of which about USD 110m in Integrated Gas with a three-to-six-month lag; USD 320m per USD 1/bbl of refining margin; about USD 300m per USD 1/mmbtu of TTF or JKM; minus USD 500m of cash flow per USD 1 of Henry Hub.
  • Volumes. Group production about 2.65 mboe/d (FY2026), 3.05, 3.2, 3.2, 3.2. ARC adds about 370 kboe/d from 2-Sep-26 (low margin: about USD 1.5bn of free cash flow a year plus USD 250m of synergies by about September 2027). Qatar is restored from 78 toward 263 kboe/d through 2027, and Pearl Train 2 returns in Q1 2027. LNG sales are about 75, 78, 81, 85 and 89 Mt. Refinery utilisation is 93-95% after FY2026. Marketing volumes drift from 2.55-2.65 mb/d to about 2.45 mb/d.
  • Trading and optimisation. A baseline of about USD 7bn of net operating profit a year in every year. The exceptional 2026 layer (about USD 3-5bn) halves in 2027 and is gone by 2028.
  • Costs. Structural savings reach about USD 7bn against 2022 by 2028 (top of target), offset by inflation; carbon compliance about USD 1.0bn (2026) rising about USD 0.3bn a year. Net cost-and-exit benefit to earnings is close to zero by 2029-30.
  • D&A. Rises with ARC's stepped-up assets and the Whale and Mero ramps, less divested assets.
  • Net financial items. Net interest including about USD 1.4-1.5bn of lease interest; falls as net debt including leases goes from about USD 45bn (end-2025) to about USD 26bn (end-2028).
  • Tax. 35.0% in FY2026, when lower-taxed refining and trading are an unusually large share of profit; 36.0-36.5% after, as Upstream and Integrated Gas regain weight. The Brazil export levy (USD 242m in Q2 2026; suspended by a reversible court ruling) is assumed to lapse in 2027.
  • Dividend. 4 x USD 0.3906 for FY2026 (declared basis), then +5% a year with each year's Q1 results. FactSet's dividend line is on a payment basis and lags by a quarter.
  • Share count. +228m for ARC in FY2026. Buybacks of USD 13.5bn (FY2026), 14.5bn, 14.0bn, 13.5bn and 13.5bn at an assumed USD 52-53 a share retire about 295m, 274m, 269m, 260m and 260m shares. Average diluted shares are about 5,645m, 5,523m, 5,252m, 4,987m and 4,727m. Total distributions are 37-45% of CFFO, inside the 40-50% policy on a through-cycle basis.
  • Working capital. The percentages are chosen to reproduce the cash flows, not as economic levels. FactSet's year-end (Q4) readings are structurally negative (-USD 6.2bn at end-2024, -USD 5.5bn at end-2025, against positive readings in every other quarter). The inputs give a FY2026 build of about USD 8.4bn (H1 actual: -11.2bn, then +3.4bn), a FY2027 release of about USD 6.7bn, a FY2028 release of about USD 2.1bn and roughly nil after.
  • Capital spending. Shell's organic cash capex (excluding the ARC consideration): about USD 20.5bn in FY2026 (H1 USD 8.4bn) and within the USD 20-22bn guidance for 2027-28, drifting to USD 22bn by 2030 with inflation and LNG Canada Phase 2. FactSet's fixed-asset capex runs about USD 1.5-2bn below Shell's cash capex (joint-venture investments and other items).
  • Leases. Lease principal repayments of about USD 5.2-5.5bn a year (USD 1.34bn in Q1 2026), slightly lower as freight normalises.
  • Acquisitions less disposals. FY2026: ARC cash consideration -USD 3.3bn, Bay du Nord 30% interest about -USD 0.5bn and the Raizen capital injection -USD 0.7bn, against disposals of about +USD 3.1bn (Jiffy Lube about 1.3, Na Kika and Coulomb 0.84, Nigerian gas, European onshore renewables). FY2027: South African retail (USD 1bn EV) and other disposals of about USD 2.5bn, less about USD 1bn of bolt-ons. FY2028: US chemicals sale (assumed about USD 5.5bn against press reports of "up to USD 8bn") and USD 1.5bn of other disposals, less USD 3bn of bolt-ons. FY2029-30: net bolt-on resource acquisitions of about USD 2bn a year. ARC's assumed net debt (about USD 2.6bn) is non-cash and is not in this line; add it to net debt in FY2026.
