The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  1. Earnings. Shell's IFRS earnings attributable to shareholders, less the current-cost-of-supplies adjustment (inventory holding gains or losses), less identified items (impairments, fair-value accounting of commodity derivatives, disposal gains, redundancy and other items), equal Shell's adjusted earnings. Consensus net income and EPS (FactSet, brokers' basis) are on adjusted earnings: FY2025 USD 18.5bn and USD 3.15. FactSet's own history lines differ: reported basic EPS USD 3.03, FactSet adjusted diluted EPS USD 3.41, and Viewforth-definition EPS USD 3.79 (USD 21.6bn of net income). Growth must be measured from the brokers'-basis actuals only.
  2. Expected gaps, reported against adjusted net income. FY2026 about nil (inventory gains offset impairments and derivative losses; Q1 2026 identified items -USD 2.4bn, Q2 +USD 0.4bn). FY2027 about -USD 4bn (inventory holding losses of USD 2-3bn as prices fall, plus USD 1-2bn of impairments). FY2028 -USD 1-3bn (likely loss or write-down on the US chemicals sale). FY2029-30 about -USD 1bn.
  3. EBITDA. Brokers'-basis EBITDA (FY2025 USD 56,621m) is Shell's adjusted EBITDA and includes the share of joint-venture and associate results. FactSet operating EBITDA (FY2025 USD 43,743m) excludes them and other income; the gap was 29.4% in FY2025.
  4. Revenue. Estimates-basis revenue (FY2025 USD 266,886m) is 3.7% above FactSet fundamentals revenue (USD 257,331m). My growth rates are applied to the fundamentals base.
  5. Tax. Reported rates (41.4% in FY2025) are on IFRS pre-tax profit. The model rate of 35-36.5% is on adjusted, joint-venture-inclusive pre-tax profit, with minorities folded in.
  6. Capex. Shell's "cash capex" includes acquisitions (the 2026 guidance of USD 24-26bn includes about USD 4bn for ARC). My capex input is organic cash capex; acquisitions sit in their own line. FactSet consensus capex (USD 20.9bn for FY2026) is on a fixed-asset basis and consistent with this.
  7. Free cash flow. My free cash flow is CFFO less capex less lease principal payments, before acquisitions and disposals. FactSet's definition (consensus FY2026 USD 38.8bn) excludes lease payments (about USD 5.5bn) and uses fixed-asset capex (about USD 1.5-2bn lower), so it is about USD 7bn higher than mine on the same cash flows.
  8. Net debt. Shell's and FactSet's net debt both include about USD 29.7bn of leases (Q2 2026: USD 41.75bn; gearing 18.7%).
  9. Dividend. FactSet's dividend line is by payment period (FY2025 USD 1.42); my inputs are by the year declared.
  10. Why my FY2026 free cash flow is above an EBITDA-less-tax build. Shell's H1 2026 CFFO before working capital (USD 34.4bn) ran about USD 6bn ahead of adjusted earnings plus D&A, because cash taxes lag the tax charge when profits surge and joint-venture dividends and derivative cash timing added to it. A model that derives cash flow from EBITDA less the tax charge will show FY2026 free cash flow about USD 6-8bn below my figure; about USD 1-3bn of the timing reverses in FY2027.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: Shell plc (SHEL-GB), the London-listed integrated energy major, reporting five segments: Integrated Gas (LNG liquefaction, gas production including the Canadian Montney assets of ARC Resources acquired on 2 September 2026, gas-to-liquids at Pearl GTL, and LNG trading), Upstream (oil and gas exploration and production, mainly deepwater in Brazil and the Gulf of America), Marketing (retail mobility, lubricants and business fuels), Chemicals & Products (refining, chemicals and oil products trading) and Renewables & Energy Solutions (power, hydrogen and carbon capture).

Request: T-D1

Peers: TotalEnergies (closest European integrated peer; organic growth, lowest supermajor unit costs, large LNG portfolio); BP (integrated trading organisation, structural cost programme, disposal-funded deleveraging); ExxonMobil (long programmatic buyback record and structural cost savings since 2019); Chevron (Hess integration and Brent-scaled buybacks); Eni (asset-sale-funded deleveraging through satellites, chemicals restructuring at Versalis); Equinor (Shell's partner in the Adura UK joint venture; spot gas seller); QatarEnergy (private; Shell's partner in Pearl GTL and Qatari LNG, whose export capacity was damaged in 2026); Vitol and Trafigura (private; trading earnings across volatility regimes); ARC Resources (acquired by Shell in 2026; its standalone record as a guide to the acquired assets). Operating conditions: a geopolitical supply shock with the Strait of Hormuz closed and Qatari LNG under force majeure (2026); a reopening of the Strait followed by the return of stranded Gulf supply; an LNG buyer's market as new US and Qatari liquefaction capacity arrives (2027-2030); normalisation of exceptionally high refining margins after product-export disruption; an oil price collapse or OPEC+ market-share war (as in 2014-2016 and 2020); weak European industrial and chemicals demand; high inflation in oilfield services, subsea and gas turbines with rising interest rates. Inputs: Can Shell integrate ARC Resources and deliver the USD 250m of synergies stated at completion (2 September 2026) on time, and what did comparable upstream integrations by majors (Shell-BG 2016, ExxonMobil-Pioneer, Chevron-Hess) deliver against plan? How quickly have Qatari LNG trains and Pearl GTL returned to full output after past outages (the Pearl shutdown of February 2017; other force majeure events), and what does QatarEnergy say about repair timelines for the 2026 damage? Is Shell's LNG sales growth target of 4-5% a year to 2030 deliverable with its current liquefaction pipeline, including LNG Canada Phase 2 (final investment decision 30 September 2026)? Can the structural cost programme reach the top of the USD 5-7bn by 2028 target (USD 5.83bn reported at Q2 2026) while "other opex changes including inflation" rose by USD 1.1bn in H1 2026, and without further reliability damage (Tier 1 and 2 process-safety events of 63 in 2023 and 90 in 2024; voluntary turnover 11.2% in 2025)? Has any major sustained buybacks of at least USD 3bn a quarter through a price downturn under a cash-flow-linked payout policy, and what happened to its balance sheet? Can Shell's refining margin capture (55% in H1 2026 per Jefferies) persist when indicator margins normalise? How did majors that exited chemicals businesses fare, and on what terms? Focus: Whether the changes now expected at Shell over the next 18 months, Middle East volume restoration, ARC integration, cost delivery and steady buybacks through falling prices, rest on capabilities Shell has demonstrably shown, and which of them depend instead on external events (the reopening of the Strait of Hormuz) or on capabilities it has not yet demonstrated (integration of a large North American gas producer).

Request: T-D2

Peers: TotalEnergies (most comparable integrated LNG and refining exposure); BP (refining and trading leverage); ExxonMobil and Chevron (larger refining integration, US gas exposure); Eni (gas and LNG optimisation); Equinor (direct European spot gas exposure); Cheniere and Venture Global (US LNG developers with Henry Hub-linked and spot-selling models); Vitol (private; trading earnings across volatility regimes). Operating conditions: the Strait of Hormuz closed against reopened; Brent above USD 100 against Brent at USD 60-75; refining margins at record highs (Shell's indicative refining margin USD 42/bbl in Q3 2026 per its update note of 7 October 2026) against mid-cycle; an LNG buyer's market from 2027; European windfall-tax escalation; an OPEC+ market-share war. Inputs: How concentrated are the drivers separating Shell's 2026-2030 financials from 2020-2025: the Brent price, the refining margin, European and Asian gas prices, the trading and optimisation contribution, Qatari and Pearl GTL volumes, ARC's contribution, the share count, tax and levies (UK Energy Profits Levy to March 2030; Brazil's crude export levy), and working capital? How wide was the dispersion of integrated majors' earnings in the 12-24 months after previous price spikes (2008, 2011-2014, 2022), how fast did consensus normalise, and how often did forecasts resting on a sustained price premium hold? What explains the range of individual broker FY2027 EPS estimates for Shell (USD 3.95 to USD 7.71 per FactSet, 25 October 2026), broker by broker: which price decks, refining margins, trading assumptions and share counts sit behind the low and high ends? How fast did trading and optimisation earnings at Shell, BP, TotalEnergies, Vitol and Trafigura revert after 2022, and what is the evidence on a normal-year baseline? How large were Shell's working-capital releases after past price falls (2014-2015, 2020, 2023), and how did cash taxes lag profits? Focus: Which one or two inputs explain most of the variance in Shell's FY2027 earnings, how wide and how skewed the realistic range of FY2027 outcomes is around the reopening or not of the Strait of Hormuz, and whether the historical record suggests forecasts made at a price peak overshoot or undershoot.

Request: T-I1

Peers: TotalEnergies, BP, Eni and Equinor (European integrated peers reporting on the same dates and facing the same deck); ExxonMobil and Chevron (US majors with read-across on refining and trading); Repsol, Galp and OMV (mid-size integrated groups whose 2026 beats were led by refining). Operating conditions: the Q3 2026 results season with Brent above USD 100 and record refining margins; a possible reopening of the Strait of Hormuz and Middle East settlement; European winter gas with storage at a record low (74% entering winter per Morgan Stanley, 27 October 2026); the UK budget of 28 October 2026; the 2027-2030 LNG supply wave. Inputs: Shell's Q3 2026 results (29 October 2026) against consensus and against its Q3 update note, and what they say about trading, refining utilisation, chemicals and Integrated Gas volumes; the size of the buyback announced at Q3 2026 and with FY2026 results (February 2027) against the at-least-USD-3bn-a-quarter floor and the 40-50% of CFFO policy; whether the dividend increase with Q1 2027 results exceeds the 4% policy again (5% with Q1 2026); 2027 capex guidance within USD 20-22bn; whether a Capital Markets Day in 2027 raises the cost target, the dividend growth rate or the distribution range; the Pearl GTL Train 2 restart (targeted Q1 2027) and Qatari volumes; how consensus FY2027 EPS, which rose about 21% in three months to October 2026 per FactSet, compares with Shell's run-rate and with peers' latest results; Shell's record of beating or missing consensus in calm against volatile quarters (misses in Q2 2023 and Q4 2025; beats of 8% and 10% in Q1 and Q2 2026). Focus: Whether Shell's next three to six reports are more likely to bring consensus FY2027 estimates down (as prices and trading normalise) or up (if disruption persists), and whether management is likely to raise distributions, guidance or targets, or to hold cash for acquisitions.

Request: T-I2

Peers: TotalEnergies, BP, Eni, Equinor, ExxonMobil and Chevron (the investor comparison set for European integrated majors); Cheniere (US LNG model); Vitol (private; trading benchmark). Operating conditions: the record-margin environment of 2026; the expected 2027-2030 LNG oversupply; rising European windfall-tax pressure; a rotation out of energy if the Middle East conflict ends; energy as a crowded systematic hedge. Inputs: how much of Shell's 2026 trading and refining earnings analysts treat as repeatable, and how much of it is in sell-side 2027 numbers; working-capital and cash-tax timing as prices move; the margin per barrel of ARC Resources' volumes against Shell's portfolio; the effect of the 228m shares issued for ARC on per-share accretion, and how the share price affects the accretion from buybacks; the gap between Shell's reported tax rate and the rate implied by consensus; rising carbon compliance costs (about USD 1bn in 2026 toward about USD 4bn by 2035 per Shell's Annual Report 2025); the three-to-six-month lag of oil-indexed LNG prices behind Brent; whether the return of Qatari volumes and the level of oil and gas prices are linked through the reopening of the Strait of Hormuz; the post-2030 liquids gap and reserve life (7.4-7.8 years); the possibility of faster dividend growth or a higher payout as gearing falls; inventory holding effects and impairments in reported earnings. Focus: Which of these dynamics investors who own or cover Shell already discuss and price, and which are discussed by one or two analysts or by no one, in particular the size and repeatability of the 2026 trading contribution, the per-share effect of buybacks against the ARC issue, and working-capital cash flows.

Request: OWN-1

Question: How do Shell plc's (SHEL-GB) realised LNG prices and Integrated Gas earnings respond in the quarters after a Brent peak, given its oil-indexed contract slopes and three-to-six-month price lags: what did the lag do to Shell's (and BG's, TotalEnergies' and Woodside's) LNG realisations and segment earnings after the 2008, 2014 and 2022 peaks, what share of Shell's LNG sales are oil-indexed, Henry Hub-linked or spot in 2026, and what slopes and price-review outcomes have new and renegotiated LNG contracts achieved in 2025-2026?

Request: OWN-2

Question: When and how fully will Shell plc's (SHEL-GB) Middle East volumes return: what is the status of shipping through the Strait of Hormuz, what repair schedules have QatarEnergy and Shell given for the damaged Qatari LNG capacity and Pearl GTL Train 2, what are Shell's equity volumes in Pearl GTL, Qatargas 4, the North Field East and South expansions and Oman, and how quickly did Qatari and other Gulf export volumes recover after past outages and force majeure declarations?

Request: OWN-3

Question: What are the likely terms, timing and accounting effects of Shell plc's (SHEL-GB) planned sale of its US chemicals business (including the Monaca polyethylene complex and the Deer Park and Norco chemicals assets): which bidders remain (ExxonMobil and LyondellBasell were reported in August 2026), what valuations have comparable petrochemical asset sales achieved in 2024-2026, what is the carrying value against likely proceeds, and what do the European chemicals closures and the Singapore exit of 2025 imply for Chemicals & Products earnings?

Request: OWN-4

Question: How large and how fast are Shell plc's (SHEL-GB) working-capital and cash-tax swings when commodity prices fall after a spike: what working-capital releases and inventory holding losses did Shell and its peers report in 2014-2015, 2020 and 2023, how did Shell's cash taxes lag its tax charge, and what does Shell's Q1-Q2 2026 build (an USD 11.2bn outflow then a USD 3.4bn inflow) imply for the release if prices normalise?

Request: OWN-5

Question: What will ARC Resources' Montney assets earn inside Shell plc (SHEL-GB) over 2027-2030: what are the AECO gas price outlook and Canadian takeaway constraints, ARC's last standalone unit costs, condensate share, marketing mix (43% AECO and 26% US Midwest exposure in mid-2026) and JKM-linked supply contracts from 2027, the synergy plan, and the cost, schedule and gas-turbine supply risks for LNG Canada Phase 2 and Coastal GasLink Phase 2?

Request: OWN-6

Question: Which windfall taxes and levies could reduce Shell plc's (SHEL-GB) cash flows over 2026-2028, and by how much: what did the UK budget of 28 October 2026 decide for the Energy Profits Levy and its successor Oil and Gas Revenue Levy, what is the court status of Brazil's 12% crude export levy (USD 242m cost to Shell in Q2 2026), what EU-wide or national windfall-tax proposals are live, and how much of Shell's, TotalEnergies' and BP's free cash flow did the 2022-2023 windfall taxes absorb?

Layer 3 and Layer 4: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months / Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

Written 8 November 2026, 05:15 to 05:43 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Shell plc (SHEL-GB): answers to the third and fourth sets of questions (written 2026-11-08)

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Preface for the Consumer (orientation only; not an answer to a question)

What is new since the second set was written (29-Oct-26, the morning of the Q3 results).

  1. Q3 2026 results (29-Oct-26). Adjusted earnings USD 10.81bn against consensus USD 10.33bn (+5%) and against the second set's own estimate of USD 10.8bn. Segments: Integrated Gas USD 3.04bn (+42% year on year, on near-record third-party sourcing; LNG trading in line with Q2), Upstream 3.19bn, Marketing 1.16bn, Chemicals & Products 3.86bn (Products 3.8bn on the USD 42/bbl indicative margin; Chemicals 0.10bn at USD 208/t; Products trading lower than Q2). Integrated Gas production 772 kboe/d (guided 740-780); refinery utilisation 96%. CFFO before working capital was 19% above consensus; the Q3 working-capital outflow was about USD 0.6bn (about USD 8.3bn over nine months); Q3 cash taxes were USD 3.2bn and German emissions certificates USD 2.4bn. Net debt USD 39.7bn, gearing 16.6%, or 2.7% excluding leases. Buyback USD 5.0bn for the quarter to the Q4 results (floor USD 3.0bn; the second set assumed about 3.5bn); dividend held at USD 0.3906. Q4 Integrated Gas production guided to 1,080-1,180 kboe/d. Nine-month operating expenses USD 26.41bn against 26.12bn. A USD 6.4bn non-cash loss is pre-announced for Q4 2026 (derecognition of the Netherlands pension plan; an identified item outside adjusted earnings). A Capital Markets Update is set for 4-Feb-27, with the Q4 results.
  2. Fiscal. The UK budget (28-Oct-26) kept the Energy Profits Levy (78% marginal) to 31-Mar-30 and legislated the successor Oil and Gas Revenue Levy (35% of revenue above USD 90/bbl and 90p/therm from April 2030, indexed). The early end Bernstein had discussed will not happen. Brazil's 12% export levy: a federal court injunction exempts the international producers pending a Supreme Court ruling, and the provisional measure lapsed in July 2026 with a 60-day extension.
  3. Hormuz and Qatar. Outbound energy-tanker transits run at about ten a day against 35 before the crisis (Morgan Stanley tracker, 4-Nov-26). QatarEnergy's damaged Trains 4 and 6 (12.8 mtpa, 17% of Qatari capacity) need about three years (Al-Kaabi, late October; Incred expects 2029-30), but the undamaged trains can restart "within a couple of weeks" of a reopening (Qatar's energy minister, September). On the sources' description the two damaged trains are ExxonMobil-partnered; Shell's Qatargas 4 train is not named among them, so Shell's Qatari LNG loss is a transit loss, not a damage loss, and it returns with the Strait. Pearl GTL Train 1 partially restarted in October (filling inventory; management expects no material earnings yet); Train 2 is to be ready by late Q1 2027, with exports gated by the Strait. The post-election signals lean toward a longer disruption: Jefferies (28-Oct-26) reports Gulf states planning for hostilities lasting up to ten years and three US carrier groups assembling; Iran's drone capacity is back at 100%; Iran's strait authority demands USD 1-2m a transit; QatarEnergy signed a 25-year Henry Hub-linked purchase from Cheniere as a hedge against its own chokepoint. Against that, Oman and Iran discussed a safe corridor (August) and the World Bank expects a partial recovery of Gulf gas exports from Q1 2027.
  4. Sell-side. Morgan Stanley (5-Nov-26) brought its USD 75 Brent forward to Q4 2027, raised Shell's 2027 refining margin to USD 25/bbl from 13, puts FY2027 EPS at USD 6.86, expects the 4-Feb-27 update to reset dividend growth from 4% to 10% a year to 2030, and says much of the sector's 42% rally is in the price ("longer disruption priced out"). Redburn (30-Oct-26) raised 2026-28 EPS by 5% (FY2027 USD 5.30, FY2028 4.80), assumes a 6% dividend rise with the Q4 results, models average trading from Q4 2026 and a flat 33% tax rate, and notes that a USD 5bn quarterly buyback alone does not reach the 40% floor without a higher dividend. Jefferies calls Shell "set up for a long cycle" and notes distributions at the 40% end of the range against 50% before the crisis.
  5. ARC. Attachie, ARC's growth asset, had its guidance withdrawn in February 2026 on variable well results. Q2 2026 standalone opex was CAD 6.98/boe (FY2025 5.39) on turnarounds; 43% of gas sold at AECO and 26% into the US Midwest; a JKM-linked 140,000 MMBtu/d supply contract to Cheniere starts in 2027. Redburn: without synergies the 2027-29 FCF-per-share accretion falls from 7.5% to 3.3%. The USD 250m synergy target splits into about USD 100m drilling, 60m trading, 60m pipeline tolls and 40m G&A. Coastal GasLink Phase 2 (CAD 4.9bn) places cost-overrun risk on the LNG Canada partners, Shell included; Phase 1 overran by 120%. Tourmaline paused its Montney Phase 2 to 2028 on oversupply.
  6. US chemicals. Non-binding bids (July) from ExxonMobil, LyondellBasell, Apollo and Kuwait Petroleum at up to USD 8bn, against about USD 24bn of chemicals assets on the books (Monaca alone cost about USD 14bn); Sawan (29-Oct-26) is weighing an outright sale, a portfolio combination or a capital-markets transaction and says he will not sell at the bottom of the cycle. Separately, Shell sold its 16.67% of North West Shelf LNG to XRG for USD 1.45bn (3-Nov-26).
  7. Co-movement. Shell's six-month daily correlation with the 19-stock peer basket is 0.84, beta 0.83, R-squared 71%; Shell returned +17.9% against the basket's +11.4%.

The model gaps flagged in the one-page summary, explained, and what I change (lines in inputs-changed.md).

  • Every forecast year's revenue and EBITDA in the model is 3.7% above the second set's intended figure (FY2026 revenue USD 370.7bn against 357.4bn; EBITDA 88.6bn against 85.4bn; FY2027 306.9bn against 296.0bn and 76.7bn against 74.0bn; and so on to FY2030). The cause is the one the second set warned of in its basis note: the model applied the FY2026 growth of 38.9% to the estimates-basis FY2025 revenue (USD 266,886m) rather than to the fundamentals revenue (USD 257,331m); 266,886 x 1.389 = 370,705. The quarterly inputs sit on fundamentals quarters and summed to 357.4bn, so the model moved them by +10 points to match the wrong annual figure. The EPS gaps in FY2027-30 (5.59 against 5.24; 4.94 against 4.61; 4.92 against 4.59; 5.14 against 4.80) are this error flowing through at a 36% tax rate, plus a share count in the model about 0.6% lower than the second set's. The fix I record: FY2026 revenue growth of 33.9% on the base the model uses, which returns revenue to USD 357.4bn and makes the quarterly shift unnecessary. If the Financial Analyst instead re-bases the revenue line to fundamentals revenue, the second set's 38.9% should be restored; the two fixes are alternatives, not additive.
  • FY2026 free cash flow: the model's USD 28.5bn against the second set's 35.1bn. The model builds cash as EBITDA less the tax charge, interest, capex, lease payments and the working-capital build. Shell's nine-month CFFO was USD 48.5bn after an USD 8.3bn working-capital outflow, so CFFO before working capital ran about USD 11bn ahead of adjusted earnings plus D&A over nine months: cash taxes lag the charge in a profit surge, joint-venture dividends arrive, and derivative cash timing helped. The second set's 35.1bn is the right figure (it implies FY2026 CFFO of about USD 61bn, against consensus of 61.5bn). The model cannot represent the timing except through the working-capital line, which the second set already treats as a cash plug rather than an economic level. I move the percentages so that the model's cash matches: a FY2026 build of about USD 0.2bn instead of 8.4bn (the USD 8.1bn timing benefit netted against the real build), a FY2027 release of about 4.7bn (the real 6.7bn release less 2.0bn of cash-tax reversal, the midpoint of the second set's 1-3bn), a FY2028 release of about 2.0bn and nil after. On the corrected EBITDA this gives model free cash flow of about USD 35.3bn, 32.4bn, 24.9bn, 22.0bn and 21.5bn. The last three sit USD 1.1-1.6bn below the second set's cross-check (26.1, 23.6, 23.0) because the second set's figures carry cash above a mechanical build that its text does not explain; I do not carry it, and the difference changes no conclusion.
  • Two inputs change on new facts. Buybacks: FY2026 USD 14.5bn and FY2027 16.0bn (from 13.5bn and 14.5bn), after the Q3 programme of USD 5bn against the roughly 3.5bn assumed; the reasoning is in I-01. Dividend: 6% a year from 2027 rather than 5% (USD 1.66, 1.76, 1.86, 1.97), after Morgan Stanley's 10% thesis, Redburn's 6% assumption and management's 5% in 2026 against a 4% policy. The FY2027 disposals line adds the USD 1.45bn North West Shelf proceeds.
  • The model's year-end net debt line excludes leases and, even after the cash fix, reads about USD 8-9bn too high for end-2026 (Shell's ex-lease net debt was about USD 5.5bn at Q3 2026 on 2.7% ex-lease gearing). Use Shell's reported USD 39.7bn including leases for any gearing statement, not the model line.
  • Effect on the return at the guideline multiple. After the fixes, model EPS returns to the second set's figures plus about 1% from the faster buyback: my cross-check is USD 6.63, 5.27, 4.65, 4.64 and 4.85 for FY2026-30. The 18-month total return at the 11.58x guideline should recompute to roughly +5-7% rather than +10.0%, and the three-year figure to roughly +15%. The guideline multiple sits at the dashboard's ten-year 90th-percentile band (11.60x, "mania") and above the first set's view that Shell's fair multiple is a yield multiple of about 7-10x (C-50). D-07 and the fourth set's judgement return to this.

Where I disagree with the earlier sets.

  • First set, C-14 and C-58: the worry about rising process-safety events (63 in 2023, 90 in 2024) is stale; the 2025 Annual Report shows Tier 1 and 2 events fell to 62 in 2025. Reliability is not deteriorating on the published measure.
  • First set, C-04 and C-20: the possible early end to the UK levy is closed; the levy runs to March 2030 and its successor is a revenue levy.
  • First set, C-86 and others: proved reserve life of 7.4-7.8 years conflicts with Redburn's statement (October 2026) of a 9.5-year reserve life in 2025, "broadly in line with the European peer average". The two are probably on different bases (proved against proved-plus-probable, or before and after ARC's 2bn boe of 2P reserves). I treat the conflict as unresolved and raise it as a research request; the Consumer should not quote either figure without its basis.
  • Second set, P-10: I move the scenario weights for FY2027 from 35/45/20 (fast reopening, gradual, prolonged) to 30/45/25, for the reasons in point 3 above. The base case remains the gradual reopening; the probability-weighted FY2027 EPS rises to about USD 5.4 from 5.23.
  • Second set, P-08: buybacks and the dividend growth rate, as above.
  • Second set, P-06 and P-10: the FY2028-30 free cash flow cross-check, as above.

Five judgements carry most of the weight.

  1. The earlier sets are congruent on the architecture, and the Q3 print validated the second set's model almost exactly; the disagreements are factual conflicts, not directional ones (D-01).
  2. The change expected over 18 months asks almost nothing of Shell that it has not already demonstrated; it asks a great deal of the Strait of Hormuz (D-02, D-03).
  3. The FY2027 EPS cone is about USD 4.0 to 7.0 and three-peaked. The downside peak is cushioned (LNG lag, working capital, buybacks at a lower price); the upside peak carries its own penalties (windfall taxes, demand destruction and a sector de-rating when it ends). Trackability is high because one weekly data series, Hormuz transits, decides most of it (D-04).
  4. Predictability is bifurcated: the cash and per-share lines are highly predictable and Shell's disclosure and the external transmission are clear exactly there; EBITDA is unpredictable and the disclosure and transmission are weakest exactly there, in trading and tax (D-05, D-06).
  5. The sell-side has the shape right. Guidance will be met and probably raised at the 4-Feb-27 update; consensus for FY2027 goes up before it comes down; the narrative misses little that favours the case, and what it misses (carbon cost, ARC's low margin per barrel, the tax mix) is mildly negative (I-01 to I-03).

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: Risks · Group: Guidance · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

What this is: daily close-to-close returns over rolling windows of 126 trading days (about six months). Correlation: how closely the daily moves line up (1 = always together, 0 = unrelated). Beta: how far the stock moves for a 1% move in the basket. R-squared: the share of the stock's daily price variance the peer basket explains; the remainder is the stock's own (idiosyncratic) movement. Basket: the peers below, equal-weighted. Prices only; the multiple's own history is in the financials block.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for the memo

Research requests for the next set

Company: Shell plc (SHEL-GB), the London-listed integrated energy major, reporting five segments: Integrated Gas (LNG liquefaction and trading, gas production including the Canadian Montney assets of ARC Resources acquired on 2 September 2026, and gas-to-liquids at Pearl GTL), Upstream (oil and gas production, mainly deepwater in Brazil and the Gulf of America), Marketing (retail mobility, lubricants and business fuels), Chemicals & Products (refining, chemicals and oil products trading) and Renewables & Energy Solutions (power, hydrogen and carbon capture).

Request: OWN-1

Question: What do investors who own or cover Shell plc (SHEL-GB) say about it, and how are they positioned, since the Q3 2026 results of 29 October 2026 (adjusted earnings USD 10.81bn and a USD 5bn buyback announced; Capital Markets Update scheduled for 4 February 2027): on X/Twitter, Substack, published fund letters and manager interviews, conference and broker-hosted call transcripts, and in disclosed holdings changes (UK regulatory filings, US 13F filings for the ADR) and short interest; which dynamics do they debate most (the repeatability of 2026 trading and refining earnings, a dividend-growth reset at the February update, the reopening of the Strait of Hormuz and the return of Qatari and Pearl GTL volumes, the ARC Resources integration and its 228m new shares, the buyback pace, the post-2030 liquids outlook, European windfall taxes), which do they not mention, how has positioning in Shell changed relative to TotalEnergies, BP, Eni and Equinor through 2026, and how do systematic and macro investors describe energy's role as a geopolitical hedge in their portfolios?

Request: OWN-2

Question: What are sell-side and company expectations for Shell plc's (SHEL-GB) Capital Markets Update on 4 February 2027, held with the Q4 2026 results: which existing targets does each covering broker expect to change (dividend growth of about 4% a year; distributions of 40-50% of cash flow from operations; a buyback of at least USD 3bn a quarter; cash capex of USD 20-22bn a year for 2027-28; structural cost reductions of USD 5-7bn by 2028 against 2022, with USD 5.83bn reported at Q2 2026; a production CAGR of about 4% for 2025-30 and LNG sales growth of 4-5% a year; free cash flow per share growth above 10% a year to 2030) and in which direction; what has management said about these targets, about the post-2030 liquids outlook and about the leverage level at which it would stop retaining cash, in results calls, conferences and press interviews since the Q3 2026 results; and how did Shell's share price, consensus estimates and broker ratings react to the Capital Markets Days of June 2023 and March 2025 against the expectations published beforehand?

Request: OWN-3

Question: What are Shell plc's (SHEL-GB) proved and proved-plus-probable reserve lives and reserve replacement ratios at end-2025 on the SEC and PRMS bases, how do they compare on identical bases with TotalEnergies, BP, Eni, Equinor, ExxonMobil and Chevron, how much do the ARC Resources assets acquired on 2 September 2026 (reported at about 2 billion boe of proved-plus-probable reserves) add to Shell's reserves and reserve life, what organic liquids decline do Shell's own disclosures and the covering brokers show for 2028-2035, and what resource additions (Namibia appraisal, Bonga North, the Orca/Gato do Mato project, LNG Canada Phase 2 feedgas, further acquisitions) do brokers and management expect to fill it, at what cost per barrel and on what timetable?
